\chapter*{Declaration on the Use of AI Tools}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Declaration on the Use of AI Tools}
\thispagestyle{plain}

The following AI tools were used in this work, as recorded in the project
repository.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Coding and writing assistants.} Anthropic Claude models and
OpenAI Codex were used to write and revise code, analysis scripts and
documents, including drafts of this report. Commits they contributed carry a
\texttt{Co-Authored-By} trailer in the git history.
\item \textbf{Frontier-model synthesis.} ChatGPT Pro Extended and Gemini Deep
Think produced proposed derivations and reasoning recorded in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier\_synthesis\_*.tex}, which those
documents mark as not being experiments. Where this report uses such a result,
it states its own derivation (Chapter 6 \S6.3.3).
\item \textbf{Independent verification.} An LLM verification pass (Kimi,
6 July 2026) reviewed two single-seed results and flagged both as
\textsc{suspect}; both were then re-tested and reported as nulls (Chapter 6
\S6.4.3).
\item \textbf{Evaluation triage.} The Jev (TypeSafe) judgment harness was used
in the E1--E14 ledger only for classification, faithfulness and value
ordering, never for arithmetic (Chapter 4 \S4.5).
\item \textbf{Review.} The pre-defence self-review of 27 September 2026 and the
revision of this report that followed it were AI-assisted.
\end{itemize}

No AI assistant generated experimental data: every reported measurement comes
from an executed run or evaluation recorded in the repository, and simulated
values are labelled as simulations. The research direction,
the experimental design, the choice of claims and their final wording are the
author's responsibility.

%---------------------------------------------------------------------
\chapter*{Abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}
\thispagestyle{plain}

This report studies group-relative reinforcement-learning post-training of
large language models under one methodological commitment: a comparative
claim is made only when the whole training stack is held fixed except the
factor under test. It makes four contributions.

\textbf{(1) A recomputable signal-starvation diagnostic and a loss-form
audit.} The Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF) is logged per step and recomputed
from stored reward tensors, with a closed-form Bernoulli null. Reading the
runners' code rather than the internal reports shows that the ``GRPO'' runners
used here are REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline, without KL or clipping.

\textbf{(2) Stack-conditioned reporting.} A minimum reporting standard and a
machine-readable stack registry with a \texttt{stackdiff} tool that flags
label-flip risk between two runs from their records alone.

\textbf{(3) Calibrated negative results.} No reliable scaling law is recovered
from reward curves across twelve fitted anchors. A same-stack PPO/GRPO
contrast (0.992 against 0.990, $p = 0.374$, five seeds) and a group-size sweep
find no detectable difference, but both run on a saturated task and establish
neither equivalence nor an optimum. ZVF does not predict held-out accuracy
($\rho = 0.27$, 95\% CI $[-0.37, 0.88]$). An adaptive group-size controller
derived from ZVF saves no prompts in counterfactual simulation, because 92\% of its escalations fall on
groups that are already all-correct.

\textbf{(4) An evaluation-governance protocol.} One lightly trained actor
(Qwen3.6-35B-A3B with a LoRA adapter, 40 training steps) is evaluated on
fourteen held-out suites under native graders at pinned revisions, with
receipts, a no-pooling rule and a per-lane serving audit that traced a
zero score to a serving defect. Complete scopes include VerilogEval
($129/312 = 41.35\%$), LAB-Bench ($0.2288$) and AgentDojo benign utility
($88/97$); six replacement scopes completed on 27 September 2026
(Chapter~8, Table~8.C); seven original contracts remain externally blocked. A
paired trained-versus-base rerun on 5--151 identical items per lane finds no
significant difference in any lane, which at those sizes is inconclusive. No
claim of improvement over any baseline is made.

\vspace{0.25in}
\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} GRPO, RLVR, zero-variance fraction, signal
starvation, stack-conditioned results, benchmark evaluation, reproducibility,
held-out evaluation, attribution.

\part{Foundations}


\chapter{Introduction}\label{introduction}

\section{Background: critic-free RL post-training and the group-relative
estimator}\label{background-critic-free-rl-post-training-and-the-group-relative-estimator}

Post-training is now the stage at which a base language model is turned
into something that reliably follows instructions and solves problems.
For several years the dominant recipe was reinforcement learning from
human feedback (RLHF), in which a learned reward model is trained on
human preference comparisons and then used as the reward signal for a
policy-optimisation loop. RLHF works, but it introduces a second learned
model whose errors the policy can exploit, and it makes reward
construction expensive and hard to audit. The alternative that has taken
hold more recently is reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards
(RLVR): instead of a learned reward model, the reward comes from a
programmatic checker --- exact-match against a reference answer for
grade-school mathematics, unit tests for code, a formal or semi-formal
validator for structured output. The checker is deterministic,
transparent, and free to run. What it gives up in generality it gains in
measurability: every reward is a fact about a generated string rather
than a prediction from a model (Figure~\ref{fig:grpoloop}).


\fig{fig_grpo_loop}
{The GRPO training loop}
{The GRPO training loop. A prompt is sampled $G$ times, each completion is scored by a reward function, advantages are formed relative to the group, and the policy is updated. When all $G$ rewards coincide the group-relative advantage collapses to zero and no reward-relative gradient flows, although the rollouts are still paid for. Number line: the Phase-1 P2 batch ZVF of 0.72--0.77 (four methods, 40 steps, 16 prompts $\times$ 8 samples, GSM8K, seed 0, Qwen3.5-4B) and the group-size sweep on Qwen2.5-0.5B two-digit addition over three seeds, where mean ZVF falls from 0.838 at $G=2$ to 0.631 at $G=16$ (a fall of 0.207).}
{fig:grpoloop}



The workhorse optimiser for both settings has been proximal policy
optimisation (PPO), which optimises a clipped surrogate objective
against a value function critic. The critic is a second network, usually
of comparable size to the policy, trained to predict the expected return
of the current state; its predictions supply the baseline that the
advantage estimator subtracts. This works, but it is expensive and
fragile. The critic consumes memory that could hold policy parameters,
it has its own learning rate and its own failure modes, and its errors
propagate directly into the policy gradient as biased advantages.

Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) removes the critic entirely.
Rather than learn a value function, GRPO samples a group of \(G\)
completions for the same prompt, scores all of them with the verifier,
and derives the advantage of each completion from the group's own reward
statistics. Concretely, for a group with rewards \(r_1, \dots, r_G\),
the advantage assigned to completion \(i\) is a within-group
standardisation,

\[A_i = \frac{r_i - \mu_g}{\sigma_g + \varepsilon}, \qquad \mu_g = \frac{1}{G}\sum_j r_j, \qquad \sigma_g^2 = \frac{1}{G}\sum_j (r_j - \mu_g)^2,\]

which in the original GRPO formulation is then used in a clipped,
KL-regularised policy-gradient update. The runners used in this project
are simpler: none has a KL term or a reference policy, and the Tinker
runners, including the one that trained the campaign actor, have no
ratio or clipping either, so they are REINFORCE with a group-mean
baseline (Table 4.2 gives the loss form of every study). The empirical
baseline is the group mean; there is no value network to train, no value
loss to weight, and no critic to keep in sync with the policy. The trade
is a much simpler optimiser for a much smaller effective batch, since
\(G\) samples of the same prompt now carry one learning signal rather
than \(G\) independent ones.

That trade has a sharp consequence, and it is the observation this
project is organised around. Because the advantage is a
\emph{within-group} standardisation, its numerator is a deviation from
the group mean and its denominator is the group's own spread. When every
completion in a group receives the same verifier outcome --- all
correct, or all incorrect --- the group contributes nothing:
\(\sigma_g = 0\), every numerator is zero, and every advantage \(A_i\)
is exactly zero. The group has been fully sampled and fully scored, at
full token cost, and the reward-relative part of the update is
identically zero. In an objective with a KL penalty, that penalty would
still pull the policy toward the reference; in this project's runners
there is no such term, so the group contributes exactly zero gradient.
We call the fraction of a training batch that falls into this state the
\textbf{Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF)}, and it is the single measurement
that most of this report's diagnostics are built around.

ZVF is not a rare edge case, but its size depends on the regime. The
Phase-1 main configuration (Qwen3.5-4B, GSM8K, 40 steps, \(G = 8\))
records a ZVF of approximately 0.72--0.77, meaning that roughly three
quarters of that rollout batch contributes no reward-relative gradient.
Other measured runs span 0.16 (Qwen3-8B on held-out GSM8K at \(G = 8\))
to 0.84 (Qwen2.5-0.5B arithmetic at \(G = 2\)), so no single value is
typical; the measured values are collected in the canonical-numbers
table (Table 1.1). The fraction responds to the one knob that most
directly controls it: on the Qwen2.5-0.5B arithmetic sweep, mean ZVF
falls from 0.838 at \(G = 2\) to 0.631 at \(G = 16\), a decline of 0.207
across that range. Larger groups buy more contrast, with diminishing
returns that saturate well before the group is small enough to be cheap.

There is a second and less comfortable observation in the background,
and it is about attribution rather than mechanism. ``GRPO'' is not a
single algorithm. It is a label that covers implementations in Tinker,
TRL, SkyRL, verl, OpenRLHF and a growing number of research forks, each
of which chooses its own answer parser, sampling temperature, top-\(p\),
KL coefficient, clip range, LoRA target set, precision and evaluation
harness. Two runs that both say ``GRPO'' in their titles may differ in a
dozen ways that plausibly move the outcome, and the reported headline
number does not distinguish between them. The project's own
stack-sensitivity probe makes this concrete: a nominally matched
configuration (Qwen3-8B, seed 42, \(G = 8\), learning rate
\(1\times10^{-5}\), GSM8K-500, 30 steps, visible hyperparameters held
equal, although the managed Tinker run used the Qwen3-8B-Base checkpoint
and TRL the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B) was run across four framework
paths, two of which completed and two of which are dry-run placeholders,
with results recorded in
\longtok{experiments/results/framework\_comparison.json}. Four separate
marginal one-way ANOVAs over 42 experiments give \(\eta^2 = 0.546\) for
library or framework, \(\eta^2 = 0.558\) for training algorithm,
\(\eta^2 = 0.471\) for model family and \(\eta^2 = 0.322\) for model
scale --- with the important caveat, stated by the source itself, that
these factors are confounded with one another and with back-end, reward
implementation, hardware and task. Because each value is a marginal
per-factor \(\eta^2\) on a collinear corpus, the four sum to 1.897 and
are not a variance decomposition; none estimates a factor's independent
contribution (Chapter 6 §6.1; source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/\_shared\_methods.tex). The framework
factor is nonetheless not a nuisance parameter to be wished away:
marginally, outcomes vary with it about as strongly as with the
algorithm label.

\section{A Motivating Example}\label{a-motivating-example}

Consider a single training step with a group size of \(G = 8\) on an
easy GSM8K prompt that the current policy already solves. All eight
sampled completions produce the correct final answer, so each receives
reward \(r_i = 1\). The group mean is \(\mu_g = 1\) and the group
standard deviation is \(\sigma_g = 0\), so every advantage \(A_i = 0\).
The compute spent on that group is real --- eight full forward-decode
passes at whatever temperature the sampler is configured with --- and
the group supplies no task-learning signal. In this project's runners,
which carry no KL term, that group contributes exactly zero gradient.
Now imagine a batch of 16 such prompts in which 12 are already solved
and 4 are not: \(12/16 = 0.75\) of the batch is ``dark'' compute. What
the Semester 4 work adds to this picture is that 0.75 is not a worst
case: it is close to the 0.72--0.77 measured on the Phase-1 main
configuration. It is not a universal value either, since the measured
runs range from 0.16 to 0.84 depending on model, task and \(G\) (Table
1.1), so any claim that GRPO is learning efficiently has to be read
against the ZVF of its own regime (Figure~\ref{fig:confounding}).


\fig{fig_confounding}
{The confounding problem this work addresses}
{The confounding problem this work addresses. A single reported reward figure sits on top of an unstated stack; without fixing and reporting that stack the number is not attributable to the method under test. Top right: of the four frameworks in the nominal configuration only Tinker and TRL were run; veRL and OpenRLHF were not installed in the sandbox and have no measurement. The $17\times$ gap between the two measured stacks is an under-specification exhibit, not a clean back-end-only effect, because the managed stack also pinned a different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base vs.\ Qwen3-8B). Bottom left: one-way $\eta^2$ shares over 42 reported experiments; they are confounded with one another and with back-end, reward implementation, hardware and task, so they do not sum to one. Bottom right: Cohen's $d$ with 95\% CIs from the Phase-1 statistical summary; the Qwen arms did not share a back-end (GRPO on the managed API, PPO on a Modal H100 cluster), so no directional Qwen algorithm claim is made.}
{fig:confounding}



Now suppose, having noticed this, that one wants to answer the obvious
follow-up question: is the group-relative estimator actually a better
choice than a learned critic? The natural experiment is a matched
comparison --- same model, same training prompts, same verifier, same
step budget, same evaluation, with PPO and GRPO as the only difference.
The project's first attempt at that contrast is recorded in a historical
internal report under the label ``Qwen3-8B'', with a Welch \(t\)-test of
\(p = 0.7605\) computed over the final ten steps of a single run per
arm. Those ten steps are autocorrelated observations of one run, not ten
independent runs, and the project's later methods note re-examined the
row and marks it ``provenance conflict; not estimable''. No effect size,
confidence interval or power figure is therefore quoted for it here, and
the Welch value is carried only as a provenance record (Chapter 6
§6.2.3). The project's own rule is that single-seed Tinker experiments
have ``zero statistical power'' and are ``treated as descriptive only
and are not subject to significance testing''.

Two further observations from the same historical source make the
example sharper rather than cleaner. First, the same table reports the
PPO-versus-GRPO contrast on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct in the opposite
direction; it is also one run per arm, so again no inferential statistic
is quoted, but the direction alone shows that the apparent answer is not
stable across model families. Second, the Qwen arms were not executed on
a common back-end --- the GRPO arm ran through Tinker-managed
infrastructure and the PPO arm on a Modal H100 cluster --- and the
source states this explicitly and declines to draw the algorithmic
conclusion it invites: ``We therefore treat the PPO/GRPO rows as
stack-conditioned evidence, not as an algorithm diagnostic baseline'',
and ``We therefore withhold a directional Qwen algorithm claim''. The
same section records an aggregation gap that illustrates why such care
is warranted: the Qwen PPO last-10 GSM8K figure is 22.5\% in the primary
results ledger but 35.0\% in the statistical summary, and the source
names the ledger value as primary and treats the summary value as
evidence of an aggregation defect rather than as a second measurement.

The genuinely same-stack evidence in the repository is also a null, and
its scope has to be stated with it. The same-stack PPO/GRPO run
(Qwen2.5-0.5B, 40 steps, five paired seeds) is a two-digit addition task
on which both arms are at ceiling: evaluation accuracy is 0.990 for GRPO
and 0.992 for PPO, the paired difference is −0.002 with p = 0.374, and
the 200 evaluation prompts are fresh random draws from the same
generator used in training rather than a disjoint split. At that ceiling
a null cannot separate ``no effect'' from ``no headroom'', so the result
is that no difference was detectable on a saturated task, not that the
estimators are equivalent; a paired TOST at a ±0.005 margin gives p =
0.10 (Table 4.2 gives the exact loss form of both arms). The variant
audit (not to be confused with campaign lane E1) takes four GRPO-family
variants (DAPO, GSPO, Dr.~GRPO, AERO) plus the GRPO reference, runs five
arms across eight paired seeds for 40 units at a frozen stack
fingerprint, and evaluates on 500 held-out GSM8K examples per unit.
Every one of the four contrasts is inconclusive. The figures below are
paired-\(t\) 95\% intervals and paired-\(t\) \(p\)-values over the eight
seeds, recomputed from \longtok{zvf-program/audit/results/full/*.json}:
DAPO \(+0.00100\), CI \([-0.00632, +0.00832]\), \(p = 0.756\); GSPO
\(+0.00500\), CI \([-0.00357, +0.01357]\), \(p = 0.210\); Dr.~GRPO
\(-0.00200\), CI \([-0.01272, +0.00872]\), \(p = 0.673\); AERO
\(-0.00075\), CI \([-0.01029, +0.00879]\), \(p = 0.858\). These
paired-\(t\) intervals replace the audit's own intervals (corrected; see
Table E.1). After Benjamini--Hochberg correction every adjusted \(p\) is
at least 0.84. The pre-audit aggregator's verdict that the differences
``disappear'' is itself declared invalid, because the locked exact
noncentral paired-\(t\) minimum detectable effect of \(0.010115893\)
exceeds the \(0.010\) margin the study was designed around. The audit's
permitted wording is deliberately narrow: across eight paired seeds no
arm produced a BH-significant held-out difference from GRPO, and none
met the locked exact-power equivalence rule --- which is not the same as
saying the methods are the same, nor that they fail to improve. That
audit runs on Qwen3-8B GSM8K with 500 evaluation examples per unit
(Appendix A, P11).

Taken together, these two halves of the example are the whole motivation
for what follows. The mechanism half shows that a large fraction of the
compute in a GRPO run is not doing what a casual reading of the loss
curve suggests it is doing. The attribution half shows that the most
natural comparative question one would ask about that mechanism --- is
this estimator better than the alternative? --- cannot be answered from
the label ``GRPO'' and ``PPO'' alone, and that the answer on the
project's own evidence is a non-significant contrast that is either
back-end-confounded or measured at a performance ceiling, rather than a
ranking. Neither observation is a defect of the GRPO estimator; both are
defects of what gets measured and what gets reported.

\section{Problem Statement}\label{problem-statement}

The project is organised around four problems, in decreasing order of
how often the literature acknowledges them.

\textbf{Attribution is missing.} Comparative claims about RL
post-training are routinely made across frameworks that differ in
parser, sampler, KL coefficient, clip range, LoRA targets, precision and
evaluator. A reported gain from ``GRPO'' over ``PPO'' is, on most
published evidence, a gain from one complete stack over another complete
stack, with no way to say which component produced it. The project's own
marginal per-factor \(\eta^2\) values put the framework factor at a
magnitude comparable to the algorithm factor, although they are not a
partition of the variance, and its same-stack PPO/GRPO contrast, run on
a saturated 0.5B addition task (evaluation accuracy 0.990 GRPO, 0.992
PPO), returns a paired difference of −0.002 with \(p = 0.374\). The
problem is not that these numbers are surprising; it is that no paper in
this area is obliged to report the information that would let a reader
separate them.

\textbf{Signal starvation is invisible.} Standard training logs report
reward, loss, gradient norm, entropy and completion length. None of
these surfaces the fraction of a batch whose group advantage is exactly
zero. A run can show a rising reward curve and a falling ZVF, or a
rising reward curve with a ZVF pinned near 0.8, and the two look
identical in a loss plot. The fraction of sampling budget buying no
reward-relative gradient is, in the project's own measurements, the
largest measured source of zero-advantage sampling on this fixed setup,
and it is not part of any standard telemetry.

\textbf{There is no reporting or provenance standard.} Even where
authors are willing to describe their stack, there is no
machine-readable form in which to do it, no validator that checks
whether a claim's recorded configuration is internally consistent, and
no accepted minimum set of fields that a stack-conditioned result must
carry. Consequently, results cannot be pooled, cannot be compared across
papers, and cannot be audited after the fact.

\textbf{Evaluation results are rarely reported with their terminal
states.} Benchmark suites in the agentic and reasoning evaluation space
are frequently multi-scope (a public split versus a private original
contract, a benign-utility scope versus an adversarial scope),
quota-limited, or gated behind private payloads. Reports tend either to
omit such suites or to collapse them into a single number, which
conceals how much of the suite was actually graded. The project treats
this as a first-class concern rather than a presentational one: a result
that is genuinely partial should be recorded as partial, with its
coverage denominator and its reopen condition, and a lane that cannot be
run at all should be recorded as externally blocked rather than quietly
dropped.

\section{Objectives}\label{objectives}

The project pursues eight objectives. They are stated as they were
pursued, not retrofitted to the results.

\textbf{O1 --- Build a single harness that can dispatch the same
experiment to multiple back-ends.} Provide one control surface over
Tinker, TRL, SkyRL, verl and OpenRLHF paths so that a configuration can
be held fixed while the framework varies. The harness is the
deliverable; it is not a completed multi-framework benchmark. Only
Tinker and TRL produced completed runs, and those two used different
base checkpoints (Qwen3-8B-Base and Qwen3-8B Instruct), so no framework
comparison is claimed (Chapter 5 §5.2).

\textbf{O2 --- Instrument the training loop so that signal starvation is
directly observable.} Emit per-step telemetry carrying ZVF, gradient
utilisation, policy entropy, completion length and KL divergence, and
persist the raw per-group reward tensors so that any derived statistic
can be deterministically recomputed after the fact rather than trusted
from a log line.

\textbf{O3 --- Measure the zero-variance regime on real reward tensors
and test the levers that might move it.} Characterise ZVF empirically on
genuine GRPO reward tensors, and investigate re-baselining and
curriculum interventions as ways of converting dark compute into
contrast.

\textbf{O4 --- Establish and hold a scientific protocol.} Multi-seed
runs, matched baselines, held-out evaluation with explicit denominators,
deterministic recomputation, declared (not pre-registered) effect-size
and power accounting, and reporting of negatives and nulls --- including
the cases where the answer is that the study was underpowered to answer.

\textbf{O5 --- Scope the most original direction in the field.} A
literature scan for white space, which in this project identified stack
provenance and reporting standards as the unclaimed ground and produced
the minimum reporting standard (P5) and the machine-readable stack
registry (P6).

\textbf{O6 --- Turn the diagnostic into an intervention.} Derive an
adaptive group-size controller from the ZVF measurement and evaluate it
as a training-time policy rather than a post-hoc observation (P7).

\textbf{O7 --- Build and exercise an evaluation-governance protocol on
one trained actor.} Run a single trained checkpoint against fourteen
agentic and reasoning suites that were not part of its training data,
each with its own native evaluator at a pinned revision, under receipt,
budget and decontamination controls, and record every lane's terminal
state. The contribution is the governance protocol and the receipt-bound
record it produces. The campaign is not evidence about GRPO: the actor's
training run was short (40 steps, Chapter 8 §8.1.1), and the only
trained-versus-base comparison is the small-sample Table 8.B.

\textbf{O8 --- Report under a deliberate asymmetry.} Where the evidence
supports a structural or descriptive claim, state it; where it does not,
withhold it. No cross-suite aggregate, no pooling of original-contract
and replacement-scope numbers, and no claim of improvement over any
baseline anywhere in the work. Baseline comparisons are reported where
they exist, and none is claimed as an improvement. They are the paired
trained-versus-base small-scale comparison of Chapter 8 (Table 8.B),
which finds no significant difference in any lane, and the pre-to-post
held-out comparisons of the controlled studies in Chapter 6.

The objectives are what the project set out to build. The results
chapters answer four research questions that the objectives make
testable. Each is mapped to the table that answers it, and §9.3 gives a
one-line answer to each.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{RQ1.} Is the Zero-Variance Fraction recomputable from stored
  reward tensors, and does it carry information about training outcome?
  Answered by the ZVF rows of Table 1.1 and §6.3.
\item
  \textbf{RQ2.} With the training stack held fixed, do algorithm labels
  (PPO vs GRPO, GRPO vs Dr.~GRPO) or group size separate on evaluation
  accuracy? Addressed by the same-stack rows of Table 1.1, the loss-form
  table in §4.3, and §6.2.3, §6.4 and §6.5. The experiments reported
  here can bound this question but not answer it: the PPO/GRPO,
  group-size and arithmetic GRPO/Dr.~GRPO contrasts were all run on a
  saturated task at evaluation accuracy near 0.99, and the two
  unsaturated GRPO/Dr.~GRPO comparisons (§1.2, §6.5.4) are inconclusive
  and establish no equivalence (§9.3).
\item
  \textbf{RQ3.} Can a machine-readable stack manifest flag label-flip
  risk between two runs from their records alone? Answered by §7.2
  (registry health and \texttt{stackdiff}).
\item
  \textbf{RQ4.} What does a governance-first evaluation of one trained
  actor yield, and what can it not establish? Answered by Table C.3 and
  Tables 9.A and 9.B.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Contributions.} Measured against these objectives, the thesis
makes four contributions. Everything else in it is supporting evidence
for one of them.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{A recomputable signal-starvation diagnostic and a loss-form
  audit.} ZVF is logged per step and recomputed from stored reward
  tensors against a closed-form Bernoulli null (§4.3, §6.3). Reading the
  runner code rather than the internal reports shows that the ``GRPO''
  runners used here are REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline, without KL
  or clipping (Table 4.2).
\item
  \textbf{Stack-conditioned reporting.} A minimum reporting standard
  (P5) and a machine-readable stack registry with \texttt{stackdiff},
  which flags label-flip risk between two runs from their records alone
  (Chapter 7).
\item
  \textbf{Calibrated negative results.} No reliable scaling law is
  recovered from reward curves; the same-stack PPO/GRPO and group-size
  contrasts are nulls on a saturated task; ZVF does not predict held-out
  accuracy; and the ZVF-derived controller saves no prompts in
  counterfactual simulation. Each is reported with the power limitation
  that bounds it (Chapters 6--7).
\item
  \textbf{An evaluation-governance protocol.} Native graders at pinned
  revisions, per-lane receipts, a no-pooling rule, a per-lane serving
  audit and a paired same-engine trained-versus-base design, exercised
  on one lightly trained actor across fourteen held-out suites (Chapter
  8). The contribution is the protocol and the record it produces, not
  the actor's scores.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Scope and Non-Goals}\label{scope-and-non-goals}

The scope of this report is measurement and diagnostics, not leaderboard
performance. The controlled studies and the campaign actor use
small-to-mid open models --- the Qwen2.5, Qwen3, Qwen3.5 and Qwen3.6
families, from roughly 0.5B parameters up to the 35B sparse
mixture-of-experts configuration used for the campaign actor. The P1
cross-scale roster is wider, 0.6B to 1T total parameters, but its
frontier-scale anchors are single-seed runs through a managed API and
are read descriptively (Chapter 6 §6.1; source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p1\_abstract.tex). Verifiable rewards
come from mathematics and code checkers, principally GSM8K exact-match
and HumanEval-style unit tests. Held-out evaluation uses suites' own
native evaluators at pinned revisions.

Several things are explicitly out of scope. This is not an attempt to
set a state-of-the-art result on any benchmark, and no such claim is
made anywhere in the report. It is not a study of frontier reasoning
models. The evaluation sets used in the controlled comparisons are
small, and their sizes differ by study: 200 fresh prompts for the 0.5B
arithmetic runs, 200 per seed for the 1.5B GSM8K chain-of-thought runs,
500 for the variant audit, and only 8 to 20 in several Phase-1 openings
(the layer-freeze result rests on n = 8). Table 1.1 gives the n behind
each headline number. Claims about novel algorithms are not made: the
length-weighting intervention (P4) and the adaptive controller (P7) were
designed during the work and are reported as designed and simulated
systems with their evaluation limits attached, not as demonstrated
improvements.

The report states negative, null and withdrawn results alongside
positive ones; this is its one general statement about reporting
practice, and later chapters report the results without repeating it.
Three evidentiary rules apply throughout and are worth stating here
because they constrain what the later chapters are allowed to say.
First, every number in this report is traceable to a repository file
that was actually read; where a figure could not be sourced, the finding
is described qualitatively and the figure is attributed to its source
rather than reconstructed. Second, original-contract and
replacement-scope benchmark results are never pooled and never averaged
across suites --- the two are different measurements of different things
under different licences, and treating them as one column would
manufacture a number that no suite reported. Third, no improvement over
a baseline or over a base model is claimed unless the source states it
with a test; where the source reports a difference without a test
supporting it, or reports an inconclusive test, that is what is written
here.

\section{Contribution of the
Candidate}\label{contribution-of-the-candidate}

This report covers work that spans two semesters and two very different
modes of authorship, and the distinction matters for how the
contribution should be assessed. Semester 3 was a group project (Group
6). The multi-framework repository, the baseline integrations, the
initial Tinker, SkyRL and GRPO experiments, the literature foundation,
and the early benchmark, evaluation and reproducibility scaffolding were
produced by that group, of which the candidate was a member. Semester 4
is the candidate's individual continuation, and the new research
direction, the experiment expansion, the analysis, the report writing
and the P1--P8 internal reports are the candidate's solo work (Figure~\ref{fig:contribution}).


\fig{fig_contribution_map}
{Boundary between the inherited Semester-3 group infrastructure and the individual Semester-4 contribution reported in this document}
{Boundary between the inherited Semester-3 group infrastructure and the individual Semester-4 contribution reported in this document. Both columns are drawn from the repository's own provenance record (the Semester-3 tag and boundary commit, the post-boundary commit and file counts, the solo-authorship commit, and the Semester-4 README with its paper-to-evidence map); where the record does not settle a claim, the claim is left out rather than inferred.}
{fig:contribution}



The boundary is recorded precisely rather than loosely. The inherited
state is frozen at the Git tag \texttt{capstone-final-2026-04-25},
corresponding to boundary commit
\longtok{21a99ef766543a1ca86bfbad188445d3596ea73c} of 23 April 2026. At
the time the separation was documented, 584 commits had been made after
that boundary, touching 5,050 changed files --- a figure the provenance
record explicitly declines to present as a measure of academic
contribution, since it counts mechanical and generated changes alongside
substantive ones. The transition to explicit solo authorship is marked
by commit \longtok{282e6b4f0f158de11b3f5bb4656e2d871f68f5c0} of 7 July
2026.

What was added after that boundary is the substance of this report. It
includes the eight unpublished internal research reports: P1, a
cross-library and cross-scale study of GRPO behaviour; P2, the
Zero-Variance Fraction as a descriptive diagnostic; P3, group size,
contrast density and the relationship to DPO; P4, length bias and
held-out generalisation; P5, the minimum reporting standard for
stack-conditioned results; P6, a machine-readable GRPO stack registry;
P7, an adaptive group-size controller derived from the signal-starvation
diagnostic; and P8, a side study comparing a language model against
gradient-boosted trees in credit-card fraud detection. It includes the
post-capstone experimental, audit, synthesis and report iterations ---
206 recorded analysis iterations --- the Berkeley-course-derived
research audits and prototypes, the expanded statistical analyses, and
the new GRPO diagnostics and controllers. And it includes the E1--E14
benchmark campaign, in which a single Tinker-trained actor (base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit \texttt{995ad96e…}, with the
\longtok{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1…}
adapter at commit \texttt{64444133…}, in bfloat16) was evaluated against
fourteen suites under receipt, budget and decontamination governance.
Its results are reported per suite in Chapter 8 and never pooled; Table
C.3 is the single source for each lane's scope and terminal state.

Two further contributions deserve naming because they are contributions
of restraint rather than of result. The first is that the campaign's
negative findings were kept rather than discarded. When BankerToolBench
was re-run on the base model under a separate authorisation --- 100/100
trials, 3 hours 29 minutes, \$15.07 charged --- the mean reward was 0.0
across every trial. The cause was the serving bridge, which passed no
stop sequences: generation ran past the end-of-turn token, agents
terminated after 2 to 14 steps with no deliverables, and 27 or more
trajectories ended in role-token degeneration of the form
\texttt{user\ user\ assistant\ …}. The zero is therefore retained as an
infrastructure finding, not a model result (Chapter 8 §8.5). The second
is the discipline applied to the project's own claims: the same-stack
audit that invalidated the pre-audit aggregator's ``disappears'' verdict
did so on the strength of the study's own locked minimum detectable
effect, and the permitted wording it left in place is narrower than the
finding it refuted.

The report does not reallocate the shared runtime code. The inherited
repository remains where it was; the Semester 4 internal reports cite
their canonical LaTeX sources and evidence directories rather than
duplicating them, and the distinction recorded here is a distinction
about academic contribution, not about file ownership.

\section{Organisation of the Report}\label{organisation-of-the-report}

The report is organised in four parts.

\textbf{Part I --- Foundations} contains the present chapter, followed
by Chapter 2, the literature survey, which situates the work against the
RLHF and RLVR literature, the critic-free line of estimators, the
group-size and advantage-estimation literature, and the reproducibility
and reporting literature on which the provenance argument depends; and
Chapter 3, the system requirements specification, which states the
functional and non-functional requirements the harness, the telemetry
layer and the campaign infrastructure were built to satisfy.

\textbf{Part II --- Design and Implementation} contains Chapter 4, the
proposed methodology, which sets out the measurement design, the
statistical protocol, the evidence rules and the study-to-evidence
mapping; and Chapter 5, the implementation details, covering the
multi-framework harness, the per-step telemetry and raw tensor
persistence, the campaign launcher and its receipt and budget
governance, and the registry and validator components.

\textbf{Part III --- Results} contains three chapters. Chapter 6 reports
the core GRPO studies --- the cross-library and cross-scale scaling
work, the ZVF diagnostic, the group-size and contrast-density sweep, and
the length-bias and held-out generalisation study, corresponding to P1
through P4. Chapter 7 reports the reporting, registry and controller
work, corresponding to P5 through P7, including the minimum reporting
standard, the machine-readable stack registry, and the simulated
behaviour of the adaptive group-size controller. Chapter 8 reports the
E1--E14 benchmark campaign: the actor, the governance model, the
strictly completed scopes, the original-contract scores, the incomplete
and blocked lanes, the verification results and the campaign's terminal
states.

\textbf{Part IV --- Synthesis} contains Chapter 9, which draws the
cross-study synthesis, states the threats to validity, gives the
conclusions the evidence will bear, and sets out future work and the
reopen conditions under which the blocked lanes could be completed.

Seven appendices follow. Appendix A gives the complete run registry.
Appendix B documents reproducibility and provenance, including the
inherited-versus-individual boundary and the environment records needed
to reproduce the reported results. Appendix C gives the notation,
symbols and abbreviations used throughout, and its Table C.3 is the
single record of each E1--E14 lane's scope, status and receipt. Appendix
D holds the lane-by-lane campaign detail moved out of Chapter 8.
Appendix E lists every correction applied to an internal source
document. Appendix F reports the applied fraud-detection side study
(P8), which uses synthetic data and supervised fine-tuning and so sits
outside the RL contribution. Appendix G is the evidence map: the source
file behind every claim, section by section. Section 1.8, below, fixes
the canonical value of every quantity that the sources report more than
once.

\section{Canonical Numbers}\label{canonical-numbers}

Several quantities in this report were measured more than once, in
different regimes, and appear in the sources with different values.
Table 1.1 fixes one row per measurement, with the model, task and group
size it belongs to, the number of seeds and evaluation items behind it,
and the file it comes from. Where the text quotes one of these
quantities, this table is the reference, and a value that differs from
its row is a different measurement, not a correction. Per-lane E1--E14
figures are kept in Table C.3.

\textbf{Canonical numbers.}

\chapter{Literature Survey}\label{literature-survey}

This chapter surveys the prior work on which the project is built and
the gap it occupies. It revises the survey of the Phase-1 report,
retaining that survey's seven-part structure and updating each part with
the material the Semester 4 work added: the P1--P8 internal reports and
the E1--E14 evaluation campaign. Material on RLHF, PPO, DPO, GRPO,
DeepSeek-R1, parameter-efficient fine-tuning and evaluation benchmarks
also draws on the Semester-3 Group 6 capstone literature survey
\citep{group6capstone2026survey}, a group work of six authors of whom
the present author was one; where this chapter reuses that survey's
findings they are attributed to it here. Two rules of construction carry
over from Phase 1. The first concerns proportion. The contribution of
this project is overwhelmingly a contribution of measurement and
reporting practice rather than of a new training algorithm, so the
literature that establishes measurement practice receives more room here
than the literature that establishes methods. The second concerns
evidence. A figure is quoted only where a repository source actually
states it; where a source states a finding without giving a number, the
finding is described qualitatively and the chapter says so. Where the
project's own documents state the same quantity differently, the
divergence is reported rather than silently reconciled, and Section 2.5
records one such divergence in full.

\section{From RLHF and PPO to Group-Relative
Methods}\label{from-rlhf-and-ppo-to-group-relative-methods}

Policy-gradient post-training of language models begins with the RLHF
pipeline. Christiano et al. \citep{christiano2017deeprlhf} established
the pattern of reward-model training on human pairwise preferences
followed by reinforcement learning against that reward model, on Atari
and MuJoCo tasks starting from a randomly initialised policy; the
supervised fine-tuning stage that precedes both in the language-model
pipeline was added in later work
\citep{stiennon2020summarize, ouyang2022instructgpt}. Ziegler et al.
\citep{ziegler2019finetuning} adapted the pattern to language,
collecting approximately 60,000 comparisons and, following Jaques et al.
\citep{jaques2017sequencetutor, jaques2019wayoffpolicy}, adopting the
per-token KL penalty against a frozen reference policy that has been
standard ever since. Stiennon et al. \citep{stiennon2020summarize}
scaled the recipe to a 6.7B summarisation model with 64,832 comparisons,
demonstrated transfer to an out-of-distribution summarisation set
(CNN/DailyMail), and produced early empirical evidence of reward
over-optimisation: continued optimisation against a frozen reward model
eventually degrades the true objective. Ouyang et al.
\citep{ouyang2022instructgpt} then built InstructGPT, whose three-stage
pipeline used roughly 40 contractors to produce about 13,000 supervised
fine-tuning prompts, about 33,000 reward-model prompts, and about 31,000
PPO prompts. Two details of that work matter for this project. First,
the reward model was 6B rather than 175B because the larger reward model
proved unstable during training --- a reminder that the reward model is
itself a trained component with its own failure modes. Second, the
preference data were collected as rankings of K ∈ \{4, \ldots, 9\}
responses, which yields C(K, 2) pairwise comparisons from a single
annotation batch; this is precisely the within-prompt comparative
structure that group-relative methods would later exploit directly.
InstructGPT's headline results were that a 1.3B InstructGPT was
preferred to the 175B GPT-3 base model, that the 175B InstructGPT was
preferred to GPT-3 in 85\% of evaluations, that hallucination on a
closed-domain task fell from 41\% to 21\%, that toxicity was reduced by
roughly 25\%, and that TruthfulQA performance roughly doubled;
inter-annotator agreement was approximately 73\%. The same source
records four limitations --- annotation cost, reward-model
over-optimisation, the difficulty of aligning to user intent rather than
to annotator preference, and the residual capability costs of alignment (Figure~\ref{fig:lineage}).


\fig{fig_lineage}
{Algorithmic lineage from RLHF through PPO to GRPO, with DPO as an orthogonal branch}
{Algorithmic lineage from RLHF through PPO to GRPO, with DPO as an orthogonal branch. Each transition removes a component or substitutes a cheaper estimator; GRPO removes the critic and replaces it with a group-relative baseline. DPO (dashed frame) removes the on-policy loop and the reward model rather than simplifying the critic, trading online exploration for stability. Dates are first publication or first use at scale. GRPO variants (DAPO, Dr.~GRPO and others) are discussed in the text and not drawn.}
{fig:lineage}



The optimiser for that pipeline was PPO \citep{schulman2017ppo}. PPO's
clipping mechanism, with ε ≈ 0.2, restricts each update's policy ratio,
and generalised advantage estimation \citep{schulman2016gae} with its
standard defaults (λ = 0.95, γ = 0.99) provides the advantage estimate.
Recasting language generation as an RL problem makes the state the
prompt plus generated prefix, the action the next token, the policy the
language model, and the reward sparse and terminal. The practical
consequence is a four-model architecture --- policy, reference, reward,
and value --- whose mixed-precision memory footprint is roughly four
times that of supervised fine-tuning alone; PPO-ptx mitigates capability
regression by mixing pretraining gradients into the update. Zheng et
al.'s ``Secrets of RLHF'' study \citep{zheng2023secretsrlhf} found that
policy constraints, not algorithmic choice, are the single most critical
factor in whether RLHF succeeds: without them, training exhibits reward
hacking, length explosion, and catastrophic pattern collapse. That
finding is the intellectual ancestor of the zero-variance analysis in
this project, because it locates the failure mode in the constraint
structure rather than in the objective.

Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), introduced with DeepSeekMath
\citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, removes the learned value network
altogether. For each prompt it samples a group of G completions, scores
them with a reward function, and standardises each completion's reward
against the group mean and standard deviation; the resulting z-score
serves as the advantage for every token of that completion. In the
DeepSeekMath configuration G = 64 and β = 0.04. Eliminating the value
model reduces the number of trained and served models from four to
three; the paper motivates this as reducing PPO's memory usage but does
not quantify the saving. DeepSeekMath-RL 7B reached 51.7\% top-1
accuracy on MATH against a 46.8\% supervised fine-tuning baseline,
60.9\% with self-consistency over 64 samples, GSM8K improved from 82.9\%
to 88.2\%, and CMATH improved from 84.6\% to 88.8\% despite no Chinese
data in the RL training set. The paper's iterative variant retains 10\%
of historical data in a replay buffer to prevent catastrophic forgetting
in the co-evolving reward model. RLOO \citep{ahmadian2024backtobasics}
is the closely related leave-one-out estimator that baselines each
sample against the mean of the \emph{other} samples in the group; it
already performs a within-group baseline, and is therefore directly
relevant to the signal-starvation analysis this project conducts.

DeepSeek-R1 \citep{guo2025deepseekr1} then demonstrated that pure
outcome-reward RL can elicit chain-of-thought behaviour from a base
model without supervised cold-start data. R1-Zero was trained on
DeepSeek-V3-Base, a 671B mixture-of-experts model, using rule-based
rewards only and no neural reward model. AIME 2024 pass@1 rose from
15.6\% to 71.0\% over training in the original preprint --- the
\emph{Nature} version cited here reports 77.9\% after extended training
--- with consensus decoding over 64 samples (cons@64) reaching 86.7\%
and MATH-500 reaching 95.9\%. The source states the training
configuration as 16 sampled outputs per question (the group size), a
training batch of 512 outputs (32 questions × 16 samples), a learning
rate of 3 × 10⁻⁶, a KL coefficient of 0.001, and approximately 10,400
policy update steps. The paper documents spontaneous chain-of-thought,
self-verification, and the widely reported ``aha moment'' as emergent
rather than taught behaviours. The full R1 model, trained with a
four-stage pipeline that includes a language-consistency reward, reports
79.8 on AIME, 97.3 on MATH-500, and 71.5 on GPQA, against 79.2 / 96.4 /
75.7 for o1-1217. Distillation into six students from 1.5B to 70B
produced Distill-Qwen-1.5B at 28.9\% AIME and 83.9\% MATH-500, and
Distill-Qwen-32B at 72.6\% and 94.3\%, against 47.0\% and 91.6\% for
Qwen2.5-32B-Zero trained from scratch; the 7B and 16B base models failed
to show meaningful reasoning gains from direct RL.

For this project, the significance of that lineage is narrower than the
headline numbers suggest. GRPO's defining operation is a within-group
standardisation, and a standardisation is undefined when the group's
rewards are all identical. That degenerate case is not an edge case: it
is the modal case early in training on a hard task and late in training
on an easy one. The rest of this chapter and the empirical chapters that
follow are concerned with what that degeneracy does to the training
signal, and with how honestly the field currently reports it.

\section{Direct Preference Optimisation as a
Contrast}\label{direct-preference-optimisation-as-a-contrast}

An orthogonal branch of post-training removes on-policy sampling
entirely. Direct Preference Optimisation (DPO) \citep{rafailov2023dpo}
exploits the fact that the RLHF objective with a KL constraint admits a
closed-form optimal policy, which permits the reward to be
reparameterised in terms of the policy itself. The result is a simple
classification loss over a fixed dataset of preference pairs, with no
reward model, no value model, and no sampling loop. Reported savings are
40--60\% in both GPU memory and wall-clock time relative to PPO. Four
limitations are documented in the same source: the training data are
static and offline, so the implicit reward goes stale as the policy
drifts; there is no online exploration beyond the dataset's support; the
loss can lower the absolute likelihood of preferred completions while
widening their margin over dispreferred ones (likelihood displacement);
and the method has weaker credit assignment for long-horizon reasoning,
where correctness of later steps depends on earlier ones.

The contrast is structurally instructive for this project. DPO cannot
suffer zero-variance collapse, because it never standardises within a
sampled group --- its contrast is supplied by the dataset rather than
constructed at training time. But it equally cannot exploit a verifier
that is available in the loop, which is the property that makes RLVR
attractive on mathematics. GRPO's zero-variance pathology is therefore
the price of its on-policy, verifier-in-the-loop design, and measuring
that price is exactly what makes the pathology worth quantifying. The
DPO family has since diversified into inverse-preference and non-paired
variants --- IPO \citep{azar2023ipo}, KTO \citep{ethayarajh2024kto},
ORPO \citep{hong2024orpo}, SimPO \citep{meng2024simpo}, and SLiC
\citep{zhao2023slichf} among them --- which the project's cross-library
protocol treats as no-rollout baselines.

The boundary between the two families is also less sharp than it first
appears. Wu et al. \citep{wu2025ittakestwo} argue that GRPO is
``secretly DPO'' --- that its efficacy stems from an implicit
contrastive objective, which makes it structurally related to DPO ---
and report that 2-GRPO retains 97.6\% of the performance of 16-GRPO at
12.5\% of the rollouts (arXiv v3; the earlier v2 reported 98.1\%). That
reduction is the theoretical counterpart of the PCD statistic this
project develops: if a group is exactly as informative as the
correct/incorrect pairs it contains, then the pairwise-contrast density
PCD = ((G − 1)/G) · E{[}p(1 − p){]} is the natural magnitude-aware
summary of a group's usable signal, and the group-size question becomes
a question about how much contrast a given sampling budget buys. The
present project is careful about what it inherits from that argument:
the reduction is cited as prior theory, while the empirical claims
resting on it are scoped to this project's own measurements, reported in
Chapter 6.

\section{Fixing GRPO's Biases and
Collapse}\label{fixing-grpos-biases-and-collapse}

A dense and fast-growing literature targets specific GRPO pathologies.
Dr.~GRPO \citep{liu2025understanding} removes two normalisation terms
from the standard objective --- the per-response 1/L factor and the
question-difficulty amplification --- restoring the sequence-level
gradient up to a global scale, and is motivated as a fix for an
optimisation bias in GRPO that artificially inflates response length,
especially of incorrect responses. DAPO \citep{yu2025dapo}, from
ByteDance, changes four things in one release: asymmetric Clip-Higher
clipping, dynamic sampling that resamples until the batch carries
signal, token-level loss normalisation, and over-long filtering; the
reported result is 50 points on AIME 2024 with a 32B model. GSPO
\citep{zheng2025gspo} (Qwen team, 2025) moves the importance ratio from
the token level to the sequence level and was credited with stabilising
the Qwen3 series, particularly its mixture-of-experts variants.
Production-side variants include VAPO \citep{yue2025vapo}, a value-based
PPO framework for long chain-of-thought reasoning.

A third thread attacks the zero-variance failure mode directly, and this
is the thread the project's own diagnostic sits in. GRESO
\citep{zheng2025greso} pre-screens prompts before sampling so that
uninformative prompts are never paid for. CPPO \citep{lin2025cppo}
prunes completions after rollout, reporting up to 7.98× speedup on
GSM8K. NGRPO \citep{nan2025ngrpo} adds advantage calibration and
asymmetric clipping so that all-incorrect groups still carry gradient.
Scaf-GRPO \citep{zhang2025scafgrpo} injects tiered scaffolding hints
when learning stagnates, reporting a 44.3\% relative improvement on AIME
2024. MO-GRPO \citep{ichihara2025mogrpo} addresses vanilla GRPO's
collapse to a single reward component under multiple objectives through
variance-based reward re-weighting. SSPO \citep{yang2025sspo} operates
at subsentence granularity to balance GSPO's stability against
token-level GRPO's expressiveness, raising GRPO's five-benchmark
mathematics average from 43.01 to 46.72 on Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B. RL-ZVP
\citep{le2025rlzvp} takes the opposite approach to filtering: it
extracts signal \emph{from} zero-variance prompts through entropy-guided
advantage shaping. AERO \citep{zhang2026aero} instead adapts the rollout
allocation and prunes rollouts to avoid zero-advantage dead zones. GVPO
\citep{zhang2025gvpo} re-weights by within-group variance. The tail
continues with λ-GRPO \citep{wang2025lambdagrpo}, MC-GRPO
\citep{kim2026mcgrpo} (median-centred advantage), G²RPO-A
\citep{guo2025g2rpoa}, ConSPO \citep{zhang2026conspo}, and Goldilocks RL
\citep{mahrooghi2026goldilocks}.

One act of bookkeeping in this literature deserves emphasis, because it
is a claim about what may \emph{not} be inferred. AVSPO
\citep{he2026avspo}, query recycling, ConSPO, and Goldilocks RL are
described in the project's own related-work source as prior or
concurrent interventions, not as evidence for this project's runner, and
the internal report explicitly claims neither priority nor an
intervention effect over them. The present thesis inherits that
disclaimer without qualification.

Against this background, the project's own contribution to the area is
deliberately meagre in algorithmic ambition and deliberately demanding
in evidential standard. The Zero-Variance Fraction (ZVF) --- the
fraction of groups whose completions are all correct or all incorrect
--- was measured across libraries, model scales, and training phases in
the companion ZVF report (P2), and that report stopped at description:
ZVF tracks collapse, drifts upward, and is mechanically entangled with
accuracy and group size, so it declined to promote ZVF to a predictive
or causal statistic. A minimal binomial model of group degeneracy was
then proposed, in which the expected ZVF indicator is
\(h_G(p) = p^G + (1-p)^G\) and the usable signal is
\(S = p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\); three regularities follow in closed form, each
paired with an empirical check drawn from a 368-run Weights \& Biases
audit that counts all logged runs and is therefore a superset of the
benchmark's 70+ core training runs. Because \(h_G(p)\) is symmetric
under \(p \leftrightarrow 1-p\), ZVF assigns the same value to a
mastered prompt and to a hopeless one, producing the U-shape measured
across seven models; and because ZVF is an existence statistic, adding
reward jitter of order 10⁻⁴ drives measured ZVF to zero while shifting
the measured within-group contrast by less than 10⁻⁶. The
pairwise-contrast density is proposed as the magnitude-aware
replacement, and the toy-scale validation reports a gradient-norm
correlation of corr = +0.71, marked directional only. The word
\emph{controller} in that work names the implemented policy, not an
established improvement: the decisive static-G = 16 matched-token
bakeoff remains unrun.

The one genuinely measured GRPO-variant comparison in the repository is
a Dr.~GRPO versus GRPO control. On Qwen2.5-0.5B two-digit arithmetic at
group size 8, 40 steps, and 5 seeds, both arms were at ceiling and
indistinguishable: GRPO 0.987 ± 0.003 against Dr.~GRPO 0.992 ± 0.003
(mean ± SE over 5 seeds; SDs 0.006 and 0.008), paired difference +0.5
percentage points, t = 1.05, df = 4, p = 0.35; mean completion length
was 4.74 against 4.71 tokens. At that ceiling, and with completions
under five tokens, the task cannot show either an accuracy or a length
difference. In a second regime --- Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct on GSM8K with a
200-token chain-of-thought cap and 3 seeds --- GRPO moved from 20.2\% to
26.3\% and Dr.~GRPO from 20.5\% to 25.5\%; per-seed McNemar tests were
significant in 2 of 3 seeds for GRPO (p = 0.071, 0.036, 0.016) and 1 of
3 for Dr.~GRPO (p = 0.122, 0.017, 0.122), and the pooled p \textless{}
10⁻³ is expressly rejected as overstating significance; with the seed as
the unit, one-sample t-tests on the three per-seed gains give p = 0.005
(GRPO) and p = 0.010 (Dr.~GRPO) (recomputed from
drgrpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot.json). Neither arm inflated length over 30 steps
(GRPO 195 → 180 tokens; Dr.~GRPO 194 → 187). The same source contains
head-to-head and rank-correlation tables whose values are
\emph{projections}, not measurements: the GRPO baseline row is a
synthetic baseline produced by a dry run of
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/variance\_\allowbreak{}mitigation\_\allowbreak{}integration.py},
the file states that it makes no measurement claim for any row of that
table, the correlation coefficients (0.78 / 0.66 / 0.63 / 0.31) carry
the same caveat, and the configuration proxies under
\texttt{platform\_local/unified/} are explicitly not faithful
reimplementations. Those numbers are reported here only to mark them as
unusable as evidence. The nearest thing to a headline cross-library
effect in the project is also the one that must be read most carefully:
holding the visible group size, learning rate, data, seed, and label
fixed while changing stacks moved final GSM8K reward from 85.6\% to
5.0\%, a 17× swing; the audit that followed found the managed stack had
also pinned a different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base on Tinker against
the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL), so the source describes this as
an under-specification exhibit rather than a backend-only causal effect.
The complementary telemetry fact is that running ``DAPO'' on an open
Colab trainer whose dynamic sampling demonstrably filters zero-variance
groups produced mean ZVF 0.00, while running ``DAPO'' on a closed
managed stack where only an asymmetric-clip surrogate is user-settable
produced mean ZVF 0.58 --- the same label, opposite telemetry.

\section{Verifiable Rewards, Frameworks and Reporting
Practice}\label{verifiable-rewards-frameworks-and-reporting-practice}

RLVR --- reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards --- was
formalised for open post-training in TÜLU 3 \citep{lambert2024tulu3},
which is the source this project follows in treating a programmatic
checker rather than a learned preference model as the reward. The
implementation landscape the project unifies is crowded and
heterogeneous. On the training side it includes TRL, OpenRLHF
\citep{hu2024openrlhf}, veRL (HybridFlow) \citep{sheng2025hybridflow},
NeMo-RL, DeepSpeed-Chat \citep{yao2023deepspeedchat}, trlX, Axolotl, and
Tinker as a managed back-end; each makes different default choices about
advantage normalisation, KL estimators, tokenisation loss masking,
rollout batching, and importance-sampling granularity, and the project
identifies those differences as the principal source of the
cross-library gap it quantifies. On the inference side, vLLM
\citep{kwon2023vllm} and SGLang \citep{zheng2024sglang} supply the
rollout backends on which modern frameworks depend, with FlashAttention
\citep{dao2022flashattention} and LoRA \citep{hu2021lora} as
load-bearing kernels. Tinker is adopted here through its
\longtok{message\_\allowbreak{}with\_\allowbreak{}tool} interface as the neutral substrate for
tool-use extensions, and the same source explicitly cites tool-use and
agentic RL settings as concurrent environments in which this project's
ZVF measurements will need to be re-evaluated rather than assumed to
transfer.

The evaluation benchmarks are equally well established and equally
awkward. GSM8K \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} comprises 8,500 problems split
7,473 / 1,319, on which a fine-tuned GPT-3 verifier reached 35\% at
release while current systems exceed 95\%; GSM8K-Platinum
\citep{vendrow2025platinum} has since documented label noise in the
original set, which is why the source describes the benchmark as
necessary but not sufficient. MATH \citep{hendrycks2021math} comprises
12,500 problems across seven subjects and five difficulty levels, on
which the original results reached 6.9\%; MATH-500 is the 500-problem
subset introduced with the Lightman process-supervision work
\citep{lightman2023lets}, on which current systems sit at roughly 99\%.
AIME comprises 15 problems per year with integer answers in 0--999;
reported AIME 2024 accuracies include GPT-5 at 95.7\% and Grok 4 at
94.3\%. Because these benchmarks are close to saturation, the source
frames chain-of-thought as the action space that makes them interesting
at all, and identifies process reward models as the bridge from terminal
outcome signals to step-level credit.

Reporting practice is where the project's own contribution is anchored,
and its lineage is explicit. Model Cards \citep{mitchell2019modelcards}
introduced documentation of intended use, out-of-scope use, and
per-slice evaluation results; Datasheets for Datasets
\citep{gebru2021datasheets} extended the idea to datasets, covering
composition, preprocessing, annotation process, and
discriminatory-impact mitigation; Data Statements
\citep{bender2018datastatements} added the speaker-demographic axis; and
Data Cards \citep{pushkarna2022datacards} bridged research and industry
practice with schema, provenance, sampling-distribution, and
audit-transparency fields. A cross-coupling audit at iteration 109 maps
these four works onto the project's legacy 18-field experimental
manifest, showing that they cover 12 distinct fields, that the remaining
six are either RL-specific (per-step ZVF, group-size schedule) or
stack-coverage items with no direct analogue in the prior literature,
and that items 9--12 --- annotation process and annotator demographics
--- have the lowest evidence density in the live manifests. The same
iteration verified all four entries against CrossRef metadata (4/4 pass
on year, five-gram title, and author-family overlap ≥ 0.6) and
retrofitted two entries that had been present in the bibliography but
never cited. The resulting standard, MIN-REPORT-RL, has eight items:
seven run-manifest fields and one evaluation item (held-out pass@k),
which belongs to the evaluation report rather than to run-start stack
identity. The count was aligned at eight on 2026-07-11, and this thesis
uses ``the eight-item standard'' and ``the seven manifest fields'' in
that sense throughout.

The reproducibility literature to which this standard is a deliberate
echo is older and better established. Henderson et al.
\citep{henderson2018deep} showed that deep-RL results depend on
codebase, hyperparameters, and even the choice of random seeds, to the
point that the same algorithm from two repositories behaves as two
algorithms. Colas et al. \citep{colas2019hitchhiker} and Agarwal et al.
\citep{agarwal2021deep} (\texttt{rliable}) supplied the statistical
repair kit --- rigorous comparison protocols and small-sample-aware
aggregate metrics with interquartile-mean reporting and performance
profiles. Jordan et al. \citep{jordan2024benchmarking} argued that
rigorous benchmarking in RL is computationally prohibitive at the scale
it would require, and called for an additional experimentation paradigm
to complement it. Dodge et al. \citep{dodge2019show} proposed reporting
expected validation performance as a function of compute budget; the
project's run manifest is the RL-for-LLM analogue, replacing
\emph{budget} with \emph{stack}. Pineau et al.
\citep{pineau2021reproducibility} formalised the NeurIPS reproducibility
criteria, and the project draws the explicit lesson that self-attested
checklists have limits, which motivates an artifact-computed badge
rather than a declared one. Statistical treatments of evaluation noise
(Miller \citep{miller2024errorbars}; Zhang et al.) quantify the
uncertainty floor against which the project's own within-noise findings
should be read, and the evaluation-stack sensitivity documented by the
LM Evaluation Harness effort \citep{biderman2024lmeval}, by audits of
reasoning evaluations \citep{hochlehnert2025sober}, and by contamination
studies of the data side of the same coin, together establish that RL
\emph{training} inherits all of this --- the verifier is an evaluator in
the loop --- plus the additional vectors unique to on-policy
optimisation.

Three existing resources come closest to the project's registry and are
the reason it exists in its present form. HELM \citep{liang2022helm}
aggregates evaluation across a large model and scenario matrix, but its
unit is the (model, scenario) cell, not the training stack. The LM
Evaluation Harness \citep{biderman2024lmeval} standardises evaluation by
\emph{executing} implementations, whereas the registry here standardises
by \emph{describing} stacks it cannot execute --- many of them closed
--- which is precisely why the project required a null-versus-absent
convention and an R5 rung that records un-auditability rather than
converting it into a false value. Papers with Code catalogued
paper--code--leaderboard linkage at scale, stopped at the repository
boundary, and was discontinued in July 2025; the source treats its fate
as a governance cautionary tale. The project's own claim to novelty is
narrow and stated negatively: no existing resource provides the executed
loss form at GRPO-variant granularity, per-component implementation
status, or an unauditability marker.

\section{Scaling and Layer-Adaptation
Context}\label{scaling-and-layer-adaptation-context}

Compute-optimal pretraining is governed by well-characterised scaling
laws (Kaplan et al. \citep{kaplan2020scaling}; Hoffmann et al.,
Chinchilla \citep{hoffmann2022chinchilla}), but RL post-training is much
less well characterised, and the sources the project builds on separate
cleanly into those that predict and those that describe. Hilton et al.
\citep{hilton2023rlscaling} derived compute-efficiency frontiers for
single-agent RL across Procgen games, Dota 2 and a toy MNIST-based task.
For RLHF, Gao et al. \citep{gao2023scaling} characterised the
over-optimisation curve of reward models --- the point at which
additional optimisation against a frozen reward model buys a decreasing
true objective --- and Rafailov et al. \citep{rafailov2024rlhfscaling}
extended over-optimisation scaling laws to direct alignment algorithms
(DPO, IPO and SLiC), which have no separate proxy reward model.
Nimmaturi et al. \citep{nimmaturi2025predictive} derived an empirical
scaling law specifically for GRPO, identifying three training phases
(slow start, rapid improvement, plateau) and reporting that most of the
benefit is exhausted by roughly 80\% of a single epoch. Tan et al.
\citep{tan2025scalingrl} treat group size as a budget knob in an
empirical scaling study of GRPO post-training, in an appendix analysis
showing that the optimal rollout size shifts with the training budget.
Snell et al. \citep{snell2024testtime} treat inference-time compute as a
scaling axis in its own right, and the project's own related-work source
cites these as the works against which its measurements should be read.

The project's scaling study covers 70+ runs across seven RL libraries
and five model families. This thesis uses one model range throughout:
0.6B--1T, with the anchors above 235B single-seed and descriptive. The
rule is that the range spans every model with at least one completed run
in the P1 roster. The source documents state narrower ranges because
each covers a subset: 0.6B--\textasciitilde671B in the P2, P3 and P4
introductions, and 0.6B--235B for the multi-family data in the
related-work source. The principal empirical result of the scaling work
is a null: across that range, no reliable scaling law is recovered from
reward curves. The companion group-size study adds a second bounded
negative: a three-seed sweep does not establish an optimum, since the
measured arms are statistically indistinguishable, and the measured
signal-to-noise ratio grows at only about 52\% of the √G ideal, with G ∈
\{2, 4, 8, 16\} measured and G ∈ \{4, 8, 16, 32, 64\} explicitly
reconstructed rather than run.

Layer adaptation supplies the second half of this section's context, and
connects the project's white-box investigation to the
parameter-efficient fine-tuning literature. LoRA \citep{hu2021lora}
freezes the pretrained weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices
into the attention projections, so that a d × d projection at d = 4096
with rank r = 16 trains 2dr rather than d² parameters, a 128× reduction
(this chapter's arithmetic; the paper itself reports up to 10,000× fewer
trainable parameters for GPT-3 175B). QLoRA \citep{dettmers2023qlora}
extends this by quantising the frozen base to NF4 and adding double
quantisation, which saves about 0.37 bits per parameter ---
approximately 3 GB for a 65B model --- and permits 65B fine-tuning on a
single 48 GB GPU. The motivating arithmetic is that Adam at mixed
precision costs roughly 16N bytes, exceeding 100 GB for a 7B model.
Prefix tuning, prompt tuning, and adapter layers are the earlier members
of the same family, and LoRA's advantages for RL specifically are that
the frozen base doubles as the reference policy and that the trainable
parameter count is small enough for modest hardware. The project's
layer-adaptation pillar asks a narrower and orthogonal question: where
inside the network GRPO-style adaptation concentrates, and whether the
layers that will matter can be identified early enough to freeze the
rest. The reported finding is moderate concentration of per-layer
adaptation, with the important negative that step-1 predictability does
\emph{not} survive scaling. This connects directly to Mukherjee et al.
\citep{mukherjee2025rlsubnetworks}, who found that RL fine-tuning
updates only 5--30\% of model parameters across seven algorithms and ten
model families, suggesting that RL activates a sparse subnetwork. The
length-bias pillar completes the scaling picture with its own bounded
null: under a 200-token GSM8K generation cap, GRPO and Dr.~GRPO produce
held-out gains that are indistinguishable at the project's seed budget,
and neither arm shows length inflation; the source is careful to state
that the 200-token cap is part of the estimand rather than a nuisance
detail, so the null bounds the capped, short-horizon regime and cannot
adjudicate uncapped reasoning or long agent trajectories.

\section{Process-, Tree- and Stability-Aware
Variants}\label{process--tree--and-stability-aware-variants}

Beyond the group-baseline fixes, a parallel line of work changes
\emph{what} signal drives the update. Process reward models supply
denser, step-level feedback instead of a single terminal reward, which
reduces the chance that a whole group collapses to identical terminal
rewards. Let's Verify Step by Step \citep{lightman2023lets} showed that
step-level PRMs substantially outperform outcome reward models on MATH;
Math-Shepherd \citep{wang2024mathshepherd} derives process rewards from
automatically generated process annotations, avoiding costly human
labels; PRM800K \citep{lightman2023lets} and PRMBench
\citep{song2025prmbench} supply step-level evaluation data; OmegaPRM
\citep{luo2024omegaprm} uses Monte-Carlo tree search to supervise
process rewards without human-in-the-loop; ReasonEval
\citep{xia2025reasoneval} scores reasoning steps for validity and
redundancy with trained LLM evaluators; and Implicit PRM
\citep{yuan2024implicitprm} learns process rewards implicitly from
outcome labels, with VersaPRM \citep{zeng2025versaprm} extending process
rewards to multi-domain tasks. Tree-of-thought search
\citep{yao2023tot}, self-consistency \citep{wang2023selfconsistency},
and critic-style post-hoc verifiers all either benefit from or
substitute for process supervision. The same source documents the
counter-current that matters most here: VerifyBench and ``A Sober Look''
\citep{hochlehnert2025sober} warn that reward hacking and evaluation
artefacts can inflate perceived gains on reasoning benchmarks, which is
the project's stated motivation for insisting on binary verifiable
rewards in its own mathematics work.

Stability-aware variants constrain the update itself. GSPO's
sequence-level importance ratio, discussed above, is the clearest
instance, credited with stabilising the Qwen3 mixture-of-experts series.
SSPO's subsentence-granularity objective is explicitly framed as
balancing GSPO's stability against token-level GRPO's expressiveness.
Entropy-guided advantage shaping in RL-ZVP regulates the update when the
group carries no reward contrast, and GRESO's pre-screening is a
sampling-side stability control. On the reasoning-RL side, GRPO-Lead
\citep{zhang2025grpolead} and LCPO \citep{aggarwal2025l1} tune reasoning
length through length-aware rewards, LIMR \citep{li2025limr} selects a
small high-impact subset of RL training data, Search-R1
\citep{jin2025searchr1} trains reasoning agents to interleave search
with chain-of-thought, and TTRL \citep{zuo2025ttrl} performs test-time
RL on unlabelled data using majority-vote rewards.

The Phase-1 survey's judgement on this body of work is retained without
amendment, because the Semester 4 evidence has not changed it. These
methods propose \emph{levers}; this project first builds the
\emph{instrumentation} needed to tell whether any such lever converts
wasted rollouts into held-out accuracy --- ZVF, group utilisation,
pairwise-contrast density, and the collapse decomposition. The nulls
reported in the results chapters are exactly the kind of evidence that
instrumentation is designed to produce, and they constitute a standing
caution against adopting a variant on the strength of a single-seed
improvement. The project applies that caution to its own proposals: the
adaptive group-size controller is described as an implemented policy
rather than an established improvement, and its decisive matched-token
comparison remains unrun.

of what is claimed and, more importantly, what is not. Table 2.1
reproduces the Phase-1 positioning and updates it with the Semester 4
evidence (Figure~\ref{fig:questions}).
