Three properties make the definition more than a restatement of the
training loop. First, it is non-tautological under a simple null: if
completions were independent Bernoulli draws with per-prompt success
probability \(p_g\), then
\(\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{ZVF}_t] = \mathbb{E}_{p_g}[p_g^K + (1-p_g)^K]\), a
quantity that depends on the model's accuracy distribution and the group
size rather than on the observed batch. The identity
\(\mathrm{pass@}G - p^G = 1 - \mathrm{ZVF}\) follows algebraically under
the same i.i.d.-Bernoulli model and is therefore reported as an
algebraic remark rather than as evidence. Second, it is checked against
that null on instrumented runs: a Qwen2.5-0.5B sweep over G ∈ \{2, 4, 8,
16\}, three seeds and 40 steps --- twelve runs and 480 logged steps ---
compares per-step observed ZVF with the Bernoulli-null prediction
\(p^G + (1-p)^G\). Because the null for each step is computed from the
same step's mean reward, a high correlation is partly mechanical, so the
comparison is designed as a shape check rather than a validation, and
the baseline used is the per-step average of the closed form (results in
Chapter 6 §6.3.3). Third, at larger scale the same instrument is run on
Qwen3-8B against GSM8K test problems through the Tinker managed API at G
= 8, temperature 1.0, over 200 problems and three seeds (sampling only,
no training; these are the same three stored tensors whose ZVF P2
recomputes), with the zero-variance groups decomposed into all-correct
and all-wrong and compared with the same null. Deviations from the null
in either direction are not read as evidence about dependence between
completions.

That the quantity is portable across libraries is a separate, weaker
claim, and the project states its limits explicitly. A normaliser at
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/zvf\_\allowbreak{}compute\_\allowbreak{}cross\_\allowbreak{}framework.py} maps
each framework's reward and mask fields onto the common definition ---
TRL's \texttt{rewards} with \texttt{batch\_size\ /\ group\_size} and
\texttt{completion\_mask}; Tinker's \texttt{rollout.reward} with
\texttt{rollout.group\_id} and \texttt{rollout.eos\_mask}; OpenRLHF's
\texttt{reward\_score} with \texttt{prompt\_index} and
\texttt{attention\_mask}; veRL's \texttt{data\_source/reward} with
\texttt{uid} and \texttt{response\_mask} --- and emits JSONL records of
the form \texttt{\{step,\allowbreak{}\ zvf,\allowbreak{}\ batch\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}reward\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}mean,\allowbreak{}\ n\_\allowbreak{}groups,\allowbreak{}\ K\}}.
The scope correction attached to that parser is that it normalises
reward logs and does not make its runner canonical GRPO; because the
project does not bundle raw per-group rollout JSONL for every framework,
no fresh four-way pointwise ZVF overlay is claimed, only the narrower
implementation-level portability claim. The threshold ε is fixed in
advance, and its sensitivity is reported with the P2 results in Chapter
6 §6.3.1. Boundary conditions are declared where the diagnostic does not
apply: dense or continuous reward signals, K = 1, SFT-saturated
baselines and non-outcome-reward RL all fall outside the setting in
which the definition is meaningful.

The methodological point of this section is that ZVF is a diagnostic and
not a metric. The design commits to that position before the results
rather than inferring it from them, and it takes four precautions.
First, because the statistic is unsigned, a high value is read as
consistent with either a solved or an unsolved task and never as a score
in one direction; the 368-run audit that shows both extremes is reported
in Chapter 7 §7.1 and §7.3. Second, a pooled association between ZVF and
performance is always broken down by task type before it is read,
because the task mix of a pool can set the sign of the association
(results in Chapter 6 §6.3.4). Third, the association with final
accuracy and with final held-out outcome is tested, but ZVF is not
promoted to a causal or incrementally predictive statistic whatever that
test returns. Fourth, no step-level p-value is computed on the 480
autocorrelated steps of the instrumented sweep, because treating
correlated within-run steps as independent samples would manufacture
significance. One further boundary is recorded for the same reason: a
variance-mitigation table in the internal report sources is a declared
simulation projection and not evidence, and its rows are read as
synthetic rather than as independent library deployments. The result of
these choices is a diagnostic that is useful for describing where within
a batch the learning signal is and is not, and that is explicitly not
proposed as a quantity to be maximised, ranked or reported as a headline
score.

\section{The Eight-Study Portfolio}\label{the-eight-study-portfolio}

Semester 3 delivered the shared code and the initial experimental
foundation; the Semester 4 programme --- the research direction, the
expanded experiments, the analysis and the P1--P8 internal reports ---
is the present author's solo continuation, and the roster records 206
analysis iterations behind the numbers reported in this thesis. The
portfolio is organised as eight studies, each written up as an
unpublished internal report, each asking one question and each carrying
its own claim boundary; the titles quoted below are the internal
reports' working titles. This section states the questions and designs;
the results are in Chapters 6--7 and, for P8, Appendix F. The questions
and boundaries are stated here because they are methodological
commitments: a study's question determines which comparisons it may
make, and the boundary states what it declines to conclude.

P1, ``Scaling Laws for GRPO Post-Training of Language Models: A
Cross-Library, Cross-Scale Study'', asks whether GRPO post-training
outcomes scale with model scale in a way that can be fitted and
extrapolated. Its evidence base is more than seventy runs across seven
RL libraries and five model families from 0.6B to 1T parameters, fitted
with saturating forms and tested for a cross-scale slope. The frontier
anchors --- Qwen3-235B-MoE, DeepSeek-V3.1, Kimi-K2 and Nemotron-120B ---
were run through the Tinker managed API in a single-seed,
descriptive-only regime, so the report's claim boundary is a descriptive
limits-and-identifiability audit rather than a positive scaling law;
matched multi-seed cross-scale runs are an explicit non-claim. Results
are in Chapter 6 §6.2.

P2, ``The Zero-Variance Fraction: A Descriptive Diagnostic for Signal
Starvation in GRPO'', asks how much of a training batch carries usable
group-relative signal, and whether that fraction predicts the final
outcome. The study characterises ZVF descriptively, treats the identity
of §4.3 as algebraic rather than empirical, tests the association with
final held-out outcome without promoting ZVF to a predictive statistic,
and marks the variance-mitigation table as a simulation projection; the
retired roster item that previously carried a causal framing now lives
under \longtok{archive/absorbed/R02\_\allowbreak{}main\_\allowbreak{}zvf/}. Results are in Chapter
6 §6.3.

P3, ``Group Size in GRPO: Contrast Density and the Bridge to DPO'', asks
how trainability varies with the group size G and whether GRPO admits an
all-pairs preference interpretation. Its measured sweep covers G ∈ \{2,
4, 8, 16\} on the near-ceiling arithmetic task, with seed-level
equivalence tests against G = 2 and a one-way test of held-out accuracy
across G. No directly trained matched-budget G = 32 cell exists, so
every G = 32 value comes from an explicitly labelled reconstructed
token-budget grid and is held as a hypothesis rather than a measurement.
The claim boundary is that the data may not be read as a universal
optimum, and the all-pairs preference reading resembling DPO is offered
as an interpretation and not as a proven equivalence. Results, including
a conditional utility audit over 505 tasks, are in Chapter 6 §6.4.

P4, ``Length Bias and Held-Out Generalization in GRPO and Dr.~GRPO'',
asks whether the verbosity trap associated with GRPO appears in the
measured regime and whether either method changes held-out accuracy. Its
controlled comparison runs GRPO against Dr.~GRPO on a near-ceiling
arithmetic task and in a short-horizon GSM8K chain-of-thought regime
with a 200-token generation cap, alongside a multi-seed Qwen3-8B pre-RL
versus post-RL held-out control. The 200-token cap bounds the claim:
under a cap that already controls length, absence of the verbosity
signature would be the expected outcome rather than a positive finding.
Results are in Chapter 6 §6.5.

P5, ``Report the Stack, Not the Label: RL-for-LLM Results Are
Stack-Conditioned'', asks how much of a reported result is attributable
to the stack rather than to the algorithm label, and what a reportable
stack must contain. Three exhibits carry the argument: a nominally
matched-configuration backend audit, a same-label ``DAPO'' comparison
between true dynamic sampling on an open trainer and an asymmetric-clip
surrogate on a closed stack, and a twelve-cell head-to-head of four
labelled algorithms on one fixed stack; their results are in Chapter 7
§7.1. The study proposes an eight-item minimum reportable stack (seven
run-manifest fields and one evaluation item) --- loss form;
reference-policy and KL handling; sampler, backend and precision
including base-checkpoint identity by revision or hash; per-step ZVF and
gradient-utilisation trajectory; group-size schedule; a disjoint
held-out split; decontamination plus a parser-robustness probe; and
held-out pass@k curves alongside pass@1 --- and formalises an
RL-reproducibility threat model with flip-risk grades R0--R5. This is
the standard this thesis is written to.

P6, ``GRPO-Registry: A Machine-Readable Catalog of Group-Relative RL
Stacks and Their Variant Deltas'', asks whether stacks can be compared
mechanically rather than through prose. It defines stack records
carrying the seven manifest fields of the MIN-REPORT-RL standard, a
per-component variant-delta status, three variant-delta records verified
against the DAPO, Dr.~GRPO and GSPO papers, twenty seed stack entries,
and a reference CLI with a 0--100 MIN-REPORT auditor badge and a
\texttt{stackdiff} command that maps a pair of entries to an R0--R5
flip-risk verdict. The study is explicitly a resource study rather than
a second ranking, and it cites P5's seventeen-fold exhibit only to
motivate the schema. Results are in Chapter 7 §7.2.

P7, ``From Diagnostic to Controller: A Signal-Starvation Theory of GRPO
and an Adaptive Group-Size Intervention'', asks whether the diagnostic
of §4.3 can be turned into a control signal. It models usable learning
signal per prompt as \(S = p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\) with
\(h_G(p) = p^G + (1-p)^G\), yielding three testable consequences: that
the expected ZVF indicator equals \(p^G + (1-p)^G\); that larger G
lowers ZVF; and that gradient magnitude tracks p(1 − p). Each
consequence is paired with a measurement that could falsify it: the
368-run audit, a model-by-group-size grid, and a toy-scale
open-backward-pass experiment whose result is admitted as directional
only. The study promotes pairwise-contrast density, PCD = (G − 1)/G ·
E{[}p(1 − p){]}, as the candidate control signal, subject to a
micro-jitter falsification test against ZVF, and then audits an adaptive
group-size controller against fixed recipes and DAPO-style dynamic
sampling on a four-arm open trainer and in a counterfactual controller
trace. Results are in Chapter 7 §7.3. The methodological commitment that
governs this study is stated at its head: adaptive group size is not
promoted, and no result establishes prospective superiority over static
G = 16, Dr.~GRPO or dynamic sampling.

P8 occupies the eighth slot with a study outside the RL programme, ``LLM
vs.~XGBoost in Credit-Card Fraud: Sensor and Scribe, Not Scorer''. Its
question is whether a large language model should be used as the fraud
scorer or as a component around one, and it is designed as a comparison
of a boosted-tree model with a Qwen3.5-4B scorer on held-out splits,
with the scorer's cost as a triage component recorded alongside its
accuracy. Results are in Appendix F. The roster evolved after the
semester-4 title list was fixed, and the present position is recorded as
it stands: the fraud study is parked outside the ZVF thesis, contributes
no RL evidence, and is retained for its measurement discipline; the
active P8 slot in the current report roster is an exploratory workshop
artifact. No result from P8 is used anywhere in this thesis to support
an RL claim.

\section{The Design of the E1--E14 Held-Out Evaluation
Campaign}\label{the-design-of-the-e1e14-held-out-evaluation-campaign}

The evaluation campaign is the applied half of the programme and is
deliberately narrow in a way that makes its results interpretable: it
evaluates one actor against fourteen benchmark suites that were not part
of its training data under a receipt, budget and decontamination regime,
instead of training many actors and comparing them. The single actor is
the base model \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit
\texttt{995ad96e…}, with adapter
\longtok{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1…}
at commit \texttt{64444133…}, in bfloat16. How that adapter was trained
is reconstructed in Chapter 8 §8.1.1. ``Held-out'' is used here in a
narrow sense: the actor was trained only on API-Bank-RLVR and SWE-Gym,
and none of the fourteen suites is in that training data. It does not
mean that every suite was decontamination-checked. The content check
below covers LAB-Bench, AgentDojo and Omni-MATH, the E11 receipt records
its own verified decontamination, and the E10 receipt itself declares
\texttt{heldout\_claim:\ false}. One actor fixes the object of study:
every reported per-suite figure describes the same frozen policy, so a
difference between two suites is a property of the suite and not of a
model swap. The design consequence is that the campaign cannot support
model-comparison claims at all, and it makes none (Figure~\ref{fig:campaign}).


\fig{fig_campaign_design}
{Design of the E1--E14 native-benchmark evaluation campaign}
{Design of the E1--E14 native-benchmark evaluation campaign. One frozen actor serves all fourteen lanes, and each suite is graded by its own native evaluator at a pinned revision. Each lane lists its original-contract suite and, where one exists, its replacement scope side by side; Figure~\ref{fig:lanes} names the replacement scope where one exists. Figures and terminal states come from the 2026-09-19 ledger (\longtok{E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md} and \texttt{finish/Pending\_Experiments.md}; 11/11 deterministic checks pass), updated with the six replacement scopes scored on 2026-09-27 ($\star$; \longtok{finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/<lane>/result.json}), which are separate receipts and are never pooled with a lane's original-contract figure. E14's $2271/4428 = 51.29\%$ counts the 2 unjudged rows as failures; the official scorer reports $51.31\% = 2271/4426$ on the judged rows.}
{fig:campaign}



Evaluators are the suites' own scorers, used natively rather than
reimplemented, and they are pinned by revision. The surviving native
runners live under \texttt{zvf-program/flagship/}, including
\longtok{e1\_\allowbreak{}swe\_\allowbreak{}bench\_\allowbreak{}pro\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}eval.py},
\longtok{e11\_\allowbreak{}verilog\_\allowbreak{}eval\_\allowbreak{}decontamination.py},
\longtok{eval\_\allowbreak{}pavlov\_\allowbreak{}sdab.py}, \longtok{eval\_\allowbreak{}pavlov\_\allowbreak{}xlam.py},
\longtok{mle\_\allowbreak{}bench\_\allowbreak{}eval.py} and \longtok{eval\_\allowbreak{}apex\_\allowbreak{}agents.py}. The
evaluation-side revisions pinned for the decontamination audit are
LAB-Bench \longtok{998a8e0a40cf116c80e1b0e7a805ebb5fb9fa838}, AgentDojo
\longtok{089ed468cf3ed0322acc66b0211f26d9d90dbf60} and Omni-MATH
\longtok{40ba231d8f16e29ecd40e6407e2c8640145a8f62}, with the
training-side envelope pinned at API-Bank-RLVR
\longtok{bf67c42626f02c305514b1df16dcabc5fc616333} (597 rows, MIT) and
SWE-Gym \longtok{bb94ed9e39bbeb96a7fcbfb533b80f25a7fd59cb} (2,438 train
rows, MIT). Using the native scorer is a substantive choice and not a
convenience: when the Omni-MATH lane produced two rows whose judged
outputs were truncated before any equivalence verdict, the native
scorer's omission of missing-verdict rows was adjudicated as correct
official behaviour rather than a parser bug, and the lane closed with no
re-judge because re-judging would deviate from the official scorer and
generate new evidence with no effect on the score.

For the training-selection protocol, sampling is frozen rather than
merely recorded: temperature 0.1, top-p 0.95, one sample per row, a
maximum of 128 response tokens, with the service seed fixed where the
provider supports it and otherwise recorded in the receipt. Prompt and
response limits are 1,200 and 128 tokens respectively, and the same
source requires the strict verifier implementation to be hashed before
paid work, with a perfect call defined as a verifier score of exactly
1.0 and no parser, target, split or verifier change permitted
mid-campaign. Freezing the sampler addresses the failure mode in which a
benchmark score quietly improves because decoding became more favourable
between runs; it also fixes the comparison between the base reference
and the adapter, which is evaluated side by side on untouched manifests.
The 128-token setting belongs to that protocol only. The E1--E14 lanes
were decoded under each suite's own settings, which range from 1,024 to
8,192 output tokens at temperatures 0 to 0.2, and are recorded per lane
in the serving table of Chapter 8 §8.1.2. The actor's own training run
also departed from the protocol in several fields (§8.1.1).

The receipt model is freeze-before-paid-work, and it is the part of the
campaign design that carries the most methodological weight. Before any
paid phase a run must record an immutable base-model revision, tokenizer
revision, container or environment digest and adapter revision; the
selection slice and the disjoint holdout are re-created from a pinned
dataset revision and sealed, are write-once, are not mounted in
training, and the word ``holdout'' is reserved for a suite or manifest
only once its split, task-ID, license, runtime and decontamination
receipts are complete. The selection slice is used for arm decisions and
is therefore unsuitable for confirmatory claims; the final evaluation
manifests are never used to choose an arm, alter a prompt, or re-run a
failed suite with a more favourable split. Tracking is verified before
spend rather than after: an online Weights \& Biases run must be
initialised and its Tinker run identifier recorded before the first paid
step, every step records loss, reward, configuration, seed, split
identity and cumulative cost, and every checkpoint is published to
Hugging Face with a unique repository or revision URL, with a failure of
any of these stopping the run fail-closed. If a receipt is missing, the
phase is blocked rather than silently shortened. At handoff the protocol
requires four separate evidence classes to be reported apart: completed
receipts; operational and provenance receipts; prospective gates and
budget projections; and admissible final results, the last existing only
if every gate passed.

That last distinction has teeth in practice, and the campaign's
disposition record shows it. The portfolio-level protocol defines a
predeclared equal-domain macro average over sixteen domains, a
conjunctive coverage gate, an integrity gate, a global improvement gate
requiring the lower bound of a one-sided 95\% interval for the portfolio
difference to exceed zero, a domain no-regression guard with a
predeclared five-percentage-point tolerance, and a safety guard bounding
any violation increase by two percentage points --- and it states that a
cost cap may not be met by dropping domains or suites, and that a suite
or domain which is missing, underpowered, contaminated or outside the
cost cap must be labelled incomplete or blocked, ``not negative and not
positive''. The portfolio training phase was never launched, so the
campaign does not report a portfolio result; the recorded outcome is a
set of per-lane evaluations of the one adapter, and the protocol's own
instruction that a partial subset is not a portfolio result and may not
be relabelled as one is honoured throughout.

Lane dispositions are recorded in a seven-state taxonomy rather than as
pass or fail: COMPLETE, for lanes with verified full-coverage results
and no open gate (E8 public, E10 benign, E11); COMPLETE\_\allowbreak{}TERMINAL\_\allowbreak{}NOTE,
for a lane complete under the native protocol with a recorded terminal
note (E14 replacement); CLOSED\_EXTERNAL, for lanes with no local or
paid path, closed by written closure records (E3, E7, E8-private,
E10-private, E12, E14-private); PENDING\_QUOTA, for lanes gated on cloud
quota resolution alone (E6, E9); AMENDMENT\_\allowbreak{}ACCEPTED\_\allowbreak{}LAUNCH\_\allowbreak{}PENDING
(E2, E13); REBUILD\_\allowbreak{}READY\_\allowbreak{}LAUNCH\_\allowbreak{}PENDING (E1 wave10, E5 successor27);
and CLOSED\_PARTIAL (E4). Those are the states of the 19 September
ledger. The three pending states were emptied on 27 September, when the
E1, E2, E5, E6, E9 and E13 replacement scopes were run to completion
(§8.4). This report assigns them COMPLETE on coverage, with two
qualifications recorded against the label: E6 still has 108 tasks
awaiting a judge, and E1's coverage spans two contracts reported
separately. The taxonomy exists so that a lane which has not been
measured is not silently absorbed into a percentage. Carried alongside
it is the project's evidence rule, stated in the ledger of record and
repeated in every results artefact: original-contract and
replacement-scope numbers are never pooled. The rule is not cosmetic.
The original-contract E1 lane and its replacement scope are different
suites with different task populations, and a table that added them
would produce a number that corresponds to no benchmark.

Decontamination is handled as a declared deterministic procedure with
its own stated limits. The check hashes complete UTF-8 fields and then
normalised fields with SHA-256; for fields of at least eight tokens it
hashes each consecutive eight-token sequence and computes exact set
Jaccard similarity for every training and evaluation pair sharing a
shingle; and it tests whether an entire normalised evaluation field is a
token-bounded contiguous substring of a training field. Against the
pinned training envelope, the per-suite inventories were 1,967 LAB-Bench
tasks over 13,113 text fields with 5,105 eligible for eight-token near
checks, 97 AgentDojo tasks over 71,966 fields with 9,382 eligible, and
4,428 Omni-MATH tasks over 13,284 fields with 9,394 eligible; the count
of near pairs at or above 0.80 was zero in all three suites, and the
maximum eight-gram Jaccard similarity was 0 for LAB-Bench and AgentDojo
and 0.13414634146341464 for Omni-MATH, below the declared 0.80
threshold, with all 1,338 candidate pairs sharing an eight-gram
examined. Short exact matches of zero to three normalised tokens were
retained and enumerated rather than dropped --- the longest examples
being seven characters in the LAB and Omni inventories and at most
seventeen raw characters in AgentDojo, such as ``Paris'' and
``Conference Room A'' --- and the audit states plainly that these
collisions do not demonstrate duplication of a task but do make an
unqualified ``zero overlap'' claim incorrect. The audit's scope limits
are recorded with equal care: it does not reconstruct which training
records were consumed: 512 is the size of the training pool recorded in
the E11 decontamination receipt
(\longtok{selected\_\allowbreak{}training\_\allowbreak{}row\_\allowbreak{}count}), from which the 40-step run
drew 80 prompts at two per step, and the transform and consumed-row
inventory that would identify them were not recorded; it establishes a
content comparison against the verified historical source envelope
rather than proving global pretraining cleanliness, semantic
disjointness, character-edit disjointness or image cleanliness; ASCII
normalisation loses mathematical punctuation and non-ASCII text; and
fields shorter than eight tokens fall outside the near-match and
containment rule. The audit itself is receipted: it re-verifies Weights
\& Biases run \texttt{bsv8vx04} against
\longtok{training\_\allowbreak{}identity\_\allowbreak{}verified.json}, matches the 23,733 original
source-field document hashes to the historical E11 receipt, adds 2,507
decoded nested API-Bank strings to reach 26,240 source documents, and
records 43,977,748 bytes downloaded against a 500 MiB cap, with the
checker not having enumerated the roughly 2.58 billion possible document
pairs.

Two features of the campaign's execution are methodological rather than
incidental. The first is that a lane stops when its receipts are
incomplete, and the record says why. A single inventory pass on
2026-09-21 checked every blocked lane live and found concrete, named
failures: E1 lacked W\&B authentication, a sealed reservation and a live
actor endpoint; E2 awaited an owner IAM binding that only the project
owner can create; E5 lacked both a bound authorization receipt and any
real native runtime adapter in the repository; E13's request remained a
draft with launch authorisation false; E6 and E9 were refused by cloud
quota in both regions. Those blockers were cleared on 27 September by a
single lead authorisation and a move from AWS to Google Cloud and Modal,
and each lane recorded the change as a deviation (§8.4). Recording a
lane as blocked-with-reason, rather than reporting a shortened run, is
the same principle as the terminal-state taxonomy applied to execution.
The second is that negative outcomes are reported with their full
denominators and their mechanisms. Generation failures and artifact
losses stay in a lane's denominator rather than being excluded, as in
the E1 SWE-bench Pro lane, and a zero score is audited trajectory by
trajectory before it is read, as in the base-model E4 rerun whose zero
was traced to the serving bridge rather than to the model (results in
Chapter 8 and Section 8.5; sources:
outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md;
outputs/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}small\_\allowbreak{}scale\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-26/E4/BRIDGE\_\allowbreak{}STOP\_\allowbreak{}TOKEN\_\allowbreak{}FINDING.md).
Reporting outcomes with the mechanism identified is the behaviour the
whole apparatus is designed to produce.

The campaign also carries its integrity accounting in the same register.
Eleven of eleven deterministic checks passed on 2026-09-19, covering
exact divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact hash equality and
lost-source absence probes; the recorded spend was \$5.6301 against the
\$50 additional cap counted at that date, with later budget-mode records
moving to an unlimited mode whose per-run resource and timeout bounds
remain in force. A judgment layer was added for classification,
faithfulness and value-ordering questions and then partly invalidated by
an external service outage: both blocker-classification batteries
returned degenerate distributions after the incident, their receipts are
retained as incident evidence, and the per-lane blocker column therefore
rests on document evidence and not on model judgment. A design rule was
banked from the healthy window and is followed in this thesis:
arithmetic and string lookups stay in code, and model judgment is
reserved for classification, faithfulness and value ordering, because a
parity probe was answered incorrectly even while the service was
healthy.

What the campaign does not claim follows directly from the design. It
does not claim improvement over any baseline anywhere, because no lane
carries a test establishing it. The one trained-versus-base comparison
is the paired small-scale rerun of Table 8.B, with 5 to 151 identical
items per lane, and its outcome is reported in Chapter 8; the campaign's
role in this thesis is to fix the evaluation protocol, the receipt
discipline and the disposition taxonomy that any future improvement
claim would have to satisfy. It does not claim that the fourteen suites
constitute a portfolio result. It does not pool original-contract with
replacement-scope figures, and it does not average across suites. Its
one trained actor means it says nothing about model comparisons. And
where the record is incomplete --- a lost execution source, an
unanswered external access request, a run whose budget exceeded the
authorised envelope --- the incompleteness is written into the ledger
rather than resolved by inference.



\chapter{Implementation Details}\label{implementation-details}

This chapter describes what was built. Its organising claim is that the
engineering contribution of the project is a \textbf{dispatch and
evidence layer} rather than a training algorithm: the algorithmic
content of GRPO is inherited from the literature, but the work of making
six reinforcement-learning frameworks and six compute backends
addressable through one Tinker-style interface --- and of making every
number that comes out of them traceable to a sealed, hash-pinned
artefact --- is where the implementation effort sits. The chapter
proceeds from the abstraction (the adapter architecture), through the
concrete training loop, to the two engineering episodes that most shaped
the codebase (the parallel logging defect and the Colab white-box path),
and finally to the evaluation harness and the recovery
re-implementations undertaken after an execution-source loss in the
final phase of the project (Figure~\ref{fig:receipts}).


\fig{fig_receipt_pipeline}
{The evidence chain from sealed request through execution and native grading to a deterministic ledger check}
{The evidence chain from sealed request through execution and native grading to a deterministic ledger check. A break anywhere in the chain means no number is reported for that lane. Upper row: the chain; the 11/11 PASS badge is the 2026-09-19 deterministic ledger check (\longtok{LEDGER\_\allowbreak{}CODE\_\allowbreak{}CHECK\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json}). Lower row: the E1 wave-10 recovery, where the lost execution source stops the chain.}
{fig:receipts}



\section{Two Rosters, One Dispatch
Surface}\label{two-rosters-one-dispatch-surface}

A reader approaching the project's comparison material will encounter
two different framework rosters, and conflating them is the single most
likely misreading of the implementation. The internal reports' shared
methods note states the distinction explicitly: the cross-launcher
LLM-RL scaffold referenced in the framework-gap discussion is a
seven-library roster --- Tinker, TRL, SkyRL, veRL, OpenRLHF, Atropos,
and HF reference launchers --- that targets LLM post-training launchers
specifically, while the cross-RL-library evidence reported elsewhere
uses a different roster. Critically for this chapter, the same note
records that ``only Tinker and TRL produced completed runs in this
release; veRL and OpenRLHF entries in the
\texttt{framework\_comparison.json} artefact are dry-run placeholders''.
The veRL and OpenRLHF entries in that artefact carry peak and
last-ten-average reward values, but they are the output of the harness
resolving a launch plan, not the output of a training run, and they must
never be pooled with real results or read as framework comparison
evidence.

Atropos occupies a different position again. Rather than being a cell in
the dispatch matrix, it is integrated through a dedicated package under
\texttt{platform\_tinker/atropos/}, a \texttt{tinker\_atropos} module
containing \texttt{config.py}, \texttt{api.py}, \texttt{trainer.py},
\texttt{types.py}, \texttt{dataset.py}, \texttt{stats\_utils.py}, and an
\texttt{environments/} directory with per-task environment servers. Its
integration model is the Atropos managed-server pattern: a
three-terminal workflow in which \texttt{run-api} starts the Atropos
API, \texttt{launch\_training.py\ -\/-config\ configs/default.yaml}
starts Tinker training against a \longtok{TINKER\_\allowbreak{}API\_\allowbreak{}KEY}, and an
environment server --- a built-in one or any external Atropos
environment pointed at the same config file --- serves rollouts. The
README's quickstart example runs a fifty-step training run on
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct against a GSM8K environment, and the environment
contract is that the \texttt{env} and \texttt{openai} sections are
consumed by the environment server while the \texttt{tinker} section is
consumed by the trainer and ignored by the environment. This separation
is what allows an arbitrary existing Atropos environment to be reused
against the Tinker backend without modification to the environment.

\section{The Adapter Architecture: What versus
Where}\label{the-adapter-architecture-what-versus-where}

The dispatch surface is built on one orthogonal decomposition, stated in
the backends module docstring: ``A \texttt{Backend} knows \textbf{where}
to run one experiment cell (local GPU, Modal, Colab, vast.ai, GCP, HF
Spaces); the framework dimension (trl/tinker/verl/openrlhf/skyrl) is the
\textbf{what}. \texttt{plan()} resolves a cell into a concrete
\texttt{LaunchPlan} without spending compute, so \texttt{-\/-dry-run}
and the matrix test work uniformly''. The \texttt{LaunchPlan} dataclass
carries \texttt{backend}, \texttt{framework}, \texttt{command},
\texttt{driver\_file}, \texttt{output}, \texttt{env}, and
\texttt{notes}, with a \texttt{format()} method for printing; the
default \texttt{\_execute} runs
\texttt{subprocess.run(plan.command,\allowbreak{}\ shell=\allowbreak{}True,\allowbreak{}\ check=\allowbreak{}True)}. Because
plan resolution is separated from execution, the entire matrix can be
validated without a GPU, an API key, or a dollar of spend --- a property
that the dry-run placeholders discussed above depend on, and which is
also the reason those placeholders must be labelled as such (Figure~\ref{fig:arch}).


\fig{fig_architecture}
{Six RL frameworks behind one Tinker-style API}
{Six RL frameworks behind one Tinker-style API. Five of the six are in the dispatch matrix (5 frameworks $\times$ 6 compute backends); only two executed (Tinker, TRL: last-10 mean training reward on Qwen/Qwen3-8B GSM8K, from \texttt{framework\_comparison.json}), two are dry-run launch plans and two were not executed. One configuration drives every framework, but the executed comparison bundled a different base checkpoint (Chapter~7), so outcome differences are reported with the stack rather than attributed to it.}
{fig:arch}



The matrix itself ships as data.
\longtok{platform\_local/unified/matrix.json} declares schema
\longtok{tinkerrl-framework-backend-matrix-v1} with thirty cells --- five
frameworks (trl, tinker, verl, openrlhf, skyrl) crossed with six
backends (local, modal, colab, vast, gcp, hfspaces), five cells per row
and per column --- and a \texttt{canonical\_experiment} block that pins
the single configuration every cell is meant to instantiate:
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B} on GSM8K with GRPO, thirty training steps,
\texttt{num\_generations} 8, per-device batch size 2, gradient
accumulation 8, learning rate \texttt{1e-06}, LoRA rank 16 and alpha 32
over all linear modules, \texttt{beta} 0.0, \texttt{epsilon} 0.2,
maximum completion length 1024, and seed 211. Each cell is a concrete
plan naming a framework, a backend, the launch command, the driver file
and an output location; for example, the \texttt{trl}/\texttt{local}
cell runs
\texttt{python\ -m\ platform\_local.unified\ -\/-backend\ local\ -\/-framework\ trl}
and dispatches in-process to
\longtok{platform\_local/trl\_integrations/trainer.py}.

The launcher implements the dispatch. \texttt{UnifiedLauncher} holds
three registries: \texttt{FRAMEWORKS} mapping \texttt{skyrl},
\texttt{tinker}, \texttt{verl}, \texttt{openrlhf}, and \texttt{trl} to
human-readable labels; \texttt{BACKENDS} mapping \texttt{local},
\texttt{modal}, \texttt{colab}, \texttt{vast}, \texttt{gcp}, and
\texttt{hfspaces} to labels; and \texttt{ALGORITHMS} covering
\texttt{grpo}, \texttt{ppo}, \texttt{reinforce}, \texttt{dapo}, and
\texttt{dpo}. Dispatch is outer-on-backend and inner-on-framework, which
is why the per-framework handlers can each be written against the
framework's own idioms. \texttt{\_run\_tinker} shells to
\texttt{python\ -\allowbreak{}m\ platform\_\allowbreak{}tinker.tinkerrl.grpo\_\allowbreak{}cli\ -\allowbreak{}\/\allowbreak{}-\allowbreak{}preset\ \textless{}spec.task\textgreater{}};
\texttt{\_run\_openrlhf} delegates to
\texttt{platform\_\allowbreak{}modal/\allowbreak{}openrlhf/\allowbreak{}trainer.py:\allowbreak{}run\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}openrlhf\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}training(\{\}\allowbreak{},\allowbreak{}\ output\_\allowbreak{}dir=\allowbreak{}"\allowbreak{}./\allowbreak{}checkpoints/\allowbreak{}openrlhf"\allowbreak{})},
which drives
\texttt{openrlhf.cli.train\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}ray\ -\allowbreak{}\/\allowbreak{}-\allowbreak{}advantage\_\allowbreak{}estimator\ group\_\allowbreak{}norm};
\texttt{\_run\_trl} generates a resumable GRPO training script through
\longtok{trl\_integrations.trainer.generate\_\allowbreak{}trl\_\allowbreak{}train\_\allowbreak{}script} into a
temporary directory and executes it; \texttt{\_run\_verl} imports
\texttt{verl.config} and \texttt{verl.trainer} directly; and
\texttt{\_run\_skyrl} requires a \texttt{SKYRL\_CHECKOUT} environment
variable and emits
\texttt{uv\ run\ -\/-extra\ gpu\ -\/-extra\ tinker\ -m\ skyrl.tinker.api}.
For the \texttt{local} and \texttt{colab} backends the launcher runs
\texttt{dispatch\_framework()} in-process, a path the code comments
identify as the one that breaks the Colab entry's self-recursion --- an
implementation detail worth recording because it indicates that the
backend and framework dimensions are not perfectly orthogonal in
practice, and the launcher carries framework-specific knowledge
precisely at the points where they intersect.

Fairness across frameworks is addressed by configuration auditing rather
than by assertion. The
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/framework\_\allowbreak{}config\_\allowbreak{}dumps/}
supplement ships one exact hyperparameter dump per framework for the
canonical Qwen3-8B + GSM8K matched-configuration run, and the
accompanying README recomputes the field partition from those dumps: of
44 tracked leaf fields, 25 are identical across all four frameworks
(LoRA rank, alpha, target modules and dropout; GRPO group size, rollouts
per group and max new tokens; sampling temperature, top-p, top-k and
do-sample; optimizer name, learning rate, weight decay, betas and
epsilon; reward type; and the task, seed and source-git-sha metadata),
while 11 are Tinker-managed --- serialised as
\texttt{null\ \ \#\ managed\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}by\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}tinker} in the Tinker dump --- covering
\texttt{kl.beta}, \texttt{kl.surrogate}, importance-sampling
granularity, reference-model offload and recompute, tokens per optimizer
step, reward broadcast precision, gradient checkpointing, micro-batch
size, gradient accumulation, and tensor-parallel size. The same README
records two disclaimers that matter more than the identical count:
\texttt{model} and \texttt{model\_sha256} are \emph{not} identical,
because the Tinker run uses \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base} while TRL, veRL
and OpenRLHF use the instruction-tuned \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, and
\texttt{model\_sha256} is a placeholder rather than a verified checksum.
Remaining fields are framework-specific and documented rather than
harmonised --- KL beta is 0.04 for TRL and veRL against 0.02 for
OpenRLHF, importance-sampling granularity is token-level for TRL and
veRL against sequence-level for OpenRLHF, and reference-model placement
differs across all four (\texttt{on\_device}, \texttt{managed},
\texttt{separate\_process}, and \texttt{sharded\_ray}). The appendix's
original ``byte-identical'' framing is retracted on the strength of this
partition; the accurate statement is that a majority of tracked fields
are matched, a known Base-versus-Instruct confound survives, and eleven
fields are not under user control at the Tinker API level at all.

\section{The Tinker GRPO Loop}\label{the-tinker-grpo-loop}

The reference implementation of the training loop lives in
\longtok{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py} and is exposed through
\texttt{grpo\_cli.py}. The loop follows the standard four-stage shape
recorded in the Phase-1 report under ``The Tinker GRPO Loop'': sample a
group of \texttt{G} completions per prompt, compute group-relative
advantages, form the loss, and take an optimiser step. Advantages are
normalised within the group to mean zero and unit standard deviation,
using the population σ (\texttt{normalize\_rewards}, ε = 10⁻⁸). The loss
is not the clipped, KL-regularised surrogate of the original GRPO paper.
It is REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline,

\[L(\theta) = -\frac{1}{N} \sum_i A_i \sum_t \log \pi_\theta(y_{i,t} \mid x, y_{i,<t})\]

averaged over the N completions in the batch, with no importance ratio,
no clipping, no KL term and no reference policy. The sampler is
refreshed only at checkpoint boundaries (every \texttt{save\_every}
steps), so rollouts between refreshes come from slightly stale weights
with no importance correction. The implementation uses the Tinker SDK's
\texttt{ServiceClient} with \longtok{create\_\allowbreak{}lora\_\allowbreak{}training\_\allowbreak{}client},
\longtok{forward\_\allowbreak{}backward\_\allowbreak{}custom} with
\longtok{loss\_\allowbreak{}type\_\allowbreak{}input="logprobs"} so that per-token
log-probabilities flow into a custom loss, \texttt{optim\_step} with
\texttt{AdamParams}, and \texttt{sample()} for rollouts; resumption uses
\longtok{create\_\allowbreak{}training\_\allowbreak{}client\_\allowbreak{}from\_\allowbreak{}state\_\allowbreak{}with\_\allowbreak{}optimizer}.

The single most consequential property of this loop for the project's
research questions is the degenerate case. When the group's reward
standard deviation \texttt{σ\_g} is zero, every advantage \texttt{A\_i}
is zero, and because the loss has no KL or auxiliary term, the group
contributes exactly zero gradient. This is the mechanism behind the
zero-variance-fraction diagnostic that runs through the project; it is
recorded in the Phase-1 report as the observation that motivated the
collapse analysis, and it is the reason \texttt{zero\_loss} and
\texttt{zero\_reward} are first-class outputs of the training CLI rather
than derived quantities. Zero reward is counted as an average reward
equal to zero; zero loss is counted as a step loss below \texttt{1e-6}.

The CLI exposes eight presets --- \texttt{tooluse\_synth},
\texttt{tooluse\_baseline}, \texttt{tooluse\_heldout},
\texttt{tooluse\_xlam}, \texttt{gsm8k}, \texttt{math100},
\texttt{pavlov\_xlam}, and \texttt{pavlov\_portfolio} --- each resolving
a dataset factory and a reward function through a
\texttt{DATASET\_FACTORIES} registry and a \texttt{REWARD\_MAP}
(\texttt{ExactMathReward} for GSM8K, \texttt{StrictToolCallReward} for
\texttt{pavlov\_xlam}, \texttt{PavlovNonXLAMReward} for
\texttt{pavlov\_portfolio}). Tracking and campaign gates are enforced
before a run starts, through \texttt{config.validate\_tracking()} and
\longtok{config.validate\_\allowbreak{}campaign\_\allowbreak{}gate()}, and the CLI refuses to
launch without a \longtok{TINKER\_\allowbreak{}API\_\allowbreak{}KEY}. Each seed's result is
printed as a structured record containing \texttt{run\_id},
\texttt{sampler}, \texttt{avg\_first5}, \texttt{avg\_last10},
\texttt{peak\_reward}, \texttt{zero\_loss}, \texttt{zero\_reward},
\texttt{heldout\_reward}, and \texttt{reward\_trace}, so that a run's
evidence is emitted at the point of execution rather than reconstructed
afterwards. The eight presets replaced an earlier collection of
standalone \texttt{grpo\_*.py} scripts, with \texttt{legacy\_main}
retained to translate the old flag surface.

\section{The Parallel Runner and a Real Logging
Defect}\label{the-parallel-runner-and-a-real-logging-defect}

Sweep execution is handled by \texttt{parallel\_sweep.py} and
\texttt{campaign.py}, with the runner's design shaped directly by a
defect found during development. The defect is documented in the
group-size sweep findings and is worth quoting in full, because it is
the clearest illustration in the project of why logging infrastructure
deserves the same scrutiny as training code:

\begin{quote}
The threaded runner trains all G in parallel correctly, BUT
\texttt{wandb} uses a thread-unsafe global \texttt{run} --- with 4
threads calling \texttt{wandb.init(reinit=True)}, the G8/G16
\textbf{summary} writes failed (``Run is finished'') after another
thread finished its run. Per-step \texttt{wandb.log} mostly survived;
the summary dict didn't. \textbf{Fix for next parallel batch:} isolate
each run in its own PROCESS (multiprocessing / subprocess per config),
not a thread --- or use \texttt{wandb-core} service mode with explicit
per-run objects. Training itself was unaffected (all step data is in the
run log).
\end{quote}

Two aspects of this record are instructive. First, the failure was
silent with respect to training: gradients were correct, steps
completed, and per-step metrics were written. Only the run summary ---
the aggregated dictionary from which a results table would be built ---
was lost, which is precisely the class of failure that corrupts
downstream reporting while leaving the primary artefact intact. Second,
the remedy adopted was structural rather than retry-based: isolating
each run in its own process removes the shared mutable global entirely,
whereas retrying the summary write would have left the race in place.
The finding also notes the alternative of \texttt{wandb-core} service
mode with explicit per-run objects, which would preserve in-process
execution at the cost of depending on service-mode semantics that the
project chose not to rely on.

The same findings file documents the group-size sweep that exposed the
defect, and its numbers are reported here with the qualifications the
source attaches to them. The sweep ran \texttt{G\ ∈\ \{2,\ 4,\ 8,\ 16\}}
on Qwen3.5-4B for six steps on GSM8K at seed 0 under the W\&B group
\texttt{p3-groupsize-sweep}, with total token counts of 9,663, 19,964,
34,084, and 68,055 respectively. At \texttt{G\ =\ 2} the run showed a
50\% batch-level collapse with a \texttt{+0.000} gain; at
\texttt{G\ =\ 4} a 17\% collapse with a \texttt{+0.125} gain. The source
flags the \texttt{G\ =\ 4} gain explicitly as supported by one example
at n = 8 on a single seed and therefore \emph{suggestive rather than
proven}, and records that the independent verification of the sweep
returned the verdict SUSPECT. These are not results this thesis claims;
they are the observations that motivated the group-size investigation
and that the later controlled work supersedes, and they are reported in
the register in which they were recorded.

\section{The Colab White-Box Path (Per-Layer Gradient
Profile)}\label{the-colab-white-box-path-per-layer-gradient-profile}

The per-layer gradient profile --- numbered P1 in the Phase-1 series,
and distinct from the P1 cross-scale study of Chapter 6 --- asks a
question the managed Tinker backend cannot answer. Per-layer gradient
norms require access to the model's internals during the backward pass,
and the managed service exposes no such interface. The implementation
therefore leaves the managed path entirely and runs on a Colab L4 GPU in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/openings/p1\_\allowbreak{}layer\_\allowbreak{}profile.py},
using Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with LoRA adapters on the q/k/v/o
projections, five GRPO-style advantage-weighted steps, and per-layer
LoRA gradient norms recorded at each step. The white-box path is thus a
deliberate architectural exception: the project accepts the loss of
managed-scale training in exchange for gradient visibility, and confines
the exception to a question that only gradient visibility can address.

The first-pass result appeared strong. Across 28 layers, the top-k layer
set identified at step 1 overlapped the final top-k set perfectly, with
a measured overlap of 1.0; the top 25\% of layers accounted for a 0.476
share of gradient mass; layer 0 carried a gradient norm of 63 against
6--17 elsewhere, and a secondary mid-late band appeared at layers
17--23. A scaled re-test disciplined this result. The re-run used
\longtok{p1\_\allowbreak{}layer\_\allowbreak{}profile\_\allowbreak{}scaled.py} on Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, with 24
real GSM8K problems, ten steps, \texttt{G\ =\ 4}, 36 layers, and seeds
\{0, 1\}. The step-1-to-final top-k overlap fell to 0.111 --- chance
level --- and the top-25\% concentration measured 0.39 with a standard
deviation of 0.013. The source's verdict is unambiguous: the original
predictability finding ``was an artifact of the toy
1.5B/hardcoded-arithmetic setup''. A later four-seed pass at the same
configuration (seeds \{0, 1, 2, 3\}) supersedes the two-seed figure and
gives a mean overlap of 0.083 ± 0.048, again approximately chance, with
concentration 0.39.

A companion freeze test asked whether the layer profile could be
exploited: if a small band of layers dominates the gradient, freezing
the rest should preserve the benefit at lower cost. It did not. The full
28-layer configuration trained 2.18M parameters and produced a mean
held-out gain of \texttt{+0.0625} (seed 0 \texttt{+0.125}, seed 1 0.0).
Freezing to a hot band comprising layer 0 plus layers 12--27 --- 17
layers, 1.32M parameters, or 0.61 times the full count --- produced
\texttt{+0.000}. The source concludes that ``aggressive freezing loses
the benefit\ldots{} The freeze lever does not work at this scale'', and
records that the comparison is underpowered at n = 8 held-out examples
over 2 seeds. Both P1 outcomes are negatives: the apparent structure did
not survive scaling, and the intervention it suggested did not survive
testing. They are reported as findings rather than suppressed, because a
layer-importance claim that collapses under a scaled re-test is exactly
the kind of result the project's evidence discipline exists to surface.

\section{Software Modules}\label{software-modules}

The module inventory recorded in the Phase-1 report remains the accurate
map of the codebase. The unified launcher,
\longtok{platform\_local/unified/launcher.py}, maps one configuration
onto a chosen backend; \longtok{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo.py} is the
reference GRPO loop against the managed backend;
\longtok{platform\_local/trl\_integrations/} and the \texttt{verl/}
integration hold the per-framework adapters, with
\texttt{platform\_modal/openrlhf/} and \texttt{platform\_hybrid/skyrl/}
completing the roster, and \texttt{platform\_tinker/atropos/} carrying
the Atropos integration described above. Analysis code is separated from
training code by intent: \longtok{p2\_\allowbreak{}collapse\_\allowbreak{}analysis.py} recomputes
zero-variance fraction and collapse statistics from stored reward
tensors, so the diagnostic is reproducible from evidence rather than
from a live run; \longtok{p1\_\allowbreak{}layer\_\allowbreak{}profile.py} implements the white-box
gradient probe; \texttt{parallel\_sweep.py} and \texttt{campaign.py}
drive sweeps; and \texttt{p8\_detector.py} is an exploratory
method-trace classifier evaluated by row-wise cross-validated AUROC.

The p8 detector deserves an explicit caveat in an implementation
chapter. It is an exploratory artefact evaluated by row-wise
cross-validation, and its AUROC is a measure of separability on the
traces it was given, not a benchmark result against an external suite;
it should not be read alongside the E-lane scores reported later in this
thesis. Beyond these modules, the \texttt{platform\_local/unified/}
package carries \texttt{canonical.py} for the canonical configuration
and \texttt{peft\_utils.py} for adapter handling, and the backends
package implements one module per backend (\texttt{local.py},
\texttt{modal.py}, \texttt{colab.py}, \texttt{vast.py}, \texttt{gcp.py},
\texttt{hfspaces.py}) behind the common \texttt{base.py} interface.

\section{The E1--E14 Evaluation
Harness}\label{the-e1e14-evaluation-harness}

Evaluation is governed by a completion gate,
\longtok{zvf-program/flagship/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}completion\_\allowbreak{}gate.py}, schema
\texttt{e1-e14-completion-gate-v1}. The gate maintains five evidence
classes --- \texttt{COMPLETE\_EXACT}, \texttt{PARTIAL\_EXACT},
\texttt{PARTIAL\_RECOVERY}, \texttt{BLOCKED\_EXTERNAL}, and
\longtok{LOCAL\_\allowbreak{}SETUP\_\allowbreak{}READY\_\allowbreak{}PROVIDER\_\allowbreak{}INPUT\_\allowbreak{}REQUIRED} --- and maps
lane source statuses onto them, with unknown states failing closed
rather than defaulting to a classification. Its \texttt{LANE\_SPECS}
table binds each lane to a replacement scope, a task count, and the
runner or adapter that produces its evidence: E2 FrontierSWE at 17 tasks
(\longtok{pavlov\_\allowbreak{}frontier\_\allowbreak{}swe\_\allowbreak{}eval\_\allowbreak{}adapter.py},
\longtok{frontier\_\allowbreak{}swe\_\allowbreak{}eval.py}), E3 SDAB at 80, E4 BankerToolBench at
100 (\longtok{run\_\allowbreak{}harbor\_\allowbreak{}via\_\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}bridge.py}), E5 APEX-Agents at
480, E7 BinaryAudit at 46 (\longtok{modal\_\allowbreak{}non\_\allowbreak{}e11\_\allowbreak{}readiness.py}), E9
MLE-bench at 75 (\longtok{e9\_\allowbreak{}mle\_\allowbreak{}bench\_\allowbreak{}streaming.py}), E12 AppBench at
6, and lanes E6, E8, E10, E13 and E14 with their respective runners. The
gate's own header states its scope precisely: it neither invokes an
adapter nor accepts a package as a benchmark result (Figure~\ref{fig:harness}).


\fig{fig_harness}
{Evaluation harness internals}
{Evaluation harness internals. The harness is fail-closed: when a precondition cannot be satisfied the lane records \textsc{blocked} rather than substituting an adjacent benchmark. Outcomes and the lane tally are taken from the 2026-09-19 ledger of record (\texttt{Pending\_Experiments.md}), updated with the six replacement scopes scored on 2026-09-27. Outcome A separates original-contract scores from replacement-scope results and gives coverage and score separately; Outcome B lists original-scope suites that are closed external, then the one lane whose scope is not settled. E14 is reported as $2271/4428 = 51.29\%$ with the two unjudged rows counted as failures (the official scorer reports 51.31\% over 4426 judged rows).}
{fig:harness}



The results the gate classifies are recorded in
\longtok{outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md}, verified by
eleven of eleven deterministic checks on 2026-09-19 and bound to
surviving receipts. The single Tinker-trained actor is base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit \texttt{995ad96e…} with adapter
\longtok{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1…}
at commit \texttt{64444133…}, in bfloat16. The document's opening
constraint governs how everything below may be read: original-contract
and replacement-scope numbers are never pooled, and per the project
evidence rule no cross-suite average is formed at any point.

The per-lane results the gate classified are reported once, in Chapter 8
(Tables 8.A--8.C), with each lane's scope and terminal state in Table
C.3; they are not repeated here.

One further result from this phase belongs in the implementation record
because of what it demonstrates about the harness. Under separate
authorization, the E4 BankerToolBench suite was re-run from scratch on
the base model through the Modal bridge's \texttt{/v1/responses} path at
\texttt{-\/-n-concurrent\ 2} under a cap of \$55.91445263. All 100 of
100 trials completed, with one \texttt{NonZeroAgentExitCodeError}, and
the mean reward was 0.0 --- every trial in the zero bucket. The audited
mechanism is that agents terminated after 2--14 steps (median about 6)
without producing output in \texttt{deliverables/}, causing the verifier
to short-circuit to zero on a ``No deliverables found'' condition, and
that 27 or more trajectories ended in role-token degeneration of the
form \texttt{user\ user\ assistant\ …} repeated. This bridge path set no
stop sequences, which produces exactly this degeneration, so the zero is
attributed to the bridge rather than to the model (Section 8.5); the run
cost \$15.07 of the cap over 3h29m, against 16.35M prompt and 2.18M
completion tokens. Reporting a mean reward of 0.0 with its mechanism
attached, rather than reporting a low score and moving on, is the
standard this chapter's evidence rules are meant to enforce.

\section{The Bridge and Sampler Path}\label{the-bridge-and-sampler-path}

Runners reach a Tinker-trained model through
\longtok{zvf-program/flagship/modal\_\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}openai\_\allowbreak{}bridge.py}, a
Modal-deployed OpenAI-compatible bridge whose own header states that
``the deployed bridge is infrastructure, not a benchmark result. Its
default paid-inference budget is zero''. The bridge declares app name
\texttt{pavlov-tinker-openai-bridge}, model alias
\texttt{pavlov-qwen36-tinker} over model id
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B}, and pins its sampler at
\longtok{tinker://cf0ad8c1-1f1b-5ff3-8bd7-2a0bf232657b:train:0/sampler\_weights/seed809\_final},
with the Hugging Face side pinned by commit
\longtok{64444133c55d88c3f1bf0df8a2f5d7ac646125c8}. Runtime limits are
16,384 maximum completion tokens against a 65,536-token model context,
with an 8,192-token context prefix reserved; \longtok{DEFAULT\_\allowbreak{}MAX\_\allowbreak{}USD}
is \texttt{Decimal("0.00")}, so every paid path must be opted into
explicitly at launch. The pinned dependency set is \texttt{fastapi}
0.116.1, \texttt{huggingface-hub} 1.27.0, \texttt{tinker} 0.30.0,
\texttt{transformers} 5.5.4, and \texttt{wandb} 0.21.0.

The architectural point of the bridge is that it converts a Tinker
sampler into an OpenAI-compatible endpoint. A harness written against
the OpenAI chat-completions API --- which most agentic benchmark
harnesses are --- can therefore be pointed at a Tinker-trained actor
without modification, and the E4 rerun described above used exactly this
path in its \texttt{/v1/responses} form. The same pattern appears in the
actor-side configuration of the E1 recovery wave, where the actor is
addressed as an OpenAI-compatible chat-completions service at
\texttt{http://127.0.0.1:18015/v1} (container port 8000) under model
name \texttt{pavlov-public-portfolio-bf16}, with \texttt{max\_tokens}
8192, temperature 0, seed 809, top-p 0.95, and
\texttt{chat\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}template\_\allowbreak{}\allowbreak{}kwargs\ \{"\allowbreak{}enable\_\allowbreak{}thinking"\allowbreak{}:\allowbreak{}\ false\}}. The
bridge and the local actor endpoint are two deployments of the same
interface contract, and keeping that contract stable across deployment
modes is what allows one harness to serve both.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Recovery Re-implementations under
\texttt{zvf-program/}}{Recovery Re-implementations under zvf-program/}}\label{recovery-re-implementations-under-zvf-program}

In the final phase of the project an execution-source loss --- deletion
of the \texttt{.codex-run} directory --- removed the finish-era recovery
and build code, confined in scope to the E1 wave10 controller, the E9
restored dependencies, and the E6 validator, while the replacement-lane
native runners survived under \texttt{zvf-program/flagship/}. The
project's lost-source policy is that sealed requests, validation
receipts and test logs pin any faithful re-implementation. Four such
re-implementations are worth describing, because together they show what
the evidence discipline looks like when it is expressed as code rather
than as convention. None of them produced a benchmark number; their
value is that their safety properties are testable offline.

\textbf{The four recovery re-implementations.}

return \longtok{PLAN\_\allowbreak{}READY\_\allowbreak{}NOT\_\allowbreak{}EXECUTED}, \texttt{gate} and
\texttt{launch} refuse & Needs a bound authorisation receipt and a
native runtime adapter \\
E13 supervisor (\longtok{zvf-program/e13\_balrog/hosted\_supervisor.py})
& Hosted supervisor for the BALROG lane; design artefact, not deployed &
Absolute deadline D = t0 + 3600 s that is never rebased; short
HMAC-authenticated leases that reject replays; renewal and receipt
signatures in separate domains; live execution unimplemented and guarded
by \longtok{NeverExecutedError} & 17/17 & Not deployed \\
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

\emph{Sources: zvf-program/e1\_wave10/README.md;
zvf-program/e2\_core/driver.py;
zvf-program/e5\_successor27/controller.py;
zvf-program/e13\_balrog/hosted\_supervisor.py;
outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md.}

The companion E2 \texttt{launch\_gate.py} is deliberately trivial: it
checks only that the amendment acceptance record and the surviving
CORE-Bench harness entry points exist, which keeps the gate's claim
small enough to be true.

Read together, these four artefacts are the implementation answer to a
question the results chapters raise: how a project maintains evidential
integrity when its execution environment is partly lost and partly
unreachable. The answer taken here is to re-derive the controllers from
the sealed requests and the surviving test records, to keep every
re-implementation offline-testable, and to make the no-retry, no-replay,
hash-pinned, fail-closed properties explicit in code so that they can be
demonstrated rather than asserted. The cost of that discipline is
visible: four controllers, three of them complete and one a deliberate
stub, produced no new benchmark numbers in this phase. That is the
intended trade.

None of the four controllers ran the lanes in the end. On 27 September
2026 a single lead authorisation superseded the sealed-reservation and
owner-binding gates they enforce, and each lane was run with a thin
runner that calls the benchmark's native loop and grader directly. The
runners live under
\longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/\textless{}lane\textgreater{}/code/},
and each lane's \texttt{result.json} lists the bypass as a deviation
(§8.4). The fail-closed properties above therefore describe how the
lanes were held, not how they were measured.



\part{Results}


\chapter{Results I: Core GRPO Studies
(P1--P4)}\label{results-i-core-grpo-studies-p1p4}

This chapter reports the four core GRPO studies that make up the first
half of the P1--P8 internal reports: the cross-library and cross-scale
behaviour of GRPO training reward (P1), the Zero-Variance Fraction as a
diagnostic of signal starvation (P2), the effect of group size on
trainability (P3), and the length-bias and held-out-generalisation
behaviour of GRPO against its Dr.~GRPO variant (P4). Each study is
reported here in the form the evidence supports, which in every case is
more conservative than the hypothesis that motivated it. Three of the
four pillars return a negative or an explicitly bounded result, and the
fourth returns a diagnostic whose measured association with the outcome
it is meant to predict is weaker than the literature would suggest (Figure~\ref{fig:scaling}).


\fig{fig_scaling_null}
{Cross-scale behaviour of reward across the studied model range: no reliable monotone scaling trend is recovered}
{Cross-scale behaviour of reward across the studied model range: no reliable monotone scaling trend is recovered. Whiskers are $\pm1$~SD of the per-step reward within the run, clipped to $[0,1]$; they describe step-to-step noise, not a confidence interval. Open markers have fewer than ten logged steps. The dashed line and grey band are the constant model selected by AICc (pooled mean 0.641) and the $\pm\sigma_{\mathrm{step}}$ noise floor (0.096). OLS slope $+0.081\pm0.129$ per decade ($R^2=0.038$, permutation $p=0.54$); Spearman $\rho(\log_{10}N,\bar R)=+0.149$ ($p=0.64$). Sources: experiments/results/scaling\_\allowbreak{}law\_\allowbreak{}extended\_\allowbreak{}frontier.tsv and scaling\_\allowbreak{}law\_\allowbreak{}power\_\allowbreak{}law.tsv.}

\section{Scope, Sources and Evidence
Boundary}\label{scope-sources-and-evidence-boundary}

The four pillars share one methodological commitment, which is the
organising principle of the whole project: every comparative claim is
made with the entire training stack held fixed except the one factor
under test. Where that was not possible, the result is reported as an
association rather than a contrast, and the confound is named. The
reader should treat this chapter as the primary result surface for
P1--P4; the corresponding LaTeX sources are the canonical artefacts, and
every figure quoted below is taken from one of them.

Each study draws on a different evidence base, and the differences
matter. P1 pools 70+ runs across seven reinforcement-learning libraries
and five model families spanning 0.6B to 1T parameters, but the
frontier-scale anchors within that pool are single-seed runs executed
through a managed training and inference API, so the cross-scale
analysis is descriptive and its uncertainty is wide. P2's measured
magnitudes come from a controlled five-seed panel plus a matched-budget
rollout budget check; a portion of its comparative table is a declared
simulation projection rather than a set of independent library
deployments, and that distinction is preserved below. P3's measured
sweep covers G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\}; every G = 32 and above number in the
pillar is a joint-fit reconstruction, not a trained arm. P4's controlled
comparison is sixteen runs across two algorithms and two tasks; its
roster-level audit is drawn from a separate, larger pool that mixes
models, tasks and libraries.

The corpus-level motivation for the fixed-stack commitment (Chapter 4
§4.1) is a variance decomposition computed as four separate marginal
one-way ANOVAs, each run against the same 42 experiments with valid
performance observations: library/framework η² = 0.546, training
algorithm η² = 0.558, model family η² = 0.471, model scale η² = 0.322.
These four values sum to 1.897. A set of shares that sums above one is
not a partition of a common variance budget, and on a corpus where the
factors are collinear a marginal η² absorbs the variance of each
factor's correlates, so the values show that framework and algorithm
vary a great deal across this corpus and do not estimate either factor's
independent contribution. The historical twenty-test multiple-comparison
family that accompanies the decomposition silently includes single-seed
rows whose Welch statistics are mathematically undefined, and reduces to
a single surviving comparison under the restricted family; it is
retained as a reproducibility audit trail and not as a corrected
survival claim.

Throughout this chapter, ``statistically indistinguishable'' means that
a formal equivalence or difference test failed to separate the arms at
the stated level, not that the arms were demonstrated to be equivalent.
Where an equivalence test was run and returned a null, that is stated
explicitly.

\section{P1 --- Cross-Library and Cross-Scale GRPO
Behaviour}\label{p1-cross-library-and-cross-scale-grpo-behaviour}

The question P1 sets out to answer is whether the benefit of GRPO
post-training scales with model size. The framing is deliberate: the
hypothesis of interest is a Chinchilla-style power law in parameter
count N, and the P1 report's declared claim boundary is that it reports
a descriptive limits and identifiability audit rather than a positive
scaling law. The short answer is that no reliable scaling law is
recovered from the reward curves, and that the evidence base is too
small and too heterogeneous to falsify a plausible scaling hypothesis
even if one were present.

\subsection{The anchor pool and the fitted saturation
forms}\label{the-anchor-pool-and-the-fitted-saturation-forms}

The fitted anchor pool spans roughly 2.4 orders of magnitude in
parameter count. This is a subset of the full 0.6B--1T roster: for the
cross-scale fits, only anchors with a usable per-step reward trace and a
known parameter count were retained. Two properties of that pool limit
what the fits can support, and both are stated here rather than left to
the reader. First, the anchors are not measured on a common footing:
five of the twelve are interrupted runs whose recorded values are means
over three to five logged steps, against twenty to thirty for the
remaining seven, so the dependent variable is a short-horizon snapshot
for part of the pool. Second, scale is collinear with training recipe
across these anchors rather than independent of it: the learning rate,
LoRA rank and related settings change together and only at the scale
boundary, so a fit through the pool cannot separate an absent scale
effect from recipe changes offsetting scale changes. The null reported
below is therefore a statement about this pool as configured, and not an
estimate of how reward scales with parameter count in general. The
frontier-scale anchors --- Qwen3-235B-MoE, DeepSeek-V3.1 at the 685B
class, Kimi-K2 at 1T, and Nemotron-120B --- were obtained through the
Tinker managed training and inference API in a single-seed,
descriptive-only regime, since full base weights are not released for
those anchors and the project does not own frontier hardware.

Each trace was fitted with a saturating-exponential form
\(R(t) = R_{\max}(1 - e^{-\lambda t})\), with the saturation rate λ
constrained by the optimiser to the interval {[}0, 10{]}. The fitted
results are the first place the negative result becomes visible. Across
the five characterised anchors, the ordinary-least-squares trend slope
on the raw reward trace, β\_OLS, satisfies \textbar β\_OLS\textbar{} ≤
0.0037 in every case --- that is, the traces are flat to within a few
thousandths of a reward unit per step. Qwen3.5-4B fits a mean reward of
0.817 with a peak of 1.000 and λ pinned at the bound of 10.0; Qwen3-8B
fits 0.285 with a peak of 0.625; Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct fits 0.869 with a
peak of 1.000; DeepSeek-V3.1 fits 0.844 with a peak of 1.000. Four of
these five traces have λ pinned at the fit boundary --- that is, the
optimiser wanted a faster saturation than the constraint permitted ---
and the P1 report's verdict is that for those traces no single
saturation exponent is identifiable at all.

The exception is Nemotron-120B, which fits \(R_{\max} = 0.182\) with
\(\lambda = 0.99\), well inside the bound, and which the pipeline
classifies as a collapse rather than a saturation. The autopsy table
records a peak at step 3, a fitted R̂\_max of 0.875, a late-phase mean
R̄\_late of 0.208, a drop of −0.667 from peak to late phase, a
zero-reward step fraction of 0.55, and a post-peak OLS slope of −0.0036
per step. Across the rest of the pool, the zero-reward step fraction is
at most 0.067. The Nemotron run is therefore better described as a
distinct collapse phase than as a point on a smooth trend, and this is
the reason the pillar's headline object is a local, stack-conditioned
taxonomy of endpoint ceilings rather than a functional form in N.

\subsection{The cross-scale test and its
nulls}\label{the-cross-scale-test-and-its-nulls}

Three cross-scale statistics were computed against log₁₀ N: the slope of
the mean reward, the slope of the peak reward, and the slope of the
reward variance. None is distinguishable from zero. The mean-reward
slope is −0.024 per decade with a bootstrap mean of −0.117 and a 95\%
interval of {[}−0.796, +0.313{]}, Spearman ρ = −0.07; the peak-reward
slope is +0.037 with a bootstrap mean of −0.010 and an interval of
{[}−0.623, +0.197{]}, ρ = +0.22; the variance slope is −0.002 with an
interval of {[}−0.063, +0.027{]}, ρ = −0.14. A residual-resampling
bootstrap with 1,000 draws applied to the Nemotron anchor returns R̄\_max
= 0.310, λ̄ = 3.26, P(λ ≥ 9.5) = 0.277 and a t₈₀ of 17.87 steps with an
interval of {[}0.16, 161.4{]} --- an interval wide enough to contain
almost any behavioural claim. A 70/30 anchor holdout returned slope
estimates of +0.0000, +0.0000, −0.0000, −0.0000 and +0.0016 across the
five holdout configurations. Fitting a power law over the full
twelve-anchor set from 4B to 1T returns p ≥ 0.46 for every exponent
tested.

Two model-selection checks were run to see whether the saturation story
was at least the best available description of the traces. Fitting
constant, linear and saturating forms to each trace, AIC and BIC select
the constant model on every single trace, with a ΔAIC of at most 2 over
the runner-up. That is, a flat line describes these reward curves at
least as well as any saturating curve does. A changepoint test with a
permutation null estimated change locations τ̂ of 27, 28, 5, 15 and 18
steps across five traces, with permutation p-values of 0.493, 0.789,
0.742, 0.909 and 0.137; none is significant. An
effective-plateau-horizon analysis at tolerance ε = 0.20 found that only
two of the five traces --- Qwen3.5-4B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ---
reach a horizon at all, both at 27 steps, because the others never
satisfy the tolerance.

A compute-adjusted proxy, which folds an estimated token budget into the
scale axis, returns a slope of +482 ± 490 per decade. The point estimate
is positive and the interval is an order of magnitude wider than the
estimate, so the compute axis does not rescue the scaling story either.

\subsection{Where the geometric template
fails}\label{where-the-geometric-template-fails}

The three-phase learning template used throughout the project --- a slow
start, a period of rapid improvement, and a plateau, with collapse as a
degenerate tail --- was tested directly against the anchor pool as an
internal consistency check. Only 2 of the 12 anchors match the
template's geometric prediction. The template itself survives as a
descriptive vocabulary: it names the failure modes the pipeline detects,
and the collapse criteria (peak ≥ 0.4, late mean below 40\% of peak,
zero-reward fraction ≥ 0.30) are used in the pillar's flagging
throughout. What does not survive is the claim that these traces realise
the template as a progression.

One further contrast deserves note because it is our own measurement
rather than a synthesis. Under a same-stack comparison of PPO and GRPO
on a saturated Qwen2.5-0.5B two-digit addition task, evaluation accuracy
is 0.990 for GRPO and 0.992 for PPO, and the paired algorithm difference
is −0.002 with p = 0.374 (paired t on n = 5 seeds, each seed's held-out
accuracy on 200 items; source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/frontier\_\allowbreak{}synthesis\_\allowbreak{}scaling.tex;
recomputed from
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/samestack\_\allowbreak{}ppo\_\allowbreak{}grpo.json). The 200
evaluation items are fresh random draws from the training generator, not
a disjoint split. This is the project's closest de-confound: the runner,
clipped surrogate and step budget are shared, but the arms also differ
in prompts per step (16 prompts × 8 completions for GRPO against 128
distinct prompts for PPO; Table 4.2). The two estimators are
statistically indistinguishable, and because both sit at the ceiling the
null cannot separate no effect from no headroom. They are not shown to
be equivalent. The five per-seed differences are −0.005, +0.005, −0.005,
0.000 and −0.005, each one item of 200, at a 0.98--1.00 accuracy
ceiling. A paired TOST with a ±0.005 margin gives p = 0.104, so
equivalence at that margin is not established, although it is at ±0.010
(p = 0.008). The exact sign-flip permutation p on the same five pairs is
0.625. It is reported here rather than in P2 or P3 because it is the
algorithmic counterpart of the P1 null --- the cross-scale story is not
the only axis on which GRPO's advantage fails to separate from its
alternatives. The project's historical effect-size table also carries a
Qwen3-8B PPO-versus-GRPO row. It is marked a provenance conflict and not
estimable, and its Welch test, recorded in the historical twenty-test
family at p = 0.7605, is carried as a provenance record rather than as
an estimate, so no statistic is quoted for it here.

\subsection{A withdrawn architectural
contrast}\label{a-withdrawn-architectural-contrast}

An earlier version of this analysis reported a MoE-versus-dense gap
across six pairs of anchors, with a mean gap of +0.338 and a permutation
p = 0.023. That claim has been withdrawn. The defect is architectural:
the Nemotron-120B anchor is a LatentMoE, not a dense model, so the
original six-versus-six allocation misclassified it. With the
classification corrected, the same test returns a one-sided permutation
p ≈ 0.178. A leave-Nemotron-out recomputation returns a gap of +0.278
with p ≈ 0.049, and this too is withdrawn, because removing the
misclassified anchor from one side of the comparison does not repair the
design. The withdrawal is recorded here because the project's convention
is that retired claims remain visible with the reason for retirement
attached.

A related cross-anchor statistic is retained, with its own caveat.
Across the twelve anchors, seven have \textbar Δ\_1T\textbar{} ≤ 0.2,
where Δ\_1T measures how far the anchor's endpoint deviates from the
three-phase template's terminal prediction; the largest deviation is
Qwen3.5-27B at +0.562 and the largest negative is Nemotron at −0.50. A
Cohen's κ analysis of the categorical phase assignments is also reported
in the source: across three labelling methods, the heuristic and AIC
labels agree at κ = +1.000 on the five-anchor set (both label every
trace ``plateau'') and κ = +0.625 on the twelve-anchor set, while the
changepoint labels agree with neither (κ between −0.029 and +0.077). The
supportable summary is that categorical capability structure carries
more signal than log₁₀ N, which is what the P1 report's abstract says
and what the numbers support.

\section{P2 --- The Zero-Variance
Fraction}\label{p2-the-zero-variance-fraction}

The second pillar asks a mechanistic question: when GRPO stops learning,
is the cause visible in the reward statistics of the groups it samples?
The diagnostic proposed is the Zero-Variance Fraction (Figure~\ref{fig:zvflib}).


\fig{fig_zvf_by_library}
{Mean Zero-Variance Fraction by method in a simulation projection (a) and by measured experiment family (b); neither panel compares backend libraries}
{Mean Zero-Variance Fraction by method in a simulation projection (a) and by measured experiment family (b); neither panel compares backend libraries. (a) Nine variance-mitigation methods in a simulation projection ($G=8$, math-verifiable task, 5 seeds each; hatched bars mark projected, not model-training, values): bar $=$ mean of per-seed mean ZVF, whisker $=\pm1$~SD across seeds ($\le 0.008$). The projection ranks no methods; GRPO (cross-hatched) is the reference at 0.481, and in 3 of its 5 seeds the per-step collapse flag fires.
