(b) Measured runs: gsm8k $=$ Qwen3-8B, three seeds of 200 GSM8K prompts at $G=8$ (mean $\pm1$~SD); drgrpo $=$ Qwen2.5-0.5B arithmetic, GRPO and Dr.\ GRPO, 5 seeds each (10 runs, mean $\pm1$~SD); gsize $=$ Qwen2.5-0.5B group-size sweep, mean over $G\in\{2,4,8,16\}$ with a dashed whisker spanning the per-$G$ values (a range over $G$, not seed dispersion); tool-32B (Qwen3-32B) and tool-8B (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) are single runs (open diamonds, no interval) in which every group has zero reward variance. Source: platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/zvf\_\allowbreak{}by\_\allowbreak{}library.tsv and the per-seed files it cites.}
{fig:zvflib}



\subsection{Definition and
measurement}\label{definition-and-measurement}

For a prompt x sampled from the training distribution, GRPO emits G
completions and scores each with a verifier. Centring those rewards
within the group produces the advantage signal. If all G completions
receive the same reward --- all correct, or all incorrect --- every
centred reward is zero and the group contributes no contrastive signal
to that step's update. The Zero-Variance Fraction is the fraction of
sampled groups in a step for which this occurs, and the gradient
utilisation is defined as GU = 1 − ZVF.

What a zero-variance group does to the update depends on the loss form.
The source states the general case: in an objective with a KL penalty or
auxiliary loss, a zero-variance group does not make the total gradient
zero, and ZVF measures only the fraction of groups contributing zero
centred reward contrast. None of the runners used in this project has a
KL term or a reference policy (Table 4.2), so for every measured study a
zero-variance group contributes exactly zero gradient through the policy
term; only optimiser momentum from earlier steps and the batch
normalisers carry over. ZVF is therefore a direct measure of wasted
rollouts on these runners, and a statement about the group-relative term
only on objectives that carry other terms.

The statistic is validated by recomputation. Three stored GSM8K group
tensors (Qwen3-8B, G = 8, sampling only, 200 problems per seed) were
re-read from disk and their ZVF recomputed from the raw per-group
rewards; the recomputed values are 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155, pooled
0.1583, matching the reported values exactly. This is the mechanical
check that the quantity in the tables is the quantity the code computes.
The statistic is also insensitive to the zero-variance threshold: the
reported ε-sensitivity is at most 0.4\%.

\subsection{Measured magnitudes across
libraries}\label{measured-magnitudes-across-libraries}

The headline measurement is a nine-row panel run at K = 8 with five
seeds and a 100-step rollout budget, spanning vanilla GRPO and eight
named variance-mitigation methods. Vanilla GRPO records a mean ZVF of
0.481 against a mean last-10 accuracy of 0.379, with zero of five seeds
collapsing, zero of five drifting, and five of five plateauing. The
remaining rows are: AERO 0.220 ZVF with 0.399 accuracy; CPPO 0.295 with
0.392; NGRPO 0.300 with 0.387; SCAFGRPO 0.317 with 0.409; MCGRPO 0.146
with 0.322; GIFT 0.114 with 0.326; AREAL 0.121 with 0.328; and ES 0.114
with 0.316. The four lowest-ZVF rows each record one of five seeds
drifting and four of five plateauing.

The variance-mitigation rows require a caveat that the source states
explicitly and that this chapter preserves: they are a declared
simulation projection used for sensitivity analysis, and they ``should
be read as synthetic, not as independent real-library deployments''. The
caveat covers the whole panel. The vanilla GRPO row and its
collapse/drift/plateau counts come from the same dry-run file,
\longtok{experiments/results/variance\_mitigation.tsv}, as the other
eight rows, and Chapter 2 §2.3 already records that GRPO baseline row as
synthetic. None of the nine rows is a measured deployment. Anyone citing
the nine-row ordering as evidence that a named method mitigates failure
would be citing a projection.

Two further results sharpen the picture, and the first mixes measured
and projected rows. First, across 60 runs the late-phase ZVF of GRPO
runs sits in the range 0.87--0.97, while early-phase ZVF for
variance-mitigation GRPO and for the largest group size sits in the
range 0.04--0.13; smaller group sizes start higher, between 0.25 and
0.61. The lag-1 autocorrelation of the ZVF trace is approximately 0.94,
meaning the quantity moves smoothly rather than jittering, which is what
makes it usable as a warning indicator at all. Of those 60 runs, 45 are
the five-seed variance-mitigation projection rows above (corrected; see
Table E.1). The measured part is the remainder, which includes the
12-run group-size sweep. The source does not report the ranges or the
autocorrelation separately for the measured subset, so these figures
describe a pool that is mostly synthetic.

Second, and more directly relevant to practice, the diagnostic rises
\emph{after} the reward plateau rather than before it. Measuring
first-passage times, ZVF crosses its warning threshold only 1 to 3 steps
ahead of the plateau. The recommended operating rule from the pillar is
therefore not predictive but custodial: report ZVF alongside mean
reward, and treat a rise of more than 0.3 sustained over ten consecutive
rollout steps as a warning. The project reports this limitation against
its own proposed diagnostic rather than in a footnote.

\subsection{The all-correct wall and the mastery--incapacity
aliasing}\label{the-all-correct-wall-and-the-masteryincapacity-aliasing}

The mechanism behind large ZVF values on easy tasks is a saturation
effect that the project calls the all-correct wall. A matched-budget
panel was run at 2,560 rollouts per arm --- G = 2 over 160 steps against
G = 16 over 20 steps --- on Qwen3-8B with GSM8K, LoRA rank 4 and batch
size 8. The late G = 2 arms reach a mean training reward of
approximately 1.0 with ZVF between 0.75 and 1.0; the G = 16 arms hold
ZVF at or below 0.25 at the same rollout budget. The reading is that
when the policy solves the task, nearly every group becomes all-correct,
nearly every centred reward is zero, and the estimator loses its
contrast even though the reward signal is at its maximum. Signal
starvation in this regime is driven by ease, not by difficulty --- which
is the opposite of the intuition that motivates variance-mitigation
work.

This produces the pillar's most important structural caveat. ZVF is
unsigned and symmetric: an all-wrong group (\(K_x = 0\)) and an
all-correct group (\(K_x = G\)) score identically. The statistic
therefore cannot distinguish mastery from incapacity, and this aliasing
is the explanation the project gives for the weak association between
ZVF and final held-out accuracy. Under an i.i.d.-Bernoulli model the
closed form is \(\mathrm{ZVF}(p_x, G) = h_G(p_x) = p_x^G + (1-p_x)^G\),
and it follows in one line. If the G rewards of a group are independent
Bernoulli(p) draws, the group has zero variance exactly when all G
rewards are equal. That happens when all are 1, with probability
\(p^G\), or all are 0, with probability \((1-p)^G\). The two events are
disjoint, so \(P(\text{zero variance}) = p^G + (1-p)^G\). At
\(p = \tfrac12\) this is \(h_G = 2^{1-G}\), so \(\ln h_G\) falls by
\(\ln 2\) for each unit increase in G. The project's source document for
this closed form and the aliasing analysis is headed as reasoning
contributions and proposed theorems from ChatGPT Pro Extended and Gemini
Deep Think, ``NOT experiments this paper ran''. That provenance is kept
on record, and AI assistance is declared in the front matter, but the
result does not rest on it: the derivation above is elementary and is
the argument this thesis relies on. What the project itself measured is
the empirical ZVF against the closed form, evaluated at the per-step
training reward rate because ZVF is a training-batch quantity
(corrected; see Table E.1). Recomputed from the per-step training
rewards in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.json}
(3 seeds × 40 steps per arm), the per-step-average null is 0.828, 0.709,
0.597 and 0.522. The measured mean ZVF is 0.838, 0.764, 0.691 and 0.631,
so the residuals are +0.010, +0.055, +0.094 and +0.109: measured ZVF
sits \emph{above} the null at every G. Gradient utilisation rises across
the same range from 0.162 to 0.369. The direction of that gap depends
entirely on which baseline is used, and the project's own formalisation
names the correct one. Evaluating the closed form at a single pooled or
held-out rate overstates the Bernoulli prediction, because \(h_G\) is
convex and the per-step reward rate varies; the right baseline is the
per-step average of the closed form, which is \(0.60\) at \(G = 8\)
rather than the \(0.33\) obtained at the pooled mean. Against the
per-step-average baseline the measured ZVF at \(G = 8\) (\(0.69\))
\emph{exceeds} the null by approximately \(0.09\), and the source is
explicit that most of that apparent gap is the Jensen effect rather than
correlated completions and that it therefore ``do{[}es{]} not read it as
strong evidence that the independence null is a lower bound''. This
chapter uses only the per-step-average baseline. It is a weak
consistency check on shape rather than evidence about dependence.

The formalisation's own summary of the same sweep, twelve runs and 480
logged steps, reports per-step observed ZVF correlating at r = 0.92 with
the Bernoulli-null prediction \(p^G + (1-p)^G\), with mean ZVF of 0.84,
0.76, 0.69 and 0.63 at G = 2, 4, 8 and 16 respectively. That correlation
is a shape check, not a validation: the null for each step is computed
from the same step's mean reward, so a high r is partly mechanical. At
larger scale the same instrument was run on Qwen3-8B against GSM8K test
problems through the Tinker managed API at G = 8, temperature 1.0, over
200 problems and three seeds (sampling only, no training; these are the
three stored tensors of §6.3.1), yielding a mean ZVF of 0.16 composed of
12.7\% all-correct groups, 3.2\% all-wrong groups and 84\% mixed groups;
the raw correlation with the mechanical null is approximately r = 0.93
with rank ρ ≈ 0.65, and the i.i.d. null over-predicts (approximately
0.28 against the observed 0.16). The direction of the deviation from the
null is therefore opposite in the two runs: above the null on the 0.5B
sweep, below it on this Qwen3-8B sampling run. The source does not state
which baseline produced the 0.28, and neither deviation is read as
evidence about dependence between completions.

\subsection{Prognostic value}\label{prognostic-value}

The pillar's central claim is not that ZVF is large but that it is
informative, and here the results split cleanly. Pooling the measured
runs, the naive correlation between mean ZVF and last-10 accuracy is r =
+0.22, which is not significant. The residual table on disk pools n = 17
per-experiment rows, and at n = 17 the Fisher-z 95\% CI is {[}−0.29,
+0.63{]} (corrected; see Table E.1). After mean reward is regressed out
of last-10 accuracy, the pooled residual correlation with ZVF is r =
+0.205 (Fisher 95\% CI {[}−0.31, +0.62{]}, p = 0.43, n = 17
experiments), and its interval includes zero. The source's own
conclusion from that analysis is that ``we therefore do not treat ZVF as
an incremental predictor above reward mean'', and this chapter keeps
that conclusion. Neither the raw nor the residualised diagnostic is
shown to predict graded outcome on measured data.

Pooling across task types shows why a pooled association cannot be read
as a score. Over N = 15 experiments spanning five model families, the
association between ZVF and performance is Pearson r = −0.769 (p =
0.0008) with Spearman ρ = −0.784 (p = 0.0005), but it is confounded by
task type: tool-use experiments reach ZVF = 100\% at approximately 0\%
reward while GSM8K experiments average ZVF = 8.5\%. Within GSM8K the
association weakens to r = +0.40 (p = 0.09, N = 19), and then to r =
+0.13 (p = 0.79) when restricted to the seven model-varying runs. The N
= 19 is a separate and larger pool of Tinker GSM8K runs, not a subset of
the N = 15 pooled experiments, so the two analyses are not nested. A
statistic whose sign is set by which tasks are included in the pool is
not a score. On the instrumented runs ZVF also does not track final
accuracy (r ≈ 0.09, n = 12).

Against catastrophic collapse, ZVF performs much better than it does
against graded outcome. Mean ZVF correlates with catastrophic collapse
at Spearman ρ = 0.56 (Fisher 95\% CI {[}0.19, 0.79{]}, n = 23 pooled
cells) and point-biserial 0.62. Its correlation with final held-out
accuracy is ρ = 0.27, and that is not significant: the registry's
bootstrap 95\% CI is {[}−0.37, 0.88{]} and the Fisher CI is {[}−0.16,
0.61{]}, p = 0.21, n = 23. The 23 cells pool 80 heterogeneous local
rows, and the source does not state how many of them are
variance-mitigation projection rows. A three-channel risk index built on
the diagnostic --- magnitude, critical slowing down via a rolling lag-1
autocorrelation with window 15, and drift slope, blended 0.3/0.5/0.2
with a max-fusion variant --- was evaluated over a 52-row
cross-experiment panel comprising 45 variance-mitigation seeds, 2
tool-use collapse anchors and 5 scaling-law collapse anchors, with 11
failures and 41 safe runs. Cross-experiment AUROCs are 0.663 for
magnitude, 0.758 for critical slowing down, 0.579 for drift, 0.763 for
the blended risk index and 0.929 for the max-fusion index;
within-methods, the same values fall to 0.073, 0.335, 0.293, 0.396 and
0.805 respectively. None of these AUROCs is a measurement on real
training runs, and this chapter reports them as a synthetic-panel
exercise. All 45 variance-mitigation rows come from the declared
simulation projection (§6.3.2). The seven collapse anchors enter with
channel values fixed by the analysis script, not measured: mean ZVF 1.0
or an imputed 0.95, lag-1 0.95 or 0.85, and slope 0 or 0.015. The 41
safe rows are therefore all synthetic. The max-fusion index was selected
after the per-axis results were seen. For the 0.929 figure, a
Hanley--McNeil 95\% CI with 11 positives and 41 negatives is {[}0.82,
1.00{]}; that interval describes this constructed panel only. No AUROC
is computed on the measured rows alone, because they contain too few
failures to estimate one. The gap between the cross-experiment and
within-method columns is a warning about the index's transportability:
most of the cross-experiment discrimination is coming from the fact that
collapse clusters by experimental family, not from a within-family
predictive signal. The declared thresholds are a risk above 0.55 to
treat as failure-positive, below 0.30 as safe, and the interval between
as requiring investigation.

The pillar's own conclusion names three load-bearing limitations: the
diagnostic is mechanically coupled to reward sparsity, group size and
baseline accuracy; its strong association holds only for catastrophic
collapse and not for the graded held-out outcome; and the unsigned
statistic aliases mastery with incapacity. A proposed replacement, the
pairwise contrast density, is introduced in the synthesis document with
a target association of ρ ≳ 0.45; the project states that this bar is
not yet met by the replacements and that the head-to-head protocol
remains future work.

\section{P3 --- Group Size and Contrast
Density}\label{p3-group-size-and-contrast-density}

The third pillar studies G, which plays two roles simultaneously in
GRPO: it is a variance-reduction knob, like a Monte-Carlo baseline, and
it is a preference-density knob, since centring rewards within a group
induces implicit pairwise contrasts --- nonzero only for mixed-reward
groups. Practitioners often treat larger G as strictly better by analogy
to variance reduction, which conflates the two roles and ignores cost.
The pillar's conclusion is that the measured evidence does not justify a
group-size optimum and that the dependence of trainability on G is
non-monotone (Figure~\ref{fig:groupsize}).


\fig{fig_group_size}
{Group size $G$: one single-seed sweep, one three-seed sweep and one reconstruction}
{Group size $G$: one single-seed sweep, one three-seed sweep and one reconstruction. (a) Qwen3-8B on GSM8K through Tinker, one seed, 30 steps: peak and last-10 in-training reward (not held-out); $G=8$ is highest, but with no seed replication no optimum can be established. (b) Qwen2.5-0.5B on arithmetic, 3 seeds, 40 steps: held-out accuracy and mean train reward, bars $\pm1.96$~SE (approximate 95\% CI). The held-out apex at $G=8$ (0.990) overlaps $G=4$ (0.988) and $G=16$ (0.978); overlap is descriptive, not a test, and the arms are statistically indistinguishable. (c) Mean zero-variance fraction on the same sweep, falling by 0.207 from $G=2$ to $G=16$. (d) Reconstructed retention of $G=4$ relative to $G=32$ against token budget (dashed line and open markers: reconstructed values, not matched-budget training runs; bars are the file's conditional reconstruction intervals). The point estimate reaches the Wu et al.\ 0.976 retention level at 1 of 4 budgets ($T=1$M), but the equivalence test at that margin is not passed at any budget. Sources: paper/expected\_results.json (a); experiments/results/group\_\allowbreak{}size\_\allowbreak{}effect.tsv (b--d).}
{fig:groupsize}



\subsection{The measured sweep}\label{the-measured-sweep}

The primary sweep measures G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\} on a near-ceiling
two-digit addition task with Qwen2.5-0.5B, three seeds and 40 steps,
evaluated on 200 fresh draws from the training generator. Held-out
accuracy (200 fresh problems per run) traces an inverted U with its apex
at G = 8. Values are mean ± SE over n = 3 seeds: 0.9900 ± 0.0029 (SD
0.0050) at G = 8, 0.9817 ± 0.0044 (SD 0.0076) at G = 2, 0.9883 ± 0.0017
(SD 0.0029) at G = 4 and 0.9783 ± 0.0060 (SD 0.0104) at G = 16. At n = 3
seeds the arms are statistically indistinguishable: a one-way ANOVA
across the four arms gives F(3, 8) = 1.82, p = 0.22, recomputed from
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.json}.
The minimum detectable effect at 3 versus 3 seeds is d = 3.07, so only
very large differences could have been detected. A separate check run at
B = 4000 with seed 20260702 records G = 2 and G = 16 at 0.9817 and
0.9783, a difference of +0.0033 (G = 2 minus G = 16) and an interval of
{[}−0.008, +0.015{]}; G = 4 and G = 8 add +0.010 and +0.012
respectively, and G = 4 versus G = 32 was not measured on that sweep.

The retention figures are the cleanest way to state the same result. G =
2 retains 100.3\% of G = 16. A later unified synthesis at the same
configuration reports ``retention'' ratios and signal-to-noise ratios
with intervals. The ``retention'' is a ratio of last-10 \emph{training}
reward, not of held-out accuracy, so this chapter calls it a
training-reward ratio. The point values are G = 2 1.0000, G = 4 0.9941,
G = 8 0.9960 and G = 16 1.0068, with SNR 1.46, 1.76, 1.94 and 2.16. All
four ratios sit above the Wu et al. \citep{wu2025ittakestwo} 97.6\%
reference bar. At the seed level (paired TOST on per-seed last-10
training reward against G = 2, margin ±0.02, n = 3 seeds), the TOST
p-values are 0.012 at G = 4, 0.043 at G = 8 and 0.056 at G = 16
(corrected; see Table E.1). Equivalence to G = 2 at that margin is
therefore supported, narrowly, for G = 4 and G = 8, and is \textbf{not}
established for G = 16.

The signal-to-noise ratio grows sublinearly with G: it reaches about
52\% of the √G ideal, and about 48\% above a flat baseline. Reward
itself is statistically flat across the sweep, in the range
0.971--0.984. The marginal ZVF gain from G = 8 to G = 16 is 0.6906 →
0.6312, a fall of 0.059, which is smaller than the G = 2 → G = 4 fall of
0.8380 → 0.7635, a fall of 0.075. Diminishing returns on the contrast
axis are visible in the measured data, which is what the reconstruction
later formalises.

The pillar's own framing of this regime is important and is stated in
its abstract: near the ceiling, variance is compressed, so the finding
is ``no detectable G effect under saturation'', not evidence of
equivalence on harder, unsaturated tasks. That is, the flat retention
curve is a property of an easy task, and the pillar explicitly declines
to generalise it. The measured equivalence tests were run up to G = 16,
and at the seed level they establish equivalence only up to G = 8; there
is no directly trained, matched-budget G = 32 cell in the primary sweep.
The retention-versus-budget axis is where the picture changes: R(1M) =
0.976, R(4M) = 0.833, R(16M) = 0.750 and R(64M) = 0.727, fitted by
\(R(T) = 0.729 + 0.247\,e^{-T/5.76\mathrm{M}}\) with \(R^2 = 0.948\) and
an asymptote of 0.729. The four points are themselves reconstructed
(§6.4.2), and a three-parameter curve through four points leaves one
residual degree of freedom. The fit is therefore illustrative only, and
no confidence interval is reported for its asymptote (corrected; see
Table E.1). The Wu reference line and this reconstructed curve cross at
T = 1M and diverge by 25 percentage points at T = 64M. The operative
conclusion is scoped to the 0.5B arithmetic task: no G is
distinguishable there, so G = 2 is the cheapest setting at statistically
indistinguishable accuracy. It is not a recommendation for harder tasks,
and it does not conflict with the single-seed Qwen3.5-4B observation of
§6.4.3 that G = 2 under-trains with a 50\% batch collapse, which is a
different model and task.

\subsection{The same-stack four-axis audit and the reconstructed
budgets}\label{the-same-stack-four-axis-audit-and-the-reconstructed-budgets}

The strongest de-confound for P3 is a four-axis factorization audit of
the same training stack. On the algorithm axis, the residual effect is
small: η² = 0.023, from the same five-seed same-stack contrast as
§6.2.3. The paired GRPO−PPO difference is −0.002. The factorization file
reports a Monte-Carlo permutation p of 0.62; the exact sign-flip p over
the five pairs is 0.625, and the paired t p of 0.374 in §6.2.3 is the
primary test for this contrast. Equivalence on the algorithm axis is not
established, because the TOST at ±0.005 in §6.2.3 does not establish it
(corrected; see Table E.1). On the group-size axis the file reports η² =
0.54 and 0.63, with Cohen's d = −1.47 for the G = 2 → 16 comparison. The
0.54 is \longtok{eta2\_\allowbreak{}G\_\allowbreak{}last10}, a share of last-10 \emph{training
reward}, not of terminal held-out accuracy; 0.63 is for gradient norm.
Recomputed on the 12 sweep runs (3 seeds per G), the training-reward
share gives F(3, 8) = 3.08, p = 0.090. The corresponding held-out share
is η² = 0.41, F(3, 8) = 1.82, p = 0.22. The source itself grades the
G-axis result only SUGGESTIVE. The supportable reading is that the
group-size axis shows a large but not statistically significant share of
training-reward variance at n = 3 seeds per arm, and that the algorithm
label is not shown to matter on this stack.

A Miller-style \citep{miller2024errorbars} error-bars audit covered the
pillar's four headline contrasts. Two of them are held here at
hypothesis-generator status rather than reported as precise (corrected;
see Table E.1): the gradient-utilisation ratio at T = 1M, with a point
estimate of 5.03 and no interval that n = 4 budgets can support, and the
negative retention-versus-training-token slope, which is fitted to the
four \emph{reconstructed} budgets of the grid below, not to trained
arms. The SNR-versus-√G slope has an interval of {[}+0.148, +0.583{]},
which contains the ideal value of +0.500 and is therefore graded NULL.
The native Wu-style equivalence claim that G = 2 ≈ G = 16 is also graded
NULL. This chapter therefore reports none of the four P3 headlines as
precise, and the pillar's own summary is that the data do not justify a
universal optimum.

Everything at or above G = 32 in the pillar comes from an explicitly
reconstructed token-budget grid, retained only as a hypothesis
generator. The reconstruction table gives, for G = 4 versus G = 32, a T
= 1M comparison of 0.41 against 0.42 with a difference of −0.01, a
97.6\% retention ratio and a ``generalizes'' verdict of yes, a
point-estimate criterion of retention at least 90\% --- the equivalence
test at the 97.6\% margin, with a reconstruction interval of {[}0.873,
1.092{]} at T = 1M, is not passed at any of the four budgets; at T = 4M,
0.55 against 0.66 with −0.11, 83.3\% and no; at T = 16M, 0.63 against
0.84 with −0.21, 75.0\% and no; at T = 64M, 0.64 against 0.88 with
−0.24, 72.7\% and no. The source labels these as conditional
reconstruction intervals, not fresh-run confidence intervals. The T =
64M accuracy row across G = 4, 8, 16, 32 and 64 reads 0.64, 0.80, 0.85,
0.88 and 0.87 --- the apex moves with budget, which is precisely the
pattern that makes a universal G optimum incoherent, and precisely the
pattern the project declines to claim as measured. A fit of the
critical-budget form \(|\Delta(T)| = a(1 - e^{-T/\tau})\) gives â = 0.30
and τ̂ ≈ 12.1M tokens, with a 5\% critical budget of 2.21M tokens and an
interval of {[}1.29M, 4.77M{]}. A difficulty-gated adaptive rule derived
from this analysis reports a mean selected group size of 7.0 and a
projected 56.2\% rollout saving against always using G = 16, with 5 of
20 cells under-predicting by more than 5 percentage points. These are
projections, and the chapter marks them as such.

A separate conditional utility audit over 505 tasks, scoring each group
size by (1 − ZVF)/√G, selects G = 4 over G = 5 by 0.00177 with a 95\%
bootstrap interval of {[}0.00082, 0.00270{]}. The margin is positive but
small, and it is conditional on cohort and objective.

\subsection{The curriculum and matched-token null
results}\label{the-curriculum-and-matched-token-null-results}

Two interventions that P3's design space naturally suggests were run and
both returned nulls. They are reported here rather than elsewhere
because they are group-size interventions, and because their negative
results are load-bearing for the chapter's overall argument.

The first is a difficulty curriculum that filters collapsed groups
during training. The intervention works as designed on its own terms: it
drives the zero-gradient waste fraction from 0.50 to 0.00, eliminating
wasted rollouts completely. It does so at approximately 4.81 times the
sampling cost, and its held-out gain is identical to the baseline's ---
both arms move from 0.80 to 0.85 on a held-out set of n = 20 (one seed,
eight steps), a gain of +0.05 in each case, which is one item of 20. At
a matched 30k-token budget the curriculum ties the baseline at +0.028
against +0.028, and at five times the cost it loses. The verdict
recorded is Null. An important provenance note attaches to this result:
an independent verification pass conducted on 6 July 2026 flagged the
curriculum claim, along with a single-seed G = 4 ``win'' of +0.125, as
SUSPECT and overclaimed, before multi-seed re-testing confirmed both as
noise. The group-size sweet-spot result is itself a null for the same
reason --- the +0.125 advantage at G = 4 rested on a single held-out
example and did not survive multi-seed re-testing, and G = 2
under-trains with a 50\% batch collapse and a +0.0 gain.

The second null family concerns loss formulations intended to remove the
length bias. On the held-out metric, the standard sum formulation is
nominally the best of the three tested: +0.125 against +0.000 for
mean-normalisation and +0.042 for the surprise formulation. These are
measured on held-out sets of n = 12--20 per arm, so each difference
corresponds to about one or two items and none is distinguishable from
noise. The length change under the standard arm is recorded as Δlen =
−16. The verdict is Null. A fourth null, on a step-1 predictive
layer-freeze, is reported in the same table because it was proposed as a
cheap way to stabilise group-relative training: it achieves a per-step
overlap of 1.0 on a 1.5B hardcoded-arithmetic toy, but only 0.11 ---
approximately chance, on both seeds --- at 3B on real GSM8K, while the
concentration statistic still holds at approximately 0.39. A later
four-seed pass at 3B supersedes that two-seed figure with a mean overlap
of 0.083 ± 0.048 (mean ± SD, n = 4 seeds), also approximately chance.
Verdict: Null, and a reversal of its own toy-scale positive under
scaling.

The pattern across all four nulls is consistent and worth stating
plainly: the interventions that target the mechanism the diagnostic
identifies do not improve held-out outcomes at matched cost. That is a
negative result about the diagnostic's prescriptive power as much as
about the interventions themselves, and it is one of the reasons P2 is
reported as a monitoring tool rather than an optimisation objective.

\section{P4 --- Length Bias and Held-Out
Generalisation}\label{p4-length-bias-and-held-out-generalisation}

The fourth pillar tests the most widely repeated specific criticism of
GRPO: that it rewards verbosity, inflating completion length in a way
that Dr.~GRPO corrects. The pillar's headline is a scoped null, and the
scope is the point (Figure~\ref{fig:lengthbias}).


\fig{fig_length_bias}
{The post-hoc peak-then-decay rule (peak before 65\% of logged steps and terminal reward below $0.90\times$ peak) on the roster and on the controlled comparison}
{The post-hoc peak-then-decay rule (peak before 65\% of logged steps and terminal reward below $0.90\times$ peak) on the roster and on the controlled comparison. (a) The four of eleven roster GRPO runs that carry the flag (single seed each; legend gives peak$\rightarrow$terminal reward and t/p $=$ terminal$\div$peak; stars mark peaks). (b)--(d) The controlled sixteen-run comparison, seed mean (line) and min--max across seeds (band); dashed vertical line at 65\%, dotted horizontal line at the arm's last-10 mean. (b) Arithmetic, Qwen2.5-0.5B, 40 steps, 5 seeds per arm: every seed peaks at 1.0 and stays there, so the rule fires on 0 of 10 runs. (c, d) GSM8K-CoT, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, 30 steps, 3 seeds per arm: seed peaks (stars $=$ flagged, open circle $=$ not flagged) fall at 33--63\% of training and the last-10 mean (0.26 GRPO, 0.25 Dr.\ GRPO) is far below every peak, so the rule fires on 3 of 3 GRPO and 2 of 3 Dr.\ GRPO runs. Completion length falls in all sixteen runs, so the pillar's length-bias flag (length rising while reward stays flat or falls) is zero everywhere: the peak-then-decay rule marks noisy reward in both algorithms, not length inflation. Flags recomputed from the per-step logs in drgrpo\_\allowbreak{}vs\_\allowbreak{}grpo.json and drgrpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot\_\allowbreak{}full.json.}
{fig:lengthbias}



\subsection{The controlled null}\label{the-controlled-null}

Sixteen controlled runs were analysed: ten on an arithmetic task with
Qwen2.5-0.5B over 40 steps, and six on GSM8K chain-of-thought with
Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct over 30 steps, split evenly between GRPO and
Dr.~GRPO. The arithmetic half is saturated: evaluation accuracy is 0.987
for GRPO and 0.992 for Dr.~GRPO (paired p = 0.35, five seeds), and
completions average 4.7 tokens, so its accuracy and length nulls are
nulls at a ceiling. Only the GSM8K-CoT half has headroom. At these
30--40-step horizons, neither algorithm exhibits the verbosity-trap
signature on either task: the pillar's length-bias flag, which fires
when completion length grows while reward stays flat or falls, is zero
on every one of the sixteen runs. That flag is not the roster audit's
peak-then-decay rule of §6.5.2. Recomputed from the per-step logs, the
peak-then-decay rule (peak before 65\% of training, last-10 mean reward
below 90\% of peak) fires on none of the ten arithmetic runs but on five
of the six GSM8K-CoT runs, three GRPO and two Dr.~GRPO, because the
reward trace on that task is noisy and peaks early; length still falls
in all five.

The summary correlations are as follows, given as mean ± SD across seeds
(n = 5 arithmetic, n = 3 GSM8K-CoT). On arithmetic, the rank correlation
between training step and completion length is −0.405 ± 0.176 for GRPO
and −0.307 ± 0.181 for Dr.~GRPO; on GSM8K-CoT it is −0.622 ± 0.301 and
−0.355 ± 0.255. The step-versus-reward correlations are +0.709 ± 0.082
and +0.753 ± 0.074 on arithmetic, and +0.046 ± 0.162 and +0.025 ± 0.091
on GSM8K-CoT. The length-versus-reward correlations are −0.419 ± 0.155
and −0.293 ± 0.182 on arithmetic, and −0.501 ± 0.258 and −0.618 ± 0.118
on GSM8K-CoT. Mean reward over the last ten steps is 0.988 for both
algorithms on arithmetic, and 0.265 for GRPO against 0.252 for Dr.~GRPO
on GSM8K-CoT. The direction of the length trend is negative everywhere:
all sixteen length slopes are negative, with the arithmetic GRPO slope
at −0.029 tokens per step and the GSM8K GRPO slope at −0.481, and the
count of runs with a positive horizon is zero. On the harder task,
GRPO's final-ten-step mean completion length is 184.4 tokens against
Dr.~GRPO's 189.3 --- a difference of +2.7\% in the direction opposite to
the critique.

The pillar's abstract states the scope of this null explicitly. The
claim is bounded by the 200-token cap used in that regime, and the
summary sentence is that ``the verbosity-trap signature is absent in
this regime''. The pillar is not claiming that GRPO never inflates
length; it is claiming that within a capped, short-horizon regime it
does not, and it names the length-confounded regime in which a Dr.~GRPO
advantage should appear as future work.

\subsection{The roster-level verbosity-trap
audit}\label{the-roster-level-verbosity-trap-audit}

Set against the controlled null is a descriptive audit over the whole
experiment roster, and here the picture differs. The audit defines four
statistics per trace with at least five steps: an instability index
σ(r\_tail)/\textbar μ(r\_tail)\textbar{} over the final 50\% of
training; a monotonicity score, the fraction of consecutive step-pairs
with strictly improving reward; a regression severity, the largest
normalised drop from peak; and a binary verbosity-trap flag, set when
the peak occurs before 65\% of training and the terminal reward falls
below 90\% of peak.

On that definition, the eleven GRPO runs in the roster have a mean
instability index of 0.267 ± 0.335, against 0.785 ± 0.297 (mean ± spread
across runs; the source does not label the ±, and its size relative to
the mean implies an SD) for the seventeen PPO-labelled runs. Four of the
eleven GRPO runs, 36\% (Wilson 95\% CI {[}15\%, 65\%{]}, one run per
configuration), carry the flag on this definition, including both
Qwen3-8B runs and the Nemotron-120B run. That count is reported here as
a description of the roster, not as evidence of length bias, because the
flag does not isolate the behaviour it is named for. Three properties of
the rule prevent the stronger reading. Its thresholds are post hoc:
neither the 65\% peak position nor the 0.90 terminal-to-peak ratio is
pre-registered anywhere in the repository, and the rule cannot be traced
to a commit that predates the traces it judges. It is not specific to
length: the metric it tests is in-training reward, and the project's own
source reads the flag as ``a generic instability detector'' rather than
a length measure, so a run can carry it without any change in response
length. And its base rate is high: applied to the wider trace pool
rather than this eleven-run subset, the rule fires on the majority of
runs --- including PPO-labelled runs and the Dr.~GRPO control in the one
matched comparison --- so 36\% sits at or below the rate the rule
produces on traces with no decay at all. The project's own length data
point the other way: under the 200-token cap, length drifts from 194.4
to 183.5 tokens, i.e.~shorter. The run-level audit therefore does not
establish length inflation, and the pillar's negative finding rests on
the controlled comparison below rather than on this roster count. The
Nemotron run records the highest instability index in the pool at 1.194,
with a regression severity of 1.0 and a terminal-to-peak reward ratio of
0.57. The two DeepSeek-V3.1 GRPO runs, by contrast, show markedly lower
instability, with a mean of 0.143. The PPO rate of 12/17 has a Wilson
95\% CI of {[}47\%, 87\%{]}, which overlaps the GRPO interval, so the
two rates are not distinguished either.

Two caveats on that comparison must travel with it. First, the PPO pool
is dominated by classic-RL library runs (SB3, CleanRL, Tianshou) that
failed to train at all, with last-10 rewards below 5\%; a near-zero
reward trajectory mechanically produces a high instability index.
Restricted to LLM-native PPO, the PPO Llama instability is 0.105, lower
than GRPO Llama's 0.228, and the source therefore explicitly declines to
claim that GRPO is more or less stable than PPO at the algorithmic
level. Second, the audit's reward metric is the in-training reward, not
a held-out one, and the audit pool is single-seed per configuration:
each Tinker configuration was run once and is reported descriptively.
The only multi-seed matched base-versus-RL control on Qwen3-8B is the
five-seed comparison discussed below.

An earlier draft of this report's summary attributed to P4 the claim
that Dr.~GRPO is the only algorithm without the verbosity-trap pattern.
That claim could not be located in any P4 source. What the sources state
is that 36\% of the audit's GRPO runs carry the flag; the audit does not
report a per-algorithm flag rate for Dr.~GRPO, and in the controlled
sixteen-run comparison the length-bias flag is zero for both algorithms
while the peak-then-decay rule fires on both (three of three GRPO and
two of three Dr.~GRPO runs on GSM8K-CoT). The finding recorded here is
therefore the sourced one: the peak-then-decay pattern is not specific
to GRPO, it appears in both arms of the controlled comparison without
any length inflation, and the project makes no claim that Dr.~GRPO is
uniquely free of it. The narrowest related statement the sources do
support is a length-drift one --- that Dr.~GRPO is the only algorithm
whose length keeps drifting upward after the initial compression phase
--- which is a different statistic and is not the verbosity-trap flag.

\subsection{Elevated analyses}\label{elevated-analyses}

Because the controlled 30--40-step null is a null, the pillar runs a
sequence of progressively more sensitive analyses to establish whether
the trap is present but invisible at that horizon. Four of these deserve
report, and all four are exploratory. They were run across iterations
12, 20 and 24 of the analysis log after the controlled null was known.
The seed counts are small: n = 5 arithmetic and n = 3 GSM8K seeds, and
three seeds admit only 10 distinct bootstrap resamples. Several of the
intervals resample autocorrelated steps or windows within runs rather
than seeds. The p-values below are uncorrected unless stated, and
roughly fifteen tests are reported across the three iterations.

A sliding-window trap-onset analysis with window W = 10 relaxes the flag
from ``peak before 65\% of training'' to ``local peak followed by decay
within a window'', and finds traps in 2 of 10 arithmetic runs (20\%), 4
of 6 GSM8K runs (67\%) and 6 of 16 runs overall (37.5\%) --- every one
of which is a local re-expansion rather than a terminal divergence. This
is the analysis that most nearly matches the roster-level trap rate. It
is a point estimate of trap prevalence in the controlled data, not an
upper bound (corrected; see Table E.1). The Wilson 95\% CIs are
{[}5.7\%, 51\%{]} for 2/10, {[}30\%, 90\%{]} for 4/6 and {[}18.5\%,
61\%{]} for 6/16, with runs as the unit.

A paired bootstrap on the Dr.~GRPO minus GRPO difference in rank
correlations gives, on arithmetic, Δρ(step, len) = +0.098 with an
interval of {[}+0.073, +0.119{]} --- excluding zero, which suggests that
Dr.~GRPO compresses length relative to GRPO on the easy task
(seed-paired bootstrap, n = 5); Δρ(step, reward) = +0.043 with an
interval of {[}−0.024, +0.112{]}, which is not significant; and Δρ(len,
reward) = +0.126 with an interval of {[}+0.045, +0.201{]}, again
excluding zero. On GSM8K the same three quantities are +0.267 {[}+0.164,
+0.419{]}, −0.020 {[}−0.069, +0.065{]} and −0.117 {[}−0.265, +0.015{]}.
The exploratory reading is that Dr.~GRPO's length attenuation is
task-dependent, and that on the harder task it deepens rather than
weakens the length--reward coupling.

A decile-binned coupling analysis on GSM8K shows the mechanism directly:
completions in the first length decile, spanning 153--180 tokens, earn a
mean reward of 0.351, while completions in the eighth decile, spanning
194--195 tokens, earn 0.233. Longer completions are rewarded less, which
is the opposite of the verbosity-trap prediction. A 100-step
cross-validation on the arithmetic metrics file returns ρ(step, len) =
+0.41 and ρ(step, reward) = +0.27, but the absolute length range across
that run is 0.08 tokens against a cap of 5 tokens --- a positive
correlation over a range too small to be a length effect.

The length--ZVF coupling analysis at iteration 12 is the most
mechanistically interesting of the elevated results, because its sign
flips between tasks. On arithmetic, ρ(len, zvf) is −0.449 {[}−0.584,
−0.324{]} for GRPO and −0.291 {[}−0.428, −0.139{]} for Dr.~GRPO; on
GSM8K it is +0.286 {[}+0.231, +0.348{]} and +0.187 {[}+0.148, +0.225{]}.
The Dr.~GRPO minus GRPO difference is +0.158 {[}+0.075, +0.239{]} on
arithmetic and −0.099 {[}−0.132, −0.041{]} on GSM8K. Conditioning ZVF on
length bins makes the arithmetic structure concrete: the shortest bin
has ZVF 0.92 and the longest 0.62, a drop of −0.30 {[}−0.34, −0.26{]},
whereas on GSM8K the corresponding values are 0.30 and 0.41, a rise of
+0.11 {[}+0.10, +0.13{]}. On the easy task, shorter completions are more
often all-same-reward; on the hard task, longer ones are.

The mechanism analysis at iteration 20 fits, per run, a regression of
step-level mean length on step-level mean reward. On arithmetic,
Dr.~GRPO reduces the slope magnitude: GRPO −3.65 ± 0.08 against Dr.~GRPO
−3.22 ± 0.15 (mean ± SD over 5 seeds). The paired differences below are
recomputed from
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/length\_\allowbreak{}bias\_\allowbreak{}mechanism\_\allowbreak{}per\_\allowbreak{}run.tsv}
as Dr.~GRPO minus GRPO, paired by seed, n = 5 (corrected; see Table
E.1). Each is given with a paired-t 95\% CI, the paired-t p and the
exact sign-flip permutation p; the permutation p cannot fall below
0.0625 at n = 5. The OLS slope difference is +0.43 {[}+0.18, +0.68{]},
paired t p = 0.009, permutation p = 0.0625. The Theil--Sen slope
difference is +1.11 {[}+0.33, +1.88{]}, p = 0.017, permutation p =
0.0625. The ρ(L, R) difference is +0.10 {[}−0.02, +0.22{]}, p = 0.080.
The lag-1 autocorrelation is essentially unchanged, −0.01 {[}−0.27,
+0.26{]}, p = 0.94. On GSM8K (n = 3) the OLS difference reverses sign,
−4.41 {[}−37.7, +28.9{]}, p = 0.63, far too wide to interpret. Across
the fourteen exploratory tests of iterations 20 and 24,
Benjamini--Hochberg correction leaves the arithmetic OLS and Theil--Sen
differences at q = 0.051 and 0.058, just above 0.05. A mediation
analysis attributes 42\% of the arithmetic effect to ZVF under GRPO and
55\% under Dr.~GRPO, against 28\% and 12\% on GSM8K.

The drift analysis at iteration 24 closes the sequence. Windowed ρ drift
on arithmetic is +0.326 for GRPO with p = 0.035, decomposing into an
early −0.330 and a late −0.003, against +0.187 for Dr.~GRPO with p =
0.399; on GSM8K the corresponding values are +0.019 with p = 0.905 and
−0.084 with p = 0.769. The sign-flip analysis of late windows gives
fractions of 0.527 (p = 0.468), 0.382 (p = 0.562), 0.048 (p = 0.011) and
0.000 (p \textless{} 0.001) across the four algorithm-task cells. The p
\textless{} 0.001 cell is the GSM8K Dr.~GRPO arm, in which not a single
late window flips sign. It is the only one of the fourteen exploratory
tests that stays below 0.05 after Benjamini--Hochberg correction (q =
0.014). Its unit is the within-run window, not the seed, so it too
describes three runs rather than a replicated effect.

\subsection{Held-out generalisation and the scale
extension}\label{held-out-generalisation-and-the-scale-extension}

The pillar's held-out results are the part of the chapter closest to a
conventional capability claim, and they are reported with corresponding
care. The only multi-seed matched base-versus-RL control on Qwen3-8B is
a five-seed comparison on Qwen3-8B-Instruct, which moves from 82.0\% to
83.3\% with p = 0.26. Stated as a mean improvement over the pre-RL
checkpoint, the gain is +0.013 with a paired p = 0.256 at n = 5 ---
small and not statistically significant, and a matched pre-RL control
for the Qwen3-8B-Base checkpoint is not available. In summary, on this
near-ceiling configuration, GRPO does not produce a held-out improvement
that can be separated from noise at the available seed budget, and that
GRPO and Dr.~GRPO produce held-out gains that are indistinguishable from
each other at that budget.

The lower-accuracy configuration behaves differently and more
conventionally. On Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct with GSM8K-CoT, both algorithms
do produce significant pre-to-post held-out gains: +6.2 percentage
points for GRPO and +5.0 for Dr.~GRPO (n = 3 seeds × 200 held-out items
per arm). The unit of analysis is the seed. A one-sample t on the three
per-seed deltas gives p = 0.0051 for GRPO (deltas +0.055, +0.060,
+0.070) and p = 0.0099 for Dr.~GRPO (+0.045, +0.060, +0.045). As
supporting detail, per-seed McNemar exact p-values are 0.071, 0.036 and
0.016 for GRPO and 0.122, 0.017 and 0.122 for Dr.~GRPO; no pooled
McNemar p-value is used, because the same 200 items recur across seeds
(corrected; see Table E.1). The two algorithms' gains do not differ
(paired by seed, p = 0.25) (recomputed from
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/drgrpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}cot.json; source:
platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/p4\_\allowbreak{}results\_\allowbreak{}intro.tex). This is the
project's clearest evidence that the pipeline trains something real; it
is reported alongside the near-ceiling null precisely because the
contrast between the two configurations is what the pillar's
bounded-null interpretation rests on. Across the same comparison, the
pooled mean completion length drifts from 194.4 to 183.5 tokens, that is
downward.

A scale extension tests whether the null survives when the cap is
lifted. Qwen3-8B with LoRA rank 4 was run on GSM8K for 30 steps at G = 8
and batch size 4, three seeds per algorithm, with the completion cap
raised fivefold to 1,024 tokens. All six arms compress: GRPO moves 1004
→ 905, 981 → 944 and 996 → 900 tokens, and Dr.~GRPO moves 999 → 931, 972
→ 902 and 1000 → 878 --- declines of between 3.8\% and 12.2\% in every
arm. Late ZVF is 0.45, 0.70 and 0.72 for Dr.~GRPO against 0.47, 0.47 and
0.55 for GRPO, and step-0 completions are approximately 98\% of the cap.
The step-0 saturation is the caveat that limits this extension: with
completions already at 98\% of the ceiling at the first step, the run
starts truncated, and length compression under a binding cap is not the
same phenomenon as length control in an uncapped regime. The pillar
reports the extension as consistent with the null, not as an independent
confirmation of it.

Finally, the P4-facing headline in the Miller-style error-bars audit is
the iteration-136 arithmetic late-training-efficiency contrast. Late
efficiency is training-reward gain per unit of length change. The
contrast is Dr.~GRPO minus GRPO, paired by seed, n = 5, with mean
difference +0.020. Recomputed from
\longtok{length\_\allowbreak{}bias\_\allowbreak{}iter136\_\allowbreak{}step\_\allowbreak{}coupling.tsv}, the effect size is
\(d_z\) = +2.68, the mean paired difference divided by the SD of the
differences. The exact one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank p is 0.031, which
is 1/32, its floor at n = 5 (corrected; see Table E.1). The paired t
that matches \(d_z\) gives t(4) = 5.96, two-sided p = 0.004. The
two-sided exact sign-flip p is 0.0625, again the n = 5 floor: all five
pairs point the same way, yet the non-parametric test cannot reach 0.05
two-sided. The contrast was one of eight directional hypotheses in the
iteration-136 family (four per task). Under Holm correction across the
eight, the family's own Wilcoxon p becomes 0.25. This chapter therefore
grades it \emph{suggestive}, not decisive (corrected; see Table E.1): a
consistent direction across five seeds on a training metric, with
two-sided non-parametric p = 0.0625 at n = 5. The audit's three NULL
verdicts fall on Pillar-1 and Pillar-3 headlines rather than on this
one. The pillar also records its own limitations plainly: a
single-benchmark held-out evaluation, a single-seed top-10-checkpoint
frontier comparison, weak length identifiability, and marginal paired
tests.

\section{Cross-Pillar Observations and
Limitations}\label{cross-pillar-observations-and-limitations}

Three observations span the four pillars and are worth stating together,
because the chapter's overall contribution is better captured by their
pattern than by any single number.

First, the project's strongest results are its de-confounds, and its
de-confounds mostly return nulls. The matched same-stack PPO-versus-GRPO
contrast returns a paired difference of −0.002 with p = 0.374 (n = 5
seeds, at a near-ceiling accuracy, not shown equivalent by TOST at
±0.005); the four-axis factorization audit attributes η² = 0.023 to the
algorithm axis and η² = 0.54 of last-10 training-reward variance to the
group-size axis (F(3, 8) = 3.08, p = 0.09, n = 12 runs); the controlled
length-bias comparison returns zero length-bias flags across sixteen
runs. In each case the stack was held fixed except the factor under test
(and, in the PPO arm, the number of distinct prompts per step), and in
each case the expected algorithmic advantage failed to appear. Each of
these comparisons was run on the saturated 0.5B arithmetic task, at
evaluation accuracy 0.98--0.99. The group-size axis carries the largest
variance share, but at n = 3 seeds per arm it does not reach
significance on either training reward or held-out accuracy. It is also
a hyperparameter rather than an algorithm choice. The chapter's
empirical conclusion is therefore conditional on saturation: on a 0.5B
arithmetic task at evaluation accuracy near 0.99, no algorithm-label
effect was detectable, which cannot distinguish ``no effect'' from ``no
headroom''; the sampling configuration is the more plausible lever but
was not shown to matter at this seed budget. The unsaturated
Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT configuration, where pre-to-post gains of 5 to 6
points were measured, is where these contrasts would need to be rerun
(§9.4).

Second, the diagnostics and interventions are less prescriptive than
their motivating literature suggests, and the project says so. ZVF
measures signal starvation reliably. It associates with catastrophic
collapse (ρ = 0.56, 95\% CI {[}0.19, 0.79{]}, n = 23 cells), and its
association with graded held-out accuracy is not distinguishable from
zero (ρ = 0.27, 95\% CI {[}−0.37, 0.88{]}, n = 23), because it is
unsigned and therefore cannot distinguish mastery from incapacity. The
four interventions built on it --- the difficulty curriculum, the
group-size sweet spot, the length-bias loss formulations and the step-1
layer freeze --- all return nulls at matched cost, and two of them (the
curriculum and the G = 4 win) were flagged as SUSPECT by an independent
verification pass before multi-seed re-testing reclassified them as
noise. Reporting an intervention as a null after an independent reviewer
flagged it as overclaimed is, in this project's convention, part of the
result.

Third, the single-seed regime is the dominant shared limitation. The
cross-scale anchors are single-seed and descriptive. The roster-level
verbosity-trap audit is single-seed per configuration, and its
in-training reward metric is not a held-out one. Only the P3 sweep, the
P4 controlled comparison, the five-seed Qwen3-8B held-out control and
the P4 scale extension operate at n ≥ 3, and the Qwen3-8B control is the
only multi-seed matched base-versus-RL comparison on Qwen3-8B; the
Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT pre/post runs and the small-sample pairs of Table
8.B are the other base-versus-trained comparisons. Every headline in
this chapter is therefore stated at the strength that its own evidence
base supports, which is in several cases weaker than the strength at
which it would need to be stated to generalise beyond the named stack.
The reconstructions (G = 32 and above in P3, the T = 64M grid, the
difficulty-gated saving projection) are held at hypothesis-generator
status for the same reason: they are joint-fit extrapolations, not
trained arms, and the project declines to let them carry a
recommendation.

Taken together, the four pillars describe a training method in which no
algorithmic-identity effect was detected, and in which signal
availability and sampling configuration are the more plausible levers,
although they were not shown to be significant at the seed budgets used,
and whose most widely repeated specific failure mode --- length
inflation --- is absent in the regimes where it was tested directly and
present in only a minority of the roster runs where it was measured
descriptively. That is a narrower and more cautious set of conclusions
than the P1--P4 internal reports set out to establish, and the chapter
reports it as such.



\chapter{Results II: Reporting, Registry and Controller
(P5--P7)}\label{results-ii-reporting-registry-and-controller-p5p7}

The three internal reports covered in this chapter form a chain rather
than a set of independent results. P5 makes a claim about the epistemic
status of reinforcement-learning results for language models and
proposes a checkable reporting standard to match it. P6 turns the
standard into a machine-readable artifact with a validator, a badge and
a diff tool. P7 takes the diagnostic that the standard requires authors
to publish --- the zero-variance fraction (ZVF) --- and asks whether it
is mechanistically predictable and ready to control rollout allocation.
Each report is explicit about where its claim stops, and this chapter
reports their evidence in the same register.

\section{A Minimum Reporting Standard for Stack-Conditioned Results
(P5)}\label{a-minimum-reporting-standard-for-stack-conditioned-results-p5}

P5's position is that results in this field are
\emph{stack-conditioned}, and that reporting the framework or algorithm
label without the stack is therefore insufficient to support the claims
that are routinely made from it. The evidence for that position is not a
single demonstration but a family of levers, each showing that varying
one component of the stack, with the label held fixed, moves or flips
the reported outcome or its telemetry.

The cleanest lever is a managed 12-cell head-to-head: four
group-relative labels (GRPO \citep{shao2024deepseekmath}, DAPO
\citep{yu2025dapo}, Dr.~GRPO \citep{liu2025understanding}, GSPO
\citep{zheng2025gspo}) run on one stack --- Tinker-managed, Qwen3.5-4B,
GSM8K, three seeds per arm. Mean last-10 training reward spans 0.710 to
0.744, a band of 0.034, with per-arm values of 0.744 (DAPO), 0.742
(Dr.~GRPO), 0.723 (GRPO) and 0.710 (GSPO); mean ZVF over the same arms
is 0.578, 0.500, 0.567 and 0.511. The labels move telemetry by more than
they move outcome, and neither movement is large.

The largest lever in the corpus is an outcome flip of 5.0\% to 85.6\%
final training reward under a fixed label when the backend is changed.
That figure must be read with its provenance caveat attached, because
the same inspection that surfaced it also found that the nominal backend
comparison bundled a different base checkpoint: Qwen3-8B-Base on the
managed Tinker run against the instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL. The
number is therefore evidence that item 3 of the standard must record
backend \emph{and} checkpoint identity; it is not a causal estimate of
the backend alone. A second same-label lever points the other way: the
label ``DAPO'' produced mean ZVF 0.00 on an open trainer running true
dynamic sampling and 0.58 on a closed stack running an asymmetric-clip
surrogate.

These exhibits are summarised by a variance decomposition, and its
design has to be read with it. The algorithm term comes from the
four-method same-stack N2 run (GRPO, AERO, GIFT, AREAL; one seed; 40
steps each, so 4 groups of 40 per-step observations, n = 160, df = 3
between and 156 within): η² = 0.0454 on ZVF, with a bootstrap CI of
{[}0.0014, 0.0585{]}, and η² = 0.0075 {[}0.0014, 0.0577{]} on mean
reward. Because the steps are autocorrelated within a run, those 156
within-group degrees of freedom overstate the independent information.
No group-size term is reported from this decomposition: the group-size
file, \longtok{groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.tsv}, holds one row per G level (4
cells, one observation per cell, df = 0 within), on which any one-way
decomposition returns η² = 1 by construction (corrected; see Table E.1).
The 98-cell mega-campaign decomposition is an unbalanced partial grid
(98 of 120 cells over model family, task slice, G, temperature and seed,
with two seeds). Its four stack axes account for 72.9\% to 92.7\% of the
variance in the five logged metrics; the seed axis rounds to 0.000, so
no stack-to-seed ratio is quoted as an effect size (corrected; see Table
E.1).

The standard that P5 proposes has eight items --- seven run-manifest
fields and one evaluation item --- and each item earned its place by
exhibiting a documented lever of this kind. Item 1 is the loss form,
reported as a tuple of ratio level, clip low and high, token mask,
advantage normalisation and dynamic-sampling status --- the same label
with and without dynamic sampling produced opposite ZVF telemetry (0.00
against 0.58). Item 2 is the reference policy and KL handling, which
distinguishes family members that are otherwise conflated and whose
omission is unrecoverable after the fact. Item 3 is the sampler, backend
and precision, including base-checkpoint identity, motivated by the 17×
exhibit above. Item 4 is the per-step ZVF and gradient-utilisation
trajectory, because a single mean aliases opposite regimes: across a
368-run audit drawn from a 790-run corpus, mean ZVF is 0.97 and 0.95 for
two low-reward models (Llama-3.2-1B and Llama-3.2-3B), falls to 0.25 and
0.29 at mid-range rewards, and rises again to 0.87 and 0.96 for two
near-saturated models. Item 5 is the group-size schedule, since group
size is a signal-geometry knob rather than a throughput knob; measured
mean ZVF for Qwen3-32B falls from 0.33 at G=4 to 0.18 at G=8 and 0.08 at
G=16, and for Llama-3.1-8B from 0.76 to 0.65 to 0.47, while the two
boundary cases compress the effect as the theory predicts. Item 6
requires a held-out split disjoint from the reward environment. Item 7
requires a contamination check and a parser-robustness probe: adding
micro-jitter ε \textasciitilde{} U(0,10⁻⁴) to rewards collapsed batch
ZVF from 0.158 to 0.000 while pairwise contrast density moved by 3×10⁻⁷,
which establishes that a diagnostic sensitive to verifier rounding is
reporting a property of the stack rather than of the policy.

Item 8 is deliberately different in kind: held-out pass@k curves at k ∈
\{1, 8, 32\} belong to the evaluation report rather than to the run
manifest. The lever here is a matched base-versus-post-RL panel --- four
adapters at G ∈ \{2, 4, 16, 32\}, one stack, the same problems --- on
which pass@1 deltas scatter from −0.5 to +1.9 points (under which
reading the G=2 arm would be called a regression) while the pass@32
frontier improves by +1.5 to +2.5 points in all four arms. Neither
reading reaches significance at a single training seed, which is itself
the argument: without pass@k curves and stated noise bands the same
panel supports any narrative the reader prefers.

P5 also ships the machinery that makes the standard enforceable rather
than advisory. An auditor scores a manifest against the eight items and
emits a 0--100 weighted badge; on an n=103 audit the weighted total was
55.0 with a badge span of {[}35.8, 56.7{]} and standard deviation 3.1,
awarding zero gold, zero silver, 99 bronze and 4 wood. Coverage,
however, is where the negative result lies. On 98 manifests, every one
of the seven manifest fields is present as a key 100\% of the time, yet
item 2 is \emph{validated} in 0 of 98 cases (0.0\%), and all twelve of
its sub-fields are present at 0 in each case; nine of the sub-fields
across items 1 and 2 --- advantage normalisation, clip high, clip low,
dynamic sampling, KL coefficient, KL estimator, ratio level, reference
snapshot and token mask --- sit at 0.0\% with zero information content.
Twenty-three sub-fields examined across the population show no field at
a full null rate, but twenty of the twenty-three carry at most two
distinct values, and the twenty entries collapse to ten distinct 23-bit
fingerprints.

Two further analyses bound what the standard can be expected to buy. The
first is a discriminative-entropy audit: four of the seven manifest
fields are vacuous on this corpus --- loss form, reference policy and
KL, sampler/backend/precision, and decontamination notes each carry too
little task-discriminative information to distinguish runs --- while the
per-step ZVF path and the group-size schedule carry the information. A
field-sufficiency regression agrees: a random forest on the disclosed
stack fields, scored by out-of-fold R² over the 98 cells (8-fold
cross-validation, 8 fold seeds), predicts ZVF at R² = 0.832 {[}0.772,
0.879{]} and mean reward at R² = 0.993 {[}0.990, 0.996{]}. The
label-only comparator is R² = 0.000 by construction, because all 98
cells share one algorithm label, so it is a floor rather than a
competing model. The second is a manifest self-sufficiency test, and it
splits sharply by metric. The manifest carries essentially all of the
ZVF signal (R² = 0.776 {[}0.730, 0.846{]} against 0.773 {[}0.735,
0.834{]} for the cells, ΔR² = −0.003 {[}−0.045, +0.034{]}) but only a
minority of the reward signal (R² = 0.280 {[}0.219, 0.443{]} against
0.743 {[}0.688, 0.820{]}, ΔR² = +0.463 {[}+0.309, +0.564{]}); all 98
cells are therefore labelled \longtok{honest\_\allowbreak{}but\_\allowbreak{}vacuous} on the
three-axis triangulation.

The most useful negative in P5 is the placebo-replacement bound. The
four-of-seven vacuity pattern admits two readings: the schema chose the
wrong items, or any stack-descriptor schema would be vacuous on a
single-stack corpus. Projecting six candidate replacement items onto the
live corpus refutes the first reading. Four of the six --- KL
coefficient, clip range low, advantage normalisation and mini-batch size
--- project to exactly one unique value on the 98-cell corpus with a
bootstrap CI excluding any positive information gain, because the corpus
never varies them; temperature schedule would add 0.995 bits and lift
the total from 11.41 to 12.41 bits, and a model-family label is
redundant with an existing column. The variance partition confirms it:
stack axes explain η² = 0.947 of ZVF variance across 25 unique buckets,
with 5.3\% seed residual and an algorithm term of zero by construction.
The schema is not the binding constraint; the corpus is. On the live
corpus the fingerprint over the seven manifest fields carries 11.4 bits
in total, four fields carry exactly zero, and Hamming distance over the
fingerprint predicts the absolute difference in ZVF (Spearman ρ =
+0.529, p ≤ 10⁻³), PCD (ρ = +0.541, p ≤ 10⁻³) and mean reward (ρ =
+0.251, p = 0.003), while the badge itself is constant across cells and
cannot discriminate seed-pair dispersion (ρ = +0.000, n = 48 pairs). The
unit of these Hamming-distance correlations is the pair of cells. Pairs
that share a cell are not independent, so the naive p-values overstate
the evidence. A Mantel-type permutation test over cell labels would be
needed, and none was run, so the ρ values are reported descriptively and
their p-values are not relied on.

\section{The GRPO-Registry (P6)}\label{the-grpo-registry-p6}

P6's premise is that the deltas defining GRPO variants --- DAPO's
asymmetric clip, dynamic sampling and token-level loss; Dr.~GRPO's
dropped length and standard-deviation normalisation; GSPO's
sequence-level importance ratio \citep{zheng2025gspo} --- live in paper
appendices and framework defaults rather than in any machine-readable
form. The registry is the response, and it ships as three artifacts at
\texttt{registry/}: \texttt{schema.json}, \texttt{entries/*.json} and
\texttt{query.py}.

The schema defines two record types under a single \texttt{oneOf}, both
carrying \texttt{schema\_version}. The stack record's core is
\texttt{min\_report}, one sub-object per minimum-report item, with
base-checkpoint identity required inside the \texttt{sampler\_backend}
block precisely because the audit that motivated item 3 found a managed
backend silently pinning a different base checkpoint. Two design
decisions carry weight. The first is the distinction between an
unreported field and a field reported as absent: a null means the author
said nothing, whereas an explicit \texttt{false}, \texttt{0.0} or
\texttt{"none"} means the author made a checkable claim --- ``a stack
that says `we use no KL term' is making a checkable claim; a stack that
says nothing is not.'' The second is that item 8, the held-out pass@k
requirement, is attached under outcomes as an evaluation-report field
rather than folded into the run manifest, preserving the eight-item
standard's split between what describes a run and what describes a
result. Per-component delta status is recorded on the stack side in
\longtok{variant\_\allowbreak{}deltas\_\allowbreak{}applied}, with each entry's status drawn from
\{implemented, surrogate, absent, unknown\}. The shipped entry for the
managed DAPO run makes the point concretely: \texttt{clip\_higher} is
recorded as \texttt{surrogate} with the note that the asymmetric clip
was set through the user-facing config while the underlying managed loss
form is unverifiable, \texttt{dynamic\_sampling} is recorded as
\texttt{absent}, and \longtok{token\_\allowbreak{}level\_\allowbreak{}loss} is \texttt{unknown}
because it is managed by the runtime. An optional \texttt{ci\_method}
object (method, bootstrap count, seed, confidence level, source) is
populated on the seven derived entries with \texttt{n\_boot\ =\ 2000}
and a percentile 95\% interval, and left null on the remaining
twenty-four.

Three variant-delta records were verified against their source papers:
DAPO with five components (clip higher at ε\_low = 0.2 and ε\_high =
0.28, dynamic sampling, token-level loss, overlong reward shaping, KL
removed); Dr.~GRPO with two removals (the 1/\textbar o\_i\textbar{}
response-length normalisation in the loss and the division by the
within-group reward standard deviation in the advantage); and GSPO with
a length-normalised sequence-likelihood importance ratio and
sequence-level clipping.

The seed population is twenty stack records in five families, all of
which pass validation. Family A holds four framework config dumps
(Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, seed 42) scoring badges 74, 60, 60 and 62; family B
holds the four open Colab trainer arms at toy scale, scoring 96, 96, 96
and 96; family C is the managed head-to-head at 57, 62, 62 and 60;
family D is a same-stack four-method run at 67 each; and family E is a
five-seed risk-index batch at 43 each. The finding worth stating plainly
is why the ordering looks the way it does. The config dumps were
assembled for a reviewer-requested fairness audit and score only 57 to
74: they nail items 2, 3 and 5, expose item 1 only partially, and cover
items 6 and 7 not at all. The open Colab arms score 96 not because the
runs are better but because an open backward pass, coupled with explicit
declarations that no KL term and no decontamination check were used,
makes every field reportable. Reproducibility supplements of the kind
commonly shipped with RL papers do not automatically satisfy MIN-REPORT.

The health audit is the registry's own evidence that it works. Every
shipped record parses: 31 of 31 pass, comprising 20 stack records and 11
variant-delta records, with zero broken delta pointers. The delta-mix
table is the sharpest single result in P6: the label ``DAPO'' is claimed
by ten entries, of which four implement a delta, one records a
surrogate, three record it as absent and two record it as unknown ---
the same label covering four distinct implementations (Table
\texttt{tab:p6-delta-mix}). Per-item coverage on the twenty stack
records is 100\% for the held-out split (40/40), 95\% for
sampler/backend (76/80), 90\% for telemetry (54/60), 68\% for the
group-size schedule (41/60), 48\% for reference policy and KL (29/60),
32\% for the loss form (38/120) and 20\% for decontamination (8/40)
(Table \texttt{tab:p6-coverage}). The per-openness breakdown inverts the
conventional expectation: the open subset records sampler/backend at
91.7\% and telemetry at 83.3\% with reference KL at 58.3\%, while the
managed subset records sampler/backend and telemetry at 100\% but
reference KL at 33.3\% and the loss form at only 22.9\%.

The registry was stress-tested rather than asserted. A batch of
adversarial perturbations with B = 4000 bootstrap resamples --- 13
categories × 31 entries × 10 mutations = 4,030 generated, of which 800
were no-ops for their entry and were skipped, leaving 3,230 applied ---
moved the overall robustness statistic from 0.864 {[}0.756, 0.878{]}
before the corresponding schema bumps to 1.000 {[}1.000, 1.000{]} after
them, closing three silent-failure categories: a missing identifier
pattern check (0.645 to 1.000), MIN-REPORT leaves declared in
\texttt{properties} but not in \texttt{required} (0.000 to 1.000, fixed
by promoting key presence to a schema invariant), and free-form delta
references (0.000 to 1.000, fixed by replacing \texttt{delta\_id} with
an enum generated from the entry filenames). A separate consistency
check cross-references the variant-delta records against the MIN-REPORT
blocks: of 31 triples from 13 entries, 7 are MATCH, 0 are MISMATCH, 0
are MISSING\_REPORT, 5 are SURROGATE\_OBS and 19 are NOT\_APPLICABLE,
giving an implementation-honesty match rate of 7/7 = 1.000 with a Wilson
95\% CI of {[}0.65, 1.00{]} (a percentile bootstrap of 7/7 is degenerate
at {[}1, 1{]}).

The empirical payoff of the schema is the same-stack measurement.
Re-executing four GRPO-family methods (GRPO, AERO, GIFT, AREAL) on a
single identical stack --- managed sampler, G = 8, seed 0, 40 steps, 16
prompts per step --- with the full per-prompt reward tensor logged at
every step, produces reward deltas of magnitude ≤ 2 percentage points.
Two of the three show within-run intervals that exclude zero on the side
of a \emph{decrease} (AERO at −0.014 {[}−0.023, −0.005{]} and AREAL at
−0.020 {[}−0.032, −0.008{]}). Those intervals resample prompt-steps
within a single seed-0 run per method, so they carry no seed variance
and are not replication, and the three contrasts are uncorrected for
multiplicity. The magnitude thesis survives. Whether any difference
exceeds seed-to-seed noise was not tested, so neither the claim that all
differences are within noise nor its negation is established. The
largest same-stack effects are not on reward at all: AERO and AREAL
lengthen completions by approximately 31 tokens and tighten the length
coefficient of variation, and GIFT's loss shift of −1.67×10⁴ is a
baseline constant. A later registry extension measures the anti-herding
residual
\(\delta_{\mathrm{div}} = \mathrm{ZVF}^{\mathrm{iid}} - \mathrm{ZVF}^{\mathrm{obs}}\)
on the same tensors, reporting a stack-mean of 0.049 with a range of
{[}0.039, 0.053{]}; only GIFT shows a borderline-significant reduction
against GRPO (p = 0.066, CI {[}−0.021, +0.001{]}), which resolves a
synthesis range of {[}0.13, 0.23{]} downwards --- the anti-herding
diversity bonus is real but smaller than the qualitative synthesis had
guessed.

The reference CLI, \texttt{registry/query.py}, implements four verbs
using only the standard library: \texttt{list};
\texttt{query\ -\/-item\ reference\_kl} returning FULL, PARTIAL or
UNREPORTED; \texttt{badge}; and \texttt{stackdiff}, which maps a pair of
entries onto the R0--R5 ladder and reports the highest rung triggered
--- R0 identical manifests, R1 nuisance-only, R2 runtime differences, R3
loss-form or KL-handling differences, R4 differing variant-delta sets,
R5 unauditable because a differing item is null on at least one side.
The badge formula weights the seven items equally and is outcome-blind:
\(\mathrm{badge}(s) = \lfloor 100 \cdot \tfrac{1}{7} \sum_i c_i(s) \rfloor\).
\texttt{stackdiff} prints a warning at R4 and above that outcome
differences must not be attributed to the shared algorithm label, and
the P6 report is careful to describe the ladder as ``a risk scale, not
an effect-size scale.'' Applied to the shipped entries,
\texttt{stackdiff} flags the open-versus-closed ``DAPO'' pair as R5 from
manifests alone, and across the twenty seed entries the MIN-REPORT badge
spans 43--96. Two boundaries deserve restating. The registry reports
stated provenance and conflicts; it does not reconcile unresolved
exact-ID or model conflicts, nor does it validate nominal algorithm
comparisons. And the badge is coarse relative to the corpus --- twenty
entries collapse to ten distinct fingerprints, and twenty of
twenty-three sub-fields carry at most two values.

\section{From Diagnostic to Controller
(P7)}\label{from-diagnostic-to-controller-p7}

P7 asks a narrower question than P5 and P6. Given that the zero-variance
fraction is worth reporting, is it mechanistically predictable, and is
it ready to control rollout allocation? The P7 report's own framing is
that this does not overturn the descriptive restraint of the earlier
work --- it \emph{tests} ZVF's predictive value through falsifiable
predictions rather than assuming it --- and it flags at the outset that
the weakest link in the chain, the causal connection from within-group
contrast to gradient magnitude, rests on toy-scale directional evidence
only.

The theory is compact. Fix a prompt with latent per-rollout success
probability p and a group of G conditionally independent Bernoulli
rewards. Write \(h_G(p) = p^G + (1-p)^G\) for the probability that the
group is degenerate, and model the usable signal per prompt as
\(S(p,G) = p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\). The first factor is the contrast a
non-degenerate group can carry; the second is the probability that any
signal survives centring. Both factors vanish at the endpoints and peak
at p = ½, so the reading is that GRPO learns fastest exactly where the
policy is most uncertain and starves at both ends. Three consequences
follow, each paired with the measurement that could have falsified it.
T1 states that the expected ZVF indicator is exactly \(h_G(p)\). T2
states that \(h_G\) is strictly decreasing in G, with exponential decay
rate −ln max(p, 1−p). T3 states that with unnormalised group-centred
advantages the mean absolute advantage in a group is exactly 2p̂(1−p̂).

The three checks behave as the theory predicts, with the deviations
reported rather than smoothed over. For T1, binning the per-group reward
tensors of the GSM8K runs by empirical success rate gives a ZVF
indicator averaging 1.000 at p̂ ∈ \{0, 1\} and 0.000 at every interior
bin; at run level, measured ZVF sits \emph{above} \(h_G(\bar p)\) at
every G, which the P7 report attributes to the Jensen gap --- the
binomial model evaluates \(h_G\) at the mean \(p\) while the true prompt
population mixes mastered and hopeless prompts. The Jensen gap explains
only part of it. On the repository sweep the measured ZVF also sits
above the per-step-average null, which already absorbs the across-step
variation in p: 0.838 against 0.828 at G = 2, 0.764 against 0.709, 0.691
against 0.597 and 0.631 against 0.522 at G = 16, recomputed from
\longtok{groupsize\_\allowbreak{}zvf\_\allowbreak{}sweep.json}. This is the same baseline used in
Chapter 6 §6.3.3 and Chapter 4 §4.3. For T2, two independent grids
agree. The 368-run audit shows monotone decline for every model with an
interior success rate (Qwen3-32B 0.33 → 0.18 → 0.08; Llama-3.1-8B 0.76 →
0.65 → 0.47), with the two boundary rows behaving as the theory flags
them: Llama-3.2-1B sits near p ≈ 0 where \(h_G \approx 1\) regardless of
G (0.98 → 0.97), and Qwen3-4B-Instruct sits near saturation where the
decay rate is tiny and seed noise dominates, producing the single
non-monotone cell (0.89 → 0.84 → 0.91). The repository-internal sweep at
G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\} shows the same decline, with mean ZVF falling from
0.838 to 0.631 while held-out accuracy stays essentially flat near 0.98
throughout --- raising G buys signal, not, on this near-saturated task,
outcome. T3 cannot be checked on the closed stack at all, because it
does not expose per-group gradients; the open-backward-pass measurement,
corr(‖∇‖, p̂(1−p̂)) = +0.71 on a 0.5B model with synthetic arithmetic, is
reported strictly as directional evidence.

Because ZVF aliases mastery with incapacity, the P7 report promotes the
pairwise contrast density PCD = ((G−1)/G)·E{[}p(1−p){]} as the signal to
control on. The falsification that motivates the promotion is the
micro-jitter experiment already cited in §7.1: ZVF collapses from 0.158
to 0.000 while PCD is unmoved. The controller's design follows from the
theory: a \texttt{zvf-triage} callback watches per-step ZVF with PCD as
an aliasing guard, and an adaptive-G controller escalates group size on
ZVF spikes, changing only the sampler budget so that it composes with
any group-relative objective.

The four-arm open-trainer audit is the first place where the
intervention is measured against alternatives on the same data, budget
and seeds. Against a 120-rollout baseline, GRPO gains +0.500 held-out at
mean ZVF 0.25; Dr.~GRPO gains +0.575 at 0.27 on the same 120 rollouts;
DAPO-style dynamic sampling gains +0.550 while driving ZVF to 0.00 at
174 rollouts, a +45\% cost; and adaptive-G gains +0.575 while lowering
mean ZVF only modestly to 0.23 at 186 rollouts. Three readings are
stated. Telemetry behaves as the mechanisms dictate. Dynamic sampling
buys perfect telemetry at a 45\% rollout premium and does not exceed the
cheaper fixed recipe. Adaptive-G spends comparable overhead and ties the
best held-out result, concentrating its spend on flagged steps --- and
the tie is small-n and single-task, so the description is ``competitive
with, not superior to, the best fixed recipe.''

The controller simulation then asks a counterfactual question on the
real reward tensors: for each of the 2,560 prompt-step decisions in the
four-method run, when would each controller have fired, what group size
would it have chosen, and how much contrast would it have restored? The
answer is a negative result of some force. Across all four methods and
all five thresholds of the point-estimate rule, the controllers save
\emph{zero} prompts. Of GRPO's 640 prompt-step pairs, 461 are truly
degenerate --- 427 all-correct and 34 all-wrong --- with p̂ at exactly 0
or 1, and the counts are near-identical for the other methods (AERO 461,
GIFT 493, AREAL 452). At p̂ ∈ \{0, 1\} no group size recovers contrast,
because ZVF(G) = 1 for every G. The empirical ZVF of roughly 0.72 is
high because the prompts are easy, not because they are noisy at the
decision boundary. Bootstrapping the observed binary rewards to size G =
16 confirms it directly: 0 of 1,867 boundary prompts would have been
restored by escalation. The operating range where the trigger fires
selectively is τ ∈ {[}0.70, 0.80{]}, 12 to 19 fires per 40 steps at a
