cost ratio of 1.30 to 1.48, and the different thresholds differ only in
how wastefully they fire.

In the same trace the escalation asymmetry is stark: 1,723 of 1,867
escalation events (92.3\%) occur on all-correct groups and only 144 on
all-wrong groups. A symmetric ZVF trigger mostly spends on solved
prompts. The P7 report is explicit that deleting those fires is a
counterfactual proposal only, because performance preservation has not
been measured.

Two refinements sharpen the picture and then complicate it. The Bayesian
controller replaces the point estimate with a Beta(1,1) posterior and a
mid-range probability m(k, G) = Pr{[}0.05 ≤ p ≤ 0.95 \textbar{} k, G{]},
escalating when m exceeds a posterior threshold. At τ\_post = 0.60 it
fires on the same 466.75 degenerate prompt-steps as the point-estimate
rule at τ = 0.50 --- with a 95\% bootstrap CI of {[}454.25, 485.00{]}
--- but at strictly lower cost, 8,854 rollouts against 10,240, a 14\%
saving; and all 466.75 of those fires are on saturated prompts, so it
too saves zero prompts. Above τ\_post = 0.65 it never fires, because the
maximum m over degenerate observations is 0.630.

The calibrated eight-controller comparison then produces a genuine
metric reversal with bootstrap CIs attached. On the cost axis the Pareto
winner is Dualformer-Auto, an automatic difficulty-gated de-escalation
rule, at a mean cost ratio of 0.657 {[}0.599, 0.738{]} against 1.000 for
fixed G = 8 --- a 34\% rollout saving, achieved because the prompts it
shrinks to G = 2 would have been degenerate at G = 8 anyway. On a
different axis, contrast restored per 1,000 extra rollouts, the ordering
reverses: \texttt{zvf-triage} at τ = 0.50 restores 90.10 units per 1,000
extra rollouts {[}89.40, 90.77{]}, against 86.68 for τ = 0.70, 84.00 for
Dualformer-Auto and 80.00 for the Bayesian rule, and all four pairwise
differences have CIs excluding zero. The Bayesian controller ---
Pareto-dominant on the earlier axis --- is dominated here, because its
principled prior fires on the hardest-to-restore degenerate prompts. The
regime decomposition explains the mechanism rather than leaving it as a
ranking: \texttt{zvf-triage} at τ = 0.50 fires on 100\% of steps in
every regime on this panel, so it is operationally equivalent to
``escalate every step,'' and its apparent advantage is mechanical
exposure rather than a smarter rule. Its restoration decomposes 64.76\%
degenerate, 13.69\% boundary and 21.56\% mid-range; the non-degenerate
35.24\% share is what the Bayesian rule sacrifices.

Two further analyses bound what this family of shrink-only rules can
achieve. A per-prompt hindsight-optimal calculation scans G' down from a
base of 8 and finds a bimodal optimum: G* = 8 for 72.9\% of the 2,560
prompt-steps, which are fully saturated and admit no rescue by any group
size, and G* = 2 for the remaining 27.1\%, which are already
non-degenerate at G = 8 and are cheapest to shrink. Mean G* is 6.376,
giving a cost ratio of 0.797 {[}0.776, 0.818{]} --- a 20.3\% saving that
is pure economy, since mean contrast restored per prompt is 0.000 and
mean contrast lost to shrinking is 0.130 ZVF units. This hindsight
optimum is not a ceiling on cost (corrected; see Table E.1):
Dualformer-Auto's observed 0.66 to 0.68 on the four-bin rule is
\emph{cheaper}. The two optimise different objectives. The hindsight
calculation keeps every already-degenerate prompt at G* = 8 and only
shrinks non-degenerate ones, whereas Dualformer-Auto also shrinks
prompts that would have been degenerate at G = 8. The 0.797 is therefore
the best cost ratio under a no-shrinking-of-saturated-prompts
constraint, not a lower bound on cost for any adaptive rule. A
finite-pool correction then shows that even this bound is loose: because
a finite pool never collides more than independent sampling, the exact
hypergeometric contrast-preservation probability exceeds the binomial
one everywhere, by +0.0494 {[}+0.0481, +0.0506{]} on the 693 relevant
prompt-steps, which means the binomial model over-predicts starvation
and the ``zero contrast restored'' accounting understates true preserved
contrast by roughly 34.2 ZVF units.

Cross-base replication is where the retreat from ``controller'' to
``audit'' is earned. On a second panel of five independent seeds, the
trigger fires 4.20 times per seed {[}3.00, 5.40{]} at τ = 0.70, with the
per-step rate falling from 0.48 to 0.28 as the panel's overall ZVF
drops; what replicates is the sign and the operating-point logic, not a
fixed firing magnitude. The Bayesian branch is entirely silent on this
panel, firing 0.0 times per seed, because every step's implied mid-range
probability lies in {[}0.95, 0.999{]} --- a real negative finding that
scopes the Bayesian controller to distributions containing boundary
steps. And the aggregate firing rate is stable while the \emph{location}
is not: at τ = 0.70 with a de-escalation threshold of 0.95, the
cross-seed Jaccard overlap of the fire sets is 0.133, only 13.3\% of
steps see all five seeds choose the same group size, and the mean
cross-seed Kendall-τ of per-step ZVF trajectories is −0.060. The firing
rate is seed-stable; the firing location is not, and the training-step
index is a meaningless axis for the ZVF signal across seeds. The
replayed unification confirms the direction: on this panel
Dualformer-Auto is the only controller that saves compute (+14\%
{[}0.10, 0.18{]}) while \texttt{zvf-triage} spends more than baseline
(−28\% {[}−0.36, −0.20{]}), with disjoint CIs.

Five design rules close the P7 report, and they read as constraints
discovered rather than principles asserted. Gate on PCD and alarm on
ZVF: escalate only when ZVF is high \emph{and} PCD indicates interior
difficulty, because high ZVF with near-zero PCD and high reward is
mastery --- where \(h_G(p) \approx 1 - G\varepsilon\) near the boundary
and added rollouts buy order ε --- while high ZVF with near-zero reward
is incapacity, where the correct intervention is the data, not the
sampler. Escalate multiplicatively in small steps, since at p = ½ each
additional rollout halves the degenerate fraction; the audited 4 → 6 → 8
schedule at multipliers of 1.5 and 1.33 captured most of the achievable
reduction at a total cost of +55\% rollouts, whereas G = 16 costs four
times the rollouts of G = 4 for a ZVF drop from 0.76 to 0.63 and no
held-out gain on a near-saturated task. Trigger on spikes rather than
levels, because ZVF is temporally sticky with a lag-1 autocorrelation of
approximately 0.94 and drifts upward over training, so a level threshold
fires either never or permanently. Never compare controllers across
stacks; ship the seven manifest fields with item 5 carrying the
escalation log. And treat ZVF = 0 as a purchase rather than a prize ---
dynamic sampling shows perfect telemetry is always available at a 45\%
rollout premium, and on this task it was not the best use of the
marginal rollout.

The chapter's summary of P7 is the report's own. No result establishes
prospective superiority over static G = 16, Dr.~GRPO, or dynamic
sampling; adaptive group size is explicitly \emph{not} promoted to a
recommended policy; and all intervention evidence is single-task,
small-n, or retrospective. The report's contribution is a retrospective
controller audit together with a pre-registered prospective test plan,
and the bakeoff that would close the question --- a matched-budget,
multi-seed held-out comparison against the strong static baseline --- is
future work rather than a claim. In the same spirit, the report flags
its own August 2026 evidence boundary: unsigned ZVF alone cannot select
between retiring, distilling, retrying, resampling or warm-starting a
prompt, so any operator policy remains a prospective evaluation contract
rather than a demonstrated benefit.



\chapter{The E1--E14 Held-Out Evaluation
Campaign}\label{the-e1e14-held-out-evaluation-campaign}

This chapter reports the evaluation of a single Tinker-trained actor
against fourteen benchmark suites that were not part of its training
data, recorded lane by lane under sealed reservations, immutable
receipts and explicit terminal states. Lanes that did not finish and
lanes that could not start are reported too, because the methodological
claim of this work is not that the actor performs well but that every
figure attached to it can be traced to a surviving, hash-pinned
artifact, and that the boundary between what was measured and what was
not is drawn explicitly. Lane-by-lane detail that does not change any
figure is collected in Appendix D (Figure~\ref{fig:lanes}).


\fig{fig_lane_status}
{Terminal status of all fourteen E1--E14 lanes: the 2026-09-19 ledger of record, updated with the six replacement scopes scored on 2026-09-27 (double border, $\star$)}
{Terminal status of all fourteen E1--E14 lanes: the 2026-09-19 ledger of record, updated with the six replacement scopes scored on 2026-09-27 (double border, $\star$). Each cell names the suite whose scope the state refers to, and status is carried by glyph, word, border style and hatching as well as colour. Cells show the replacement scope where one exists (E1 SWE-bench Multilingual, E2 CORE-Bench, E5 Tau3, E6 WebArena, E8 LAB-Bench public, E9 MLDevBench, E10 AgentDojo benign, E13 BALROG, E14 Omni-MATH) and the original contract otherwise; the original-contract suites and their results (e.g.\ E1 SWE-bench Pro $2/731$, E2 FrontierSWE, E5 APEX-Agents, E9 MLE-bench) are shown in the campaign-design figure (Chapter~4) and are never pooled with replacement-scope numbers. *E14 closes terminal-complete: $2271/4428 = 51.29\%$ with the two unjudged rows counted as failures (official scorer: 51.31\% over 4426 judged rows).}
{fig:lanes}



Two reporting rules govern everything below, and they are worth stating
before any number appears. First, original-contract results and
replacement-scope results are never pooled and never averaged across
suites; a replacement scope is a different suite, a different interface
and often a different grader, and combining such figures would produce a
number with no referent. Second, no figure in this chapter is quoted to
a precision beyond what its source receipt records, and no comparison
against a baseline, a sibling suite, or the untrained base model is
asserted for the campaign lanes of Sections 8.2--8.6. Those lanes have
no matched base-model arm. The one paired baseline comparison in this
chapter is the small-scale trained-versus-base rerun of Section 8.7
(Table 8.B): 14 lanes, 5 to 151 identical items each, one vLLM engine
for both arms. It finds no significant difference in any lane, and this
chapter therefore claims no improvement of any kind over the base model.
Where a lane's record itself contains an unresolved discrepancy, the
discrepancy is stated.

The consolidated ledger of record for the campaign is
\longtok{outputs/E1\_\allowbreak{}E14\_\allowbreak{}FINAL\_\allowbreak{}RESULTS\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md}, which
supersedes the working tables of 2026-09-05 and 2026-09-12; the per-lane
terminal-state ledger is
\longtok{outputs/PES\_\allowbreak{}Phase2\_\allowbreak{}Review\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-12/finish/Pending\_Experiments.md};
and the arithmetic of the ledger was re-verified by deterministic code
checks recorded in
\longtok{outputs/verification/LEDGER\_\allowbreak{}CODE\_\allowbreak{}CHECK\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json},
which reports eleven of eleven checks passing with zero failures.

\section{The evaluated actor and the one-actor
design}\label{the-evaluated-actor-and-the-one-actor-design}

A single actor was evaluated across the whole campaign. The base model
is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at revision
\longtok{995ad96eacd98c81ed38be0c5b274b04031597b0}, and the trained
parameter set is the LoRA adapter
\longtok{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1-1f1b-5ff-9f777c4018b6}
at commit \longtok{64444133c55d88c3f1bf0df8a2f5d7ac646125c8}, served in
bfloat16 under the identifier \texttt{pavlov-public-portfolio-bf16}. The
Tinker training run behind the adapter has since been purged, so the
actor can no longer be sampled on Tinker; the adapter itself is
preserved on the Hugging Face branch
\longtok{checkpoint-seed809-stepfinal-9f777c4018b6} at the recorded
commit, and a check on 26 September 2026 confirmed the 2.25 GB weight
file is present there, so the actor can be re-served off-Tinker.

The one-actor design is deliberate. No lane received a separate
checkpoint, per-suite prompt engineering beyond its native harness, or
tuning against its own evaluation set: the actor's only training suites
were API-Bank-RLVR and SWE-Gym (§8.1.1), although the E10 receipt
records its decontamination inventory as absent
(\longtok{TRAINING\_\allowbreak{}INVENTORY\_\allowbreak{}ABSENT}). Differences between suites
therefore reflect the interaction of one fixed policy with fourteen
different interfaces, observation spaces, tool conventions and graders,
and they are not evidence about what a specialised actor could achieve.
The campaign also cannot support any statement about seed variance.
Inference conditions were frozen per lane and recorded in that lane's
own receipt (§8.1.2).

\subsection{How the actor was trained}\label{how-the-actor-was-trained}

The adapter was produced by a single Tinker training run (W\&B run
\texttt{bsv8vx04}), whose configuration survives in the local W\&B run
directory, the local run receipt and the Hugging Face checkpoint
branches; where these files live and how their hashes are verified is
recorded in Appendix D. Table 8.1 gives every field they record.

Four items cannot be recovered --- the exact training code, which 80
training prompts were drawn, the Tinker sampler weights, and any
held-out training-time evaluation --- and the run departs from the
planned Pavlov protocol; both are recorded in Appendix D so that no
reader fills them in.

What this means for the chapter is direct. The actor received 40 steps
of sparse-reward, critic-free policy-gradient training, with zero loss
on 35 of them. The fourteen suite results are properties of this
checkpoint, not evidence about what GRPO-style training contributes. The
campaign is reported as an exercise in evaluation governance. The only
trained-versus-base evidence is the small-sample paired comparison of
Table 8.B.

\subsection{Serving conditions per
lane}\label{serving-conditions-per-lane}

The E4 zero (§8.5) was traced to a serving bridge that sent no stop
sequences. The same bridge,
\longtok{zvf-program/flagship/modal\_\allowbreak{}tinker\_\allowbreak{}openai\_\allowbreak{}bridge.py}, served
several other lanes, so Table 8.2 records for every lane how the actor
was served and whether that defect could apply. Its
\texttt{/v1/responses} route hard-codes \texttt{stop=None} and thinking
on. Its \texttt{/v1/chat/completions} route sends stop sequences only if
the client supplies them, and the agent clients used here (opencode,
mini-swe-agent and the APEX client) do not. The deployed bridge version
(sha256 \texttt{f59c08b9…}) matches neither committed version of the
file.

Two consequences follow. First, the lanes served through the bridge (E2,
E4, E5, E7 and the E9 streaming arm) are exposed to the same defect that
produced the E4 zero, and none of their receipts records the stop,
thinking or token settings, so their figures may measure the harness as
much as the model. Second, E8, E11 and E14 are dominated by format and
length limits, so their headline figures are end-to-end harness scores;
§8.2 gives each with its parseable-only sensitivity figure. A note on
the scope of the stop-sequence mechanism is in Appendix D.

\section{Suites with strictly complete
scopes}\label{suites-with-strictly-complete-scopes}

Three suites reached strictly complete scopes, and the ledger's own
integrity check asserts exactly this set: E8, E10 and E11. E8 and E10
are replacement scopes. E11 is an original-contract full-suite result
that also meets the strict-completeness check, because the suite
evaluated is VerilogEval itself; the ledger lists it both among its
replacement scopes and among its original-contract results, but it is
one run and one result. A fourth, E14, closed terminal-complete under
its native protocol with a recorded note. The six replacement scopes
completed on 27 September postdate that check and are reported
separately in §8.4 (Figures~\ref{fig:e8} and~\ref{fig:e11}).


\fig{fig_e8_categories}
{LAB-Bench public split, per-category accuracy, all 1{,}967 questions graded across eight categories}
{LAB-Bench public split, per-category accuracy, all 1{,}967 questions graded across eight categories. Each bar is correct/total for one category (labels give the counts). Accuracy is strongly category-dependent, so no pooled cross-category average is drawn or claimed. CloningScenarios is a measured zero (0/33), not a missing value.}
{fig:e8}



\fig{fig_e11_decomposition}
{VerilogEval decomposition: two native framings of 156 tasks, 67/156 code-completion and 62/156 spec-to-RTL, summing to $129/312 = 41.35\%$ pass@1}
{VerilogEval decomposition: two native framings of 156 tasks, 67/156 code-completion and 62/156 spec-to-RTL, summing to $129/312 = 41.35\%$ pass@1. The dashed top bar is the sum of the two rows, not a third measurement. Graded by the suite's native harness (receipt \longtok{e11\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}receipt.json}); the $129/311 = 41.48\%$ value that excludes \longtok{Prob099\_\allowbreak{}m2014\_\allowbreak{}q6c} is a non-canonical sensitivity only.}
{fig:e11}




\textbf{E8 --- LAB-Bench, public split.} All 1,967 of 1,967 expected
items were evaluated across eight categories. The receipt records 450
correct and 1,517 incorrect responses, a published overall accuracy of
450/1967 = 0.2288 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.211, 0.248{]}, unit = question, n
= 1,967); per-category figures are in Appendix D. For 1,259 of the 1,967
responses the native pipeline extracted no answer
(\longtok{native\_\allowbreak{}answer\_\allowbreak{}parse\_\allowbreak{}none}), and those are counted as
incorrect, so the figure is an end-to-end harness score. As a
sensitivity figure, not the headline, accuracy conditional on a parsed
answer is 450/708 = 0.636 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.600, 0.670{]}). The
headline therefore mostly measures the 64\% of responses that yielded no
answer, with length truncation (1,086 finishes) the dominant failure,
and not domain knowledge.

\textbf{E10 --- AgentDojo, benign utility scope.} Over the frozen
AgentDojo v1.2.2 benchmark at commit
\longtok{089ed468cf3ed0322acc66b0211f26d9d90dbf60}, the terminal native
receipt records status \longtok{COMPLETE\_\allowbreak{}NATIVE\_\allowbreak{}BENIGN\_\allowbreak{}UTILITY}, all
97 episodes completed, 88 utility passes and a score of 88/97 = 0.9072
(Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.833, 0.950{]}, unit = episode, n = 97). Its claim
boundary is stated verbatim in the receipt as ``Native default benign
task utility only;no prompt-injection security or held-out claim'', and
its decontamination status is \longtok{TRAINING\_\allowbreak{}INVENTORY\_\allowbreak{}ABSENT}.
Completion and utility are distinct: the lane is complete in coverage
and 0.9072 in benign utility, never ``97/97 correct'', and it is not a
security result of any kind. An earlier attempt stopped at 94 of 97
episodes because of a collector fault, not a model failure; the fault
and its minimal repair are recorded in Appendix D.

\textbf{E11 --- VerilogEval, two native framings.} All 312 pinned
problems were evaluated --- 156 code-completion
(\texttt{code-complete-iccad2023}) and 156 specification-to-RTL ---
under the upstream NVlabs/verilog-eval harness. The result is 129 passes
of 312, or 0.4135 (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.360, 0.469{]}, unit = problem),
decomposed into 67/156 = 0.4295 ({[}0.354, 0.508{]}) on code-completion
and 62/156 = 0.3974 ({[}0.324, 0.476{]}) on specification-to-RTL. The
receipt records 150 extraction failures, which appear as FAIL verdicts
in the denominators rather than being dropped. 129/312 is therefore an
end-to-end harness score: 150 of the 183 failures are format failures.
As a sensitivity figure, not the headline, the pass rate among the 162
responses from which code was extracted is 129/162 = 0.796 (Wilson 95\%
CI {[}0.728, 0.851{]}). One problem,
\longtok{verilog\_eval/spec-to-rtl/Prob099\_\allowbreak{}m2014\_\allowbreak{}q6c}, fails against
its own test bench under both simulators; excluding it yields 129/311 =
0.4148, but no immutable justification for the exclusion was captured,
so the raw 129/312 is canonical and 129/311 is a noncanonical
sensitivity only. Harness revisions, the verdict rule and the amendment
are in Appendix D.

\textbf{E14 --- Omni-MATH replacement scope, terminal-complete.} The
scope accepted 4,426 of 4,428 rows and reproduced the official scorer's
output of 0.5131, which is 2,271/4,426: the scorer omits the two
unjudged rows from its denominator. Under the campaign evidence rule
those rows count as failures, so the reported accuracy is 2,271/4,428 =
51.29 per cent, a difference of 0.02 percentage points. For both rows
the Omni-Judge output is truncated before any verdict, so the official
scorer's omission is correct behaviour rather than a parser defect, and
no re-judge was performed.

\section{Original-contract results}\label{original-contract-results}

Seven lanes have an original-contract figure, reported separately from
any replacement scope with its coverage caveats; two are full-suite
results. Lane detail is in Appendix D.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{E1 --- SWE-bench Pro.} 2 of 731 tasks resolved, a pass@1 of
  2/731 = 0.00274, or 0.274 per cent (Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.08\%,
  0.99\%{]}, unit = task), over the complete 731-row test split of
  \texttt{ScaleAI/SWE-bench\_Pro}. The fourteen generation failures and
  four lost artifacts stay in the denominator.
\item
  \textbf{E2 --- FrontierSWE.} 1 of 17 tasks evaluated,
  replay-normalised score 0.8628; partial, because revision-bound
  authorisation for the remaining sixteen was never obtained.
\item
  \textbf{E4 --- BankerToolBench.} 1 of 100 tasks evaluated, recovery
  metric 0.3115 --- a verifier recovery metric on a single task, not the
  simple fraction 37/128 and not a suite score; the pass3/pass16 archive
  is missing.
\item
  \textbf{E5 --- APEX-Agents.} 7 of 480 tasks natively scored. The
  prefix mean of 0.050505 treats unscored attempts as zero and is not a
  native 480-task score; the seven scored tasks average 0.079365.
\item
  \textbf{E7 --- BinaryAudit.} One of 46 attempts recorded, verifier
  reward 0.0 after an agent error, no grade.
\item
  \textbf{E9 --- MLE-bench.} 40 of 75 competitions carry native grades,
  a coverage of 53.33 per cent, and the suite score is null; a separate
  merged-vLLM arm holds one valid grade (H\&M, 0.02132) and is not
  combined.
\item
  \textbf{E11 --- VerilogEval} is reported in §8.2; it is the second
  original-contract full-suite result, alongside E1, and not an
  additional one.
\end{itemize}

No lane here has a matched base-model run, so none licenses a claim that
the actor outperforms its untrained counterpart, a predecessor model, or
a published number.

\section{Replacement-scope lanes completed on 27 September
2026}\label{replacement-scope-lanes-completed-on-27-september-2026}

At the 19 September ledger of record, six replacement scopes were
started but incomplete: E1 (SWE-bench Multilingual, 35 of 300 graded),
E2 (CORE-Bench, 45 capsule setups, no grades), E5 (Tau3, 20 of 97
cleaned) and E13 (BALROG, 13 of 255 episodes) were launch-pending, and
E6 (WebArena, 0 of 812) and E9 (MLDevBench, 0 of 34) were
\texttt{PENDING\_QUOTA} because AWS granted one vCPU per region. All six
were run to completion on 27 September (Figure~\ref{fig:replacement}).


\fig{fig_replacement_results}
{The six replacement-scope results scored on 2026-09-27, each with its 95\% confidence interval}
{The six replacement-scope results scored on 2026-09-27, each with its 95\% confidence interval. (a) Rates over all attempted items, with errors, timeouts and ungraded items counted as failures (Wilson intervals). For E6 WebArena the filled point is the lower bound $90/812$; the square is the rate over the 704 judged tasks and the triangle the value if all 108 unjudged tasks had passed. (b) E13 BALROG reports native progression, not a success rate: per-environment means with percentile-bootstrap intervals ($B = 10{,}000$), and the BALROG overall score, the unweighted mean of the six environment means, with its normal-approximation interval. Each row is a different suite with its own metric and denominator, so no cross-lane average is drawn, and none of these values is pooled with an original-contract figure. Source: \longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/<lane>/result.json}.}
{fig:replacement}



The override was self-authorised.
\longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/AUTHORIZATION.json} records
its issuer as ``lead (repository owner arvindcr4), via Claude Code
session'', its directive verbatim as ``finish all pending lanes'',
recorded at 2026-09-27T00:41Z, with per-lane spending caps, and states
that it supersedes the lead-issued bound-authorisation receipt, the
reservation seals and the GCP owner-IAM binding. The candidate, as
project lead, therefore issued the directive that set aside gates he had
himself put in place; no independent party approved it. The
evaluation-governance claim of this thesis is scoped accordingly. It
does not mean that fail-closed gates were never overridden. It means
that every override and every deviation from the reference protocol is
recorded in the lane's own result file, named per lane (Table 8.D), and
never pooled: no replacement-scope figure is combined with an
original-contract one. All six lanes were served the same actor,
\texttt{pavlov-public-portfolio-bf16}, through vLLM with the
non-thinking chat template and not through the Tinker bridge of §8.1.2,
although the result files do not record stop settings.

The six figures are on six different benchmarks with six different
metrics, and they are not averaged, pooled with the same lane's
original-contract result, or compared with a base model. Five lanes (E2,
E5, E6, E9, E13) now have a native score over their full replacement
scope. E6's is a lower bound: 108 tasks needing an LLM judge could not
be graded, and the true figure lies between 0.111 and 0.244. E1 has two
separately reported scores, 1/190 and 4/110 (the earlier waves, served
at a different context), that together cover all 300 tasks but are not
combined. E5's pass\^{}1 over the 72 tasks without an infrastructure
error, 8/72 = 0.111, is a sensitivity figure only, and E13's 26.1 per
cent is not compared with the 26-episode small-scale E13 rows of Tables
8.A and 8.B. The lanes spent \$44.25 in total against caps of \$270, not
counting the shared actor endpoint. Per-lane narratives are in Appendix
D.

\section{The E4 rerun as a
diagnostic}\label{the-e4-rerun-as-a-diagnostic}

A fresh full BankerToolBench rerun, authorised separately after the lane
closed \texttt{CLOSED\_PARTIAL} on 19 September, ran the base model with
a zero-initialised LoRA snapshot through the Modal bridge
\texttt{/v1/responses} path. It completed 100 of 100 trials with one
\texttt{NonZeroAgentExitCodeError} and a mean reward of exactly 0.0.
Agents stopped after 2 to 14 steps without producing deliverables, and
twenty-seven or more trajectories ended in
\texttt{user\ user\ assistant\ ...} role-token degeneration. The
bridge's \texttt{/v1/responses} path passed no stop sequences, so
generation ran past the end-of-turn token into invented turns; with a
stop sequence set, the same base model scored 0.41 on one of six tasks
in the small-scale rerun of §8.7. The 0.0 is therefore attributed to the
serving bridge, not to the model's tool use or finance reasoning. It is
not set against the original single-task outcome, and no full-suite
BankerToolBench score exists for this actor family. Spend was \$15.07
against a \$55.91 cap. The full audit is in Appendix D (Figure~\ref{fig:e4}).


\fig{fig_e4_diagnostic}
{The E4 rerun diagnostic}
{The E4 rerun diagnostic. Across 100 trials agents terminate at a median of roughly six steps without producing deliverables, and more than a quarter end in role-token degeneration, consistent with generation running past the end-of-turn token because the serving bridge passed no stop sequences. The 0.0 is not a measurement of finance reasoning. Left: per-trajectory step counts (\texttt{final\_metrics.total\_steps}), stacked by whether the final agent message carries at least two concatenated role tokens (29/100; the ledger records ``27+''). Right: where the verifier chain stopped (\texttt{verifier/test-stdout.txt}, \texttt{reward.json}), all bars on one scale. Base-model rerun of 2026-09-21, all 100 trial directories under \longtok{outputs/e4\_\allowbreak{}banker\_\allowbreak{}toolbench/}.}
{fig:e4}



\section{Externally blocked lanes and terminal
states}\label{externally-blocked-lanes-and-terminal-states}

Six lanes have no local or paid path and were formally closed as
externally blocked under the finish-all directive of 19 September 2026,
whose verbatim text and interpretation are recorded in
\longtok{outputs/PES\_\allowbreak{}Phase2\_\allowbreak{}Review\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-12/finish/ROOT\_\allowbreak{}DIRECTIVE\_\allowbreak{}FINISH\_\allowbreak{}ALL\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json}
at 13:50Z. The six are E3 (SDAB private bundle), E7 (BinaryAudit private
payload), E8-original (the LifeSciBench package), E10-original
(AgentHarm private tasks), E12 (AppBench deployment) and E14-original
(the FrontierMath hosted evaluation). Closure records were written for
each, with an index, under
\longtok{outputs/PES\_\allowbreak{}Phase2\_\allowbreak{}Review\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-12/finish/external\_\allowbreak{}closures\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19/};
each records ``no provider response'' and specifies the conditions under
which the lane could be reopened. A seventh original contract, E13's
OpenReward held-out games, is also blocked on external access and is
listed with the six in the ledger, but it has no closure record because
the lane continues through its BALROG replacement scope (§8.4). Access
requests, the deterministic ledger checks and the role of the
model-based triage harness are recorded in Appendix D (Figure~\ref{fig:taxonomy}).


\fig{fig_terminal_taxonomy}
{Terminal-state taxonomy used to close every lane}
{Terminal-state taxonomy used to close every lane. Each state has a defined meaning and a named reopen condition where one exists. The seven states are the ledger's full vocabulary; 17 lane-states cover 14 lanes because E8, E10 and E14 carry separate original and replacement rows. States classify lanes, not results: an unrecognised state is recorded as blocked rather than absorbed into a percentage, and original-contract and replacement-scope numbers are never pooled. The completion gate's \texttt{PARTIAL\_EXACT}/\texttt{PARTIAL\_RECOVERY} labels are a separate evidence-class vocabulary (Chapter~5). \section{Small-scale reruns of all fourteen
lanes}\label{small-scale-reruns-of-all-fourteen-lanes}

This section adds a separate, smaller set of runs, made on 26 September
2026, to give every one of the fourteen lanes a measured number under
one fixed protocol, including the lanes that never produced a score.
None of these figures is pooled with, averaged against, or substituted
for a result reported earlier in this chapter.

The protocol fixes one actor family, a fixed item-selection seed
(20260926), temperature 0 and a non-thinking chat template, and scores
each lane with its native grader where one could be run. Lanes whose
original benchmark is externally blocked were run on a public
substitute, and each substitute's gap from the original suite is
recorded in its lane file. Items that errored or timed out are counted
as failures in the denominator.

Two arms were run. The first is the base model,
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} with no adapter, sampled on Tinker (Table
8.A). The second is a paired comparison of the trained actor against the
same base model, both served by one vLLM configuration on Modal, on
identical items (Table 8.B). The pairing exists because the adapter's
effect on individual token probabilities was measured to be about the
same size as the numerical difference between the two serving engines on
the same base weights, so a trained-on-vLLM against base-on-Tinker
comparison would confound the adapter with the engine, and none is made.

These are small samples. Most lanes score between five and eighty items,
so a single item moves a lane's rate by several percentage points and
the confidence intervals are wide. In Table 8.B, b and c count items
solved only by the trained actor and only by the base model
respectively, and the CI is a paired bootstrap interval on the
difference. Four lane-specific caveats matter. For E12 the 151 rubric
items come from only six generated applications, so its McNemar p-value
overstates the evidence; the exact sign-flip test over the six per-app
differences gives p = 0.25, and the grader is an LLM judge that is the
base model itself, so the E12 rates are judge-dependent. For E4, only
\texttt{btb-19b3361c} scores above zero in either arm (trained 0.397,
base 0.557; 0.409 in Table 8.A), so no interval is valid. For E13, the
1.875 difference is the mean of per-environment differences computed
from unrounded values (recomputed 1.876), not 25.510 − 23.630. For E9,
the one competition that earned a medal in the Tinker run (nomad2018)
has a 46.8k-token prompt that exceeds the 32,768-token vLLM limit and
fails identically in both vLLM arms, so the vLLM medal rates are not
comparable with Table 8.A. The paired differences are not combined
across lanes, and none of the seventeen paired tests excludes zero. At
these sizes that is an inconclusive result, not evidence of no effect:
with 5 to 10 items, an exact McNemar test needs at least six discordant
items, all in one direction, to reach p \textless{} 0.05. No equivalence
test was run. Fuller E12 detail and the principal per-lane caveats from
each result file are in Appendix D.

\section{Summary of the campaign at the point of
writing}\label{summary-of-the-campaign-at-the-point-of-writing}

By the 19 September ledger the campaign had established two full-suite
results and one complete replacement scope with a score: E1 at 2/731 =
0.274 per cent on SWE-bench Pro, E11 at 129/312 = 41.35 per cent pass@1
(Wilson 95\% CI {[}36.0, 46.9{]}) across its two framings, and E8 at
450/1967 = 0.2288 ({[}0.211, 0.248{]}) on the LAB-Bench public split,
alongside a complete benign-utility evaluation of E10 at 88 of 97
episodes (0.907, {[}0.833, 0.950{]}), a terminal-complete E14
replacement scope at 2,271/4,428 = 51.29 per cent accuracy, and five
original-contract partials (E2, E4, E5, E7, E9), none of which is a
suite score. On 27 September six further replacement scopes were
completed under a self-issued override whose deviations are recorded per
lane (Tables 8.C and 8.D). Seven original contracts remain externally
blocked, and no lane now waits on a launch or a cloud quota. The
campaign did not establish a multi-seed estimate, a held-out security
result, a BankerToolBench suite score, a graded result for the 108
WebArena tasks that need an LLM judge, or a baseline comparison for the
campaign lanes themselves; the only trained-versus-base comparison,
Table 8.B, is inconclusive in every lane. The evidence chain and
reproduction path for each number are given in Appendix B; the next
chapter draws the cross-study synthesis and conclusions.



\part{Synthesis}


\chapter{Cross-Study Synthesis, Threats to Validity, Conclusions and
Future
Work}\label{cross-study-synthesis-threats-to-validity-conclusions-and-future-work}

\section{Cross-Study Synthesis}\label{cross-study-synthesis}

This report has presented two bodies of work built with different
instruments. The first is the P1--P8 corpus, which probes GRPO-style
post-training across libraries, model scales, group sizes and datasets
and ends in a reporting standard, a machine-readable registry and a
controller audit. The second is the E1--E14 campaign, a governance-bound
evaluation of one Tinker-trained actor against fourteen agentic and
reasoning suites that were not part of its training data. They are the
same methodological commitment applied twice --- once to \emph{what does
GRPO do}, once to \emph{what does a GRPO-trained actor score}. That
commitment is attribution: every comparative claim holds the entire
training stack fixed except the one factor under test, and where that
was unachievable the confound is reported alongside the number rather
than absorbed.

The corpus's own synthesis, recorded at the close of Phase 1, is that on
this compute scale the \emph{measurement} layer is useful while most
\emph{algorithmic} levers are noise-limited. The dominant measured
phenomenon is advantage-signal starvation, with the Zero-Variance
Fraction at approximately 0.72--0.77 on the Phase-1 main configuration,
and it is easy-driven: the collapsed groups are mostly prompts the model
has already solved. That rules out the most tempting remedy, because
re-baselining the advantages merely converts the signal into a
difficulty proxy with no learning content, and the remedy that remains
--- a curriculum that removes the wasted rollouts --- returned a null.
The narrow systems observation survived better than any algorithmic one:
per-step telemetry separated the available method traces more cleanly
than reward-only features, though the classifier's integrity
generalisation was never tested.

The Semester 4 internal reports sharpen those threads rather than
reversing them. P2's diagnostics are recomputable from stored raw
tensors rather than read back from logged summaries: three stored GSM8K
group tensors reproduce ZVF at 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155, pooling to
0.1583, and the correlation between ZVF and final held-out outcome, ρ =
0.27, is not significant (95\% CI {[}−0.37, 0.88{]}, n = 23 pooled
cells). ZVF is therefore offered as a diagnostic and not a predictor.
Once mean reward is regressed out, the pooled measured residual is r =
+0.21 (95\% CI {[}−0.31, +0.62{]}, n = 17), so ZVF is not an incremental
predictor either (Chapter 6 §6.3.4). P4's matched comparison of GRPO
against Dr.~GRPO finds the two held-out gains indistinguishable, with
pooled mean completion length drifting from 194.4 to 183.5 tokens under
a 200-token cap that bounds what the comparison can say. P4's companion
error-bar audit grades the single P4-facing headline as DECISIVE: audit
H7, the iter-136 arithmetic late-training-efficiency contrast, paired n
= 5 seeds, \(d_z = +2.68\). Re-examined, it is \emph{suggestive} only
(Chapter 6 §6.5.4). Its ``one-sided parametric p = 0.031'' is the floor
of an exact one-sided Wilcoxon test at n = 5. The two-sided exact
sign-flip p is 0.0625, even though all five pairs point the same way.
The direction was one of eight hypotheses in its family, and Holm
correction across those eight raises the family's p to 0.25. The audit's
other three DECISIVE grades do not survive either. Two (H1, H2) rest on
an interval that excludes its own point estimate and on four
reconstructed budgets (§6.4.2; Table E.1), and one (H6) is an AUROC
computed on a synthetic and imputed panel (§6.3.4). Of the seven audited
headlines, then, none is decisive, one is suggestive and the rest are
null or hypothesis-generating. What survives is a consistent-direction
late-training efficiency contrast on a training metric, not a capability
claim.

P3 and P7 both treat group size as a lever and are best read as a pair,
because they disagree in emphasis. P3 finds that under saturation the
retention ratio between G = 2 and G = 16 is approximately 1.00. That
ratio is of last-10 training reward, and a seed-level TOST (n = 3,
margin ±0.02) does not establish G = 16 as equivalent to G = 2 (p =
0.056; Chapter 6 §6.4.1). P3 also finds that the effective
signal-to-noise ratio reaches only about 52\% of the √G ideal, and that
a 505-task conditional utility audit selects G = 4 over G = 5 by 0.00177
with a 95\% bootstrap interval of {[}0.00082, 0.00270{]} --- positive,
small, and explicitly cohort- and objective-conditional. P7 models
usable signal per prompt as \(S = p(1-p)(1-h_G(p))\) with
\(h_G(p) = p^G + (1-p)^G\), derives three falsifiable consequences, and
checks them: a 368-run Weights \& Biases audit across seven models
reproduces the predicted U-shape in accuracy, with ZVF 0.95--0.97 at the
low-reward extreme and 0.87--0.96 at the high-reward extreme and
0.25--0.29 at mid-range rewards of 0.35--0.50; a model × G grid confirms
the monotone effect at interior accuracy, Qwen3-32B moving 0.33 → 0.18 →
0.08 across G = 4/8/16; and a toy-scale open-backward-pass experiment
finds corr(‖∇‖, p(1−p)) = +0.71 on a 0.5B model, which the P7 report
labels directional only. Where P3's sweep could not find an optimum, P7
supplies a mechanism under which a shallow one should exist. That is a
reconciliation, not a resolution: the mechanism is checked in telemetry,
and its causal link to the gradient remains toy-scale.

The same report is clear about the control it does not have. A
micro-jitter falsification collapses batch ZVF from 0.158 to 0.000 while
leaving the pairwise-contrast density unchanged, which is why PCD rather
than ZVF is promoted as the control signal. In a four-arm open-trainer
audit, adaptive group size matches the best fixed-recipe held-out gain,
+0.575, at 186 rollouts, while DAPO-style dynamic sampling drives ZVF to
0.00 at +45\% rollout cost --- an accuracy-versus-compute trade, not a
controller win. In the larger 2,560-observation controller trace, 1,723
of 1,867 escalation events, 92.3\%, occur on all-correct groups and only
144 on all-wrong, so a symmetric ZVF trigger spends most of its budget
on prompts already solved; deleting those fires is a counterfactual
only, because performance preservation was not measured. Adaptive group
size is therefore not promoted to a recommended policy.

P5 and P6 supply the corpus's methodological claim, and they contain its
sharpest internal tension. P5's headline exhibits are a nominally
matched-configuration backend audit whose final training rewards span
5.0\% to 85.6\% --- a 17× same-label swing the P5 report declines to
assign to the backend, because the managed stack also silently pinned a
different base checkpoint (Qwen3-8B-Base on Tinker against the
instruction-tuned Qwen3-8B on TRL), and that undisclosed bundling is
itself part of the exhibit --- and a pair of runs carrying the same
``DAPO'' label in which an open trainer with true dynamic sampling
produced mean ZVF 0.00 while a closed stack running an asymmetric-clip
surrogate produced mean ZVF 0.58. Alongside them sits a third exhibit: a
12-cell head-to-head of four labelled algorithms on one fixed stack,
landing within a 0.034 band with mean last-10 reward 0.710--0.744, a
spread the P5 report calls comparable to seed-level variation. P5's
framing is that a label-only comparison can hide stack-level variation,
and that the converse also holds: when the stack is genuinely fixed, the
labels stop separating. P6 reads the same exhibit in the reverse
direction, using it to motivate a schema while explicitly declining to
re-claim it as novel empirical science; on the twenty seed entries the
MIN-REPORT auditor badge spans 43--96, and \emph{stackdiff} flags the
open-versus-closed ``DAPO'' pair as an R5 label-flip risk from manifests
alone.

Two gaps should be named. The first is that P7's controller audit and
P5's stack-attribution programme have not been joined: the controller is
evaluated on open trainers where the update rule is auditable, while the
standard is motivated by a closed-stack divergence that cannot be
audited. The second is P8, the credit-card fraud side-probe, absorbed
here as a measurement-discipline case study and nothing more. Its two
AUCs are not a like-for-like ranking: XGBoost's 0.7955 and the
Qwen3.5-4B SFT arm's 0.48268 on a positive-enriched 500-row held-out
evaluation sit on different held-out splits by construction.

The E1--E14 campaign is a different instrument and should be read as
one. A single Tinker-trained actor --- base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} at commit \texttt{995ad96e…}, with adapter
\longtok{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1…}
at commit \texttt{64444133…}, in bfloat16 --- was evaluated against
fourteen suites that were not part of its training data, each through
its own native evaluator at a pinned revision. Three scopes closed
strictly complete: two replacement scopes, LAB-Bench public at
1,967/1,967 across eight categories and AgentDojo benign utility with
97/97 episodes completed and utility 88/97 = 0.907 (Wilson 95\% CI
{[}0.833, 0.950{]}), and one original-contract suite, VerilogEval, at
312/312 with 129/312 = 41.35\% pass@1 canonical (Wilson 95\% CI
{[}36.0\%, 46.9\%{]}). Omni-MATH closed terminal-complete under its
native protocol, with all 4,428 dispositions recorded, 4,426 accepted,
and an accuracy of 2,271/4,428 = 51.29\% with the two unjudged rows
counted as failures (the official scorer reproduces 51.31\% over the
4,426 judged rows). Two suites carry full-coverage scores under their
original contracts: SWE-bench Pro at 2/731 = 0.274\% pass@1 (Wilson 95\%
CI {[}0.08\%, 0.99\%{]}) and VerilogEval, the same E11 result counted
once. Six further replacement scopes were run in full on 27 September
2026 (Table 8.C): CORE-Bench hard at 27/45 = 0.600, Tau3 at 8/97 =
0.082, WebArena at 90/812 = 0.111 (a lower bound, with 108
judge-dependent tasks ungraded), ML-Dev-Bench at 10/34 = 0.294, BALROG
at 26.1\% progression over 255 episodes, and SWE-bench Multilingual at
1/190 on the tasks not attempted before, kept separate from the earlier
4/110. Lane scope and status are tabulated once, in Table C.3. The rest
are partial or externally blocked, each with a terminal state and a
named gate.

What the campaign establishes is descriptive, and its scope matters. For
\emph{this} actor, at \emph{this} revision, under \emph{each suite's
own} evaluator and denominator, these are the scores. It is not a
post-training delta: no per-suite base-model comparator appears in the
campaign ledger at the campaign's own scopes, so no campaign number,
high or low, is evidence about what the GRPO step contributed. The one
paired comparison of trained actor and base model is the 26 September
small-scale rerun (Table 8.B): 14 lanes, 5 to 151 identical items each,
one serving engine. It finds no significant difference in any lane,
which at those sizes is inconclusive rather than evidence of no effect.
The separately authorised E4 BankerToolBench base-model rerun returned a
mean reward of 0.0 across 100/100 completed trials. That zero is
attributed to the serving bridge, which passed no stop sequences, so
agents ran into invented turns and ended after 2--14 steps with no
\texttt{deliverables/} output (Section 8.5; source:
outputs/e1\_\allowbreak{}e14\_\allowbreak{}small\_\allowbreak{}scale\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-26/E4/BRIDGE\_\allowbreak{}STOP\_\allowbreak{}TOKEN\_\allowbreak{}FINDING.md).
It measures the harness, not the model.

What governance bought is visible in the ledger itself: eleven of eleven
deterministic checks passing on 2026-09-19, and a historical snapshot
recording E7 as ``1/28 errored'' sitting visibly beside the superseding
table's ``1/46 attempted'' rather than being quietly reconciled.

\section{Rigour and Threats to
Validity}\label{rigour-and-threats-to-validity}

\textbf{What rigour was exercised.} Diagnostics are recomputable from
stored raw tensors, and headline structural claims were re-verified by
deterministic code checks on 2026-09-19, 11/11 PASS, covering exact
divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact hash equality and
lost-source absence probes. Held-out \emph{n} is reported alongside
every gain, and failed runs are counted rather than hidden:
approximately \$30--35 of Tinker spend went to failed runs, disclosed
because concealing it would understate reproduction cost by roughly
25\% (Figure~\ref{fig:threats}).


\fig{fig_threats}
{Threats to validity grouped as internal, construct and external, each with the mitigation applied in this work}
{Threats to validity grouped as internal, construct and external, each with the mitigation applied in this work. The pairing is mostly a reduction in what is claimed rather than a repair of the evidence: where the evidence is partial the claim is narrowed to what it supports. $\dagger$ marks figures and summary statistics drawn from Tinker data only; independent replication of them requires Tinker API access (\longtok{LIMITATIONS\_\allowbreak{}AND\_\allowbreak{}IMPACT.md}, Sec.~6.3). In T3, 37/128 is the number of verifier criteria met on the single E4 task; the reported 0.3115 is the verifier's own recovery metric, not that fraction.}
{fig:threats}



\textbf{Statistical power: single-seed Tinker experiments.} Most Tinker
configurations ran once, because cost precluded repetition, and those
single-seed runs carry no variance estimates or significance tests. The
exceptions are stated where they occur. They are the three-seed-per-arm
managed head-to-head (Chapter 7 §7.1), the five-seed Qwen3-8B held-out
control (p = 0.26, Chapter 6 §6.5.4) and the Qwen3-8B scale extension.
Henderson et al.~(2018) recommend at least ten seeds, and no result
family reported here reaches that bar. The seed counts are: one seed for
most Tinker runs and all P1 anchors; three for the P3 sweep, the P4
GSM8K-CoT and scale-extension arms and the managed head-to-head; five
for the same-stack PPO/GRPO contrast, the P4 arithmetic arms and the
Qwen3-8B pre/post control; and eight for the variant audit (P11). The
limitations document says the ten-seed bar was met for the TRL baseline
and the GSM8K evaluation and then that the evaluation was cut to five
seeds; the run records in Appendix A show five seeds for both the TRL
Modal campaign (Table A.3) and the GSM8K pre/post evaluation. The 30B
MoE scaling analysis uses three seeds; the bootstrap confidence
intervals assume independent observations, so any positive correlation
from shared initialisation or data ordering would make the reported
intervals too narrow, and the true intervals wider; and secondary
analyses carry uncorrected p-values. Quantitative conclusions therefore
come from the open-source side, where every hyperparameter is auditable,
while Tinker figures are descriptive.

\textbf{Horizons of 30--50 steps.} The Tinker runs are early-training
snapshots; long-horizon effects such as reward hacking, catastrophic
forgetting and late-stage policy collapse are unlikely to manifest, and
the source conclusion is direct: ``Asymptotic claims are unsupported''.
This bounds results rather than vitiating them: P1's failure to recover
a scaling law across roughly 2.4 orders of magnitude is a bounded
negative, and P4's drift from 194.4 to 183.5 mean completion tokens is
bounded by the 200-token cap.

\textbf{Closed Tinker internals.} The Tinker SDK exposes a custom loss
path, a limited optimiser surface and standard LoRA hyperparameters, but
not the exact server-side GRPO loss formulation, the reward
normalisation or baseline-subtraction scheme, minibatch or
gradient-accumulation strategy, hardware configuration, or system-level
telemetry. This applies unevenly: the custom-loss Tinker runners,
including the one that trained the campaign actor, define their loss in
client code (REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline, Table 4.2), whereas
the runners that use Tinker's built-in \texttt{importance\_sampling}
loss leave its server-side details unrecorded. The consequence is best
quoted: Tinker results measure ``the \textbf{platform's} implementation
of GRPO, not an abstract algorithmic specification''. The illustrative
case in the limitations document compares a pooled last-10 training
reward of 99.9\% for Tinker GRPO with 73.4\% for TRL GRPO on GSM8K.
Those figures are training rewards, not held-out accuracy, and they pool
Tinker runs on 4B--235B models against TRL on Qwen2.5-0.5B, so model
size and implementation are confounded; the source itself attributes
part of the gap to the model difference. The only same-model pair,
Qwen3-8B, used the Base checkpoint on Tinker and the Instruct checkpoint
on TRL (Table A.2), so it does not remove the confound either. The
threat propagates into the campaign, whose actor was trained on Tinker.
The SDK is also a moving target: Phase 1 pinned \texttt{tinker==0.16.1},
while the September 2026 bridge required \texttt{tinker==0.30.0} after
the older SDK produced a no-longer-supported error.

\textbf{Per-suite native denominators are not comparable.} Every E1--E14
result is expressed on its own suite's denominator, and those
denominators differ in size, in what they count and in what they
exclude. SWE-bench Pro is 2/731, whose denominator retains 14 generation
failures and 4 artifact losses alongside 713 native evaluations;
APEX-Agents is 7/480; E9 MLE-bench is 40/75 competitions natively
graded, stated as 53.33\% coverage with a null suite score, because
coverage and score are different quantities; E1's replacement SWE-bench
Multilingual is 1/190 on one contract and 4/110 on another, never summed
to a 300-task figure; E6 WebArena is 90/812 with 108 tasks ungraded, so
its denominator counts items that were never graded; E13 BALROG is a
mean of six per-environment means rather than a rate over episodes; and
E14's Omni-MATH records 4,428 dispositions of which 4,426 were accepted,
with the headline accuracy computed over all 4,428 (the official
scorer's 51.31\% uses the 4,426 judged rows). A 41.35\% on one
denominator and a 0.274\% on another do not license a comparison, an
average or a ranking, and none is offered; the rule holds within lanes
too, and the ledger states it flatly: ``N/A means unverified; no pooled
results''.

\textbf{No cross-suite aggregate.} No mean, median or composite index
over suites appears anywhere in this report or in the campaign materials
it draws on: an aggregate over native denominators would be a number
with no referent, mixing suites that measure different capabilities at
different difficulties under different scoring rules. The cost is that
the campaign has no single headline; the three strictly complete scopes,
the terminal-complete Omni-MATH scope, the six replacement scopes
completed on 27 September and the two original-contract scores are the
most the record supports.

\textbf{The E10 benign-utility scope says nothing about adversarial
robustness.} The E10 lane's completed evidence is a benign-utility
evaluation of 97/97 completed tasks with utility 88/97 = 0.907 under
AgentDojo's benign scope; its original contract, private AgentHarm
tasks, is externally blocked and carries a closure record with a named
reopen condition. The completeness result therefore carries no
adversarial-robustness measurement, and this report makes no such claim
for the trained actor. The lane has a dual-use dimension, though the
limitations analysis finds the marginal uplift of the released artefacts
small, because the base model's capability is overwhelmingly
pre-existing: the pre-RL Qwen3-8B-Instruct checkpoint already scores
82.0\% on held-out GSM8K, and full GRPO adds only +1.3 points to 83.3\%,
not significant at p = 0.26. The 82.0\% is the same Instruct checkpoint
before RL, not a separate base model.

\textbf{The retained E11 result came through the original sampler.} The
receipt of the 129/312 full run records
\texttt{model.sampler\_\allowbreak{}path\ =\allowbreak{}\ tinker:\allowbreak{}/\allowbreak{}/\allowbreak{}cf0ad8c1-\allowbreak{}1f1b-\allowbreak{}5ff3-\allowbreak{}8bd7-\allowbreak{}2a0bf232657b:\allowbreak{}train:\allowbreak{}0/\allowbreak{}sampler\_\allowbreak{}weights/\allowbreak{}seed809\_\allowbreak{}final},
so the scores come from sampling the trained adapter through the Tinker
managed sampler rather than from a locally served copy of the weights.
That route no longer exists: the sampler status record of 2026-09-20
reports a bridge deployment booting to a sampler-weights 404,
\texttt{list\_checkpoints} on the \texttt{cf0ad8c1} run returning 404
with the run purged, no \longtok{last\_\allowbreak{}sampler\_\allowbreak{}checkpoint} on any of
twenty training runs, and the status string
\longtok{TINKER\_\allowbreak{}SAMPLER\_\allowbreak{}WEIGHTS\_\allowbreak{}DELETED\_\allowbreak{}E9\_\allowbreak{}AND\_\allowbreak{}BRIDGE\_\allowbreak{}AFFECTED};
the recorded recovery path is to serve the HuggingFace mirror
\longtok{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal} at commit
\texttt{64444133…} through vLLM on Modal. The threat is not that the
number is wrong --- it is receipt-bound, provenance recorded --- but
that the sampling path is not re-runnable, so a re-run would be a
same-weights, different-sampler comparison whose divergence is
unmeasured. The full receipt's \texttt{outstanding\_blockers} field is
empty. The blocker that the decontamination receipt ID does not exist
and that the split-manifest validator still fails belongs to the
separate four-task step-40 pilot, whose receipt records the
\longtok{step\_\allowbreak{}seed809\_\allowbreak{}40} sampler path (Chapter 8 §8.1; source:
outputs/e11\_\allowbreak{}verilog\_\allowbreak{}eval/e11\_\allowbreak{}trained\_\allowbreak{}step40\_\allowbreak{}receipt.json).

\textbf{Known evidence loss in the finish-era recovery source.} The
record is explicit: the \texttt{.codex-run} deletion recorded in
\longtok{finish/STATE\_\allowbreak{}RECONCILIATION\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.md} removed the
execution sources of the finish-era recovery and build code --- the E1
wave-10 controller, the E9 restored dependencies and the E6 validator
--- while sealed requests, validation receipts and test logs survive
with matching hashes, and the replacement lanes' native runners survive
under \texttt{zvf-program/flagship/}. The lost-source policy was applied
rather than waived: a faithful re-implementation was written into a
tracked path under \texttt{zvf-program/e1\_wave10/}, where it verifies
10/10 targeted regressions offline (\longtok{test\_\allowbreak{}process\_\allowbreak{}group.py}
4/4, \longtok{test\_\allowbreak{}cleanup\_\allowbreak{}regressions.py} 6/6). Two consequences
follow: a sealed request without its execution source is not a rerun, so
the E9 MLDevBench runtime recipe was never reconstructed (the 27
September run built a fresh environment on Google Cloud instead); and
the policy response --- execution sources henceforth live under tracked
paths --- cannot restore what was already lost. A related item: the Jev
(TypeSafe) judgment harness lost reliability from roughly 13:10Z on
2026-09-19, both blocker-classification batteries returning degenerate
distributions and a controlled yes-probe falling from about 1.0 to 0.3;
the receipts are retained as incident evidence and the batteries must be
re-run once the health check passes, so the ledger's
first-uncleared-blocker column stands on document evidence rather than
judgment output.

\textbf{Scope and confound inventory.} Several Phase-1 threats remain
live. Many early runs logged train-set rewards only, so time-series
comparisons spanning old and new runs must account for the
instrumentation change. Initial phases used LoRA at ranks 8--64
exclusively; historical baseline comparisons use default hyperparameters
from the Tinker cookbook and the TRL documentation; and no full
learning-rate × rank × batch × group-size grid was swept. Tinker, Modal,
Colab and the PES A100 node use different accelerators, memory
hierarchies and training stacks, so observed differences in reward
dynamics are not purely algorithmic, and the work was not
pre-registered. The model-scale range is 0.6B--1T, with the anchors
above 235B single-seed and descriptive (Chapter 6 §6.1); the limitations
record's ``reliable'' range of 0.6B--235B is the multi-run part of that
roster, so dense-70B and trillion-parameter MoE extrapolations are
unreliable; benchmark coverage is narrow and biased toward verifiable
rewards such as exact match, unit tests and schema validity, with no
human preference data, so findings should not be extended to
reward-model-based RLHF; and the work was carried out at one institution
in Bengaluru, so benchmark choices and evaluation design reflect that
context.

The resource accounting is itself a threat. The recorded out-of-pocket
spend was \$130--140, with roughly 60 A100 GPU-hours of in-kind PES
University compute valued at approximately \$270 and projected carbon of
about 776 kWh and about 296 kg CO₂-eq; the dominant uncertainty is the
Tinker GPU-hour count, inferred because the platform exposes no hardware
telemetry, giving a pessimistic Tinker contribution of about 540 kg
against an optimistic one of about 130 kg. No offsets were purchased;
the mitigation offered is reproducibility by artefact.

\section{Conclusions}\label{conclusions}

This report set out to build a multi-framework training harness for
GRPO-style post-training of large language models and to study that
post-training under a single methodological rule: hold the stack fixed,
change one factor, report what happens. The harness dispatches one
configuration to several back-ends, but only Tinker and TRL produced
completed runs, on different base checkpoints, so it is not a completed
multi-framework benchmark. The four research questions of §1.4 have the
following answers.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{RQ1 (ZVF).} Yes, it is recomputable: the three stored Qwen3-8B
  tensors reproduce 0.130, 0.190 and 0.155 exactly. Its information
  about outcome is limited: its reported association with catastrophic
  collapse is stronger than with final held-out accuracy, and the latter
  is not distinguishable from zero (§6.3; Table 1.1). Its magnitude is
  regime-dependent, from 0.16 to 0.84 across the measured runs.
\item
  \textbf{RQ2 (algorithm labels on a fixed stack).} Unanswered at this
  budget. PPO versus GRPO, GRPO versus Dr.~GRPO and G ∈ \{2, 4, 8, 16\}
  all sit at evaluation accuracy 0.98--0.99 on 0.5B two-digit
  arithmetic, and nulls at ceiling are not evidence of equivalence: they
  cannot separate no effect from no headroom. The unsaturated
  GRPO/Dr.~GRPO comparisons (Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT; the eight-seed
  variant audit of §1.2) are inconclusive and establish no equivalence
  either (§6.2.3, §6.4, §6.5; Table 1.1). The same-stack rerun on the
  Qwen2.5-1.5B GSM8K-CoT configuration set out in §9.4 is what would
  answer it.
\item
  \textbf{RQ3 (manifest-level flip risk).} Yes, for the one pair tested:
  \texttt{stackdiff} flags the open-versus-closed ``DAPO'' pair as R5
  from manifests alone (§7.2). Whether it flags risk that matters on
  outcome was not tested beyond that pair.
\item
  \textbf{RQ4 (governance-first evaluation).} It yields receipt-bound
  per-suite scores and an explicit terminal state for every lane (Table
  C.3). It does not yield a post-training delta: the actor had 40
  training steps, and the paired Table 8.B comparison is inconclusive in
  every lane.
\end{itemize}

On the analytical side the contribution is a measurement layer and a set
of bounded negatives. The Zero-Variance Fraction is a reproducible
diagnostic of advantage-signal starvation, recomputable from stored
tensors, regime-dependent in magnitude (0.72--0.77 on the Phase-1 main
configuration, 0.16 to 0.84 across the measured runs; Table 1.1), with
an association with final held-out outcome that is not distinguishable
from zero (ρ = 0.27, 95\% CI {[}−0.37, 0.88{]}, n = 23). It is
descriptive, not predictive. Its theory has been made falsifiable, and
two of its three consequences reproduced: the U-shape in accuracy across
reward levels and the monotone group-size effect; the gradient link
remains toy-scale and directional. Group size therefore has a candidate
mechanism, but the measured three-seed sweep found its arms
statistically indistinguishable and establishes no optimum (Chapter 6
§6.4). Length bias is not established: the roster-level flag that marks
four of eleven GRPO runs --- peak before 65\% of training, terminal
reward below 90\% of peak --- has post hoc thresholds, is not specific
to length, and fires on most runs in the wider trace pool, so it is not
evidence of length bias; in the controlled comparison the same rule
fires on both GRPO and Dr.~GRPO (five of six GSM8K-CoT runs) while no
run shows length growing, completion length drifts shorter, and the two
are indistinguishable on held-out gain (Chapter 6 §6.5). No claim is
made that Dr.~GRPO is uniquely free of the pattern. The levers we could
test --- a naive curriculum, a matched-token-budget intervention, an
integer group-size sweep --- did not produce reliable held-out gains.
The same-stack de-confounds (PPO versus GRPO, GRPO versus Dr.~GRPO, and
group size) were all measured on a saturated 0.5B arithmetic task with
evaluation accuracy near 0.99, so their nulls say that no difference was
detectable there, not that the methods are equivalent; the one
unsaturated configuration (Qwen2.5-1.5B on GSM8K-CoT, +5 to +6 points
pre-to-post) was not used for a PPO/GRPO or group-size contrast. The
strongest original contribution remains P5, the reporting standard,
whose status the record states precisely: a targeted search of the GRPO,
model-reporting and provenance literature through July 2026 did not
identify an equivalent GRPO-specific machine-readable run datasheet with
rollout provenance, and this is ``an evidence-bounded novelty claim, not
proof of non-existence''. The earlier second bet --- predictive layer
freezing from step-1 behaviour --- is weakened rather than withdrawn: it
held at 1.5B and collapsed to chance at 3B across four seeds, at a mean
of 0.083 ± 0.048.

The E1--E14 campaign is an evaluation-governance contribution, not
evidence about GRPO: the actor received 40 steps of critic-free
policy-gradient training (§8.1.1), so its scores describe a checkpoint
rather than a training method. It delivered what a governance-first
campaign can deliver: complete, receipt-bound results on three suites
(two replacement scopes and one original contract), with Omni-MATH
terminal-complete; full-coverage original-contract scores on two (E1 and
E11); and an explicit, auditable terminal state for every remaining
lane, with a named gate and a named reopen condition instead of an
indefinite pending. Its discipline is negative, which is why it is
credible: original-contract and replacement-scope numbers are never
pooled, no cross-suite aggregate is computed, and no claim of
improvement over a baseline or over the base model is made anywhere in
this work (the one paired trained-versus-base comparison, Table 8.B, is
inconclusive in every lane). The strongest complete positive result,
129/312 = 41.35\% pass@1 on VerilogEval at full 312/312 coverage, is an
end-to-end harness score (150 of its 183 failures are extraction
failures) and is reported as a measurement of one actor on one suite,
with its provenance recorded down to the sampler path.

The report's overall conclusion is about method rather than algorithms.
On this compute scale, and with these instruments, the measurements are
informative while the algorithmic levers are noise-limited; the stack is
a material part of the result, so a label without its stack is not a
reproducible experimental specification; and the most durable artefacts
produced here are the ones that make other people's results checkable
--- the ZVF/PCD diagnostic layer, the eight-item minimum reportable
stack (seven manifest fields and one evaluation item), the registry and
its CLI, and a campaign ledger in which every number is bound to a
receipt and every gap has a gate.

\section{Future Work}\label{future-work}

The work leaves three kinds of open question: evaluation lanes whose
measurement is incomplete, analytical claims that the present evidence
could not settle, and methodological tools that have been built but not
yet tested at scale.

\textbf{Closing the remaining evaluation gaps.} The six replacement
lanes that were launch-pending or quota-blocked on 21 September were run
to completion on 27 September 2026 for \$44.25 in lane spend (Chapter 8
§8.4). Two measurement gaps remain in them. The 108 WebArena tasks that
need an LLM judge are ungraded because the judge account had no credit,
and the native judge model has since been retired, so a regrade would
record a judge substitution. SWE-bench Multilingual has been attempted
on all 300 tasks, but under two serving contracts, so a single 300-task
figure would need the first 110 tasks rerun under the 27 September
contract (32,768-token context and the re-implemented source collector).
Six original-contract scopes are recorded \texttt{CLOSED\_EXTERNAL} with
written closure records: E3 (SDAB private bundle), E7 (BinaryAudit
private payload), E8-original (LifeSciBench package), E10-original
(AgentHarm private tasks), E12 (AppBench deployment) and E14-original
(FrontierMath hosted evaluation). A seventh original contract, E13's
OpenReward held-out games, is also blocked on external access but has no
closure record, because the lane continues through its BALROG
replacement scope (Table C.3). Each reopens only on a provider response,
not on an internal milestone. A full BankerToolBench score for E4 would
be a new-runtime experiment with a grader-only lower bound of
approximately \$59.45, and the 100/100-trial base-model rerun at mean
reward 0.0 provides its calibration point.

\textbf{Turning the campaign into a post-training measurement.} The
campaign reports scores for one actor, not the contribution of the
training step. The paired same-engine comparison of §8.7 (Table 8.B) is
the first base-model arm; scaling it from 5--151 items per lane to the
full native scopes, with more than one training seed and a longer
training run than the actor's 40 steps, would turn each lane from a
descriptive score into a post-training delta. Separately, the retained
E11 result was sampled through a Tinker sampler route that no longer
exists. Re-serving the published adapter mirror and re-running E11 would
measure the same-weights, different-sampler divergence that is currently
unmeasured.

\textbf{Settling the analytical questions.} Five Phase-1 directions
remain open, each answering a threat identified above:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  adequately powered evaluation, moving from the Phase-1 probes'
  held-out \emph{n} of 8--20 (the multi-seed controlled comparisons
  already use 200--500 items, Chapter 1 §1.5) to the full GSM8K test
  split or a large stratified subset at five or more seeds;
\item
  a token-budget-optimal curriculum with staleness bounds, tested
  multi-seed;
\item
  testing whether the mid-to-late band in the per-layer gradient profile
  supports a FLOP-saving freeze schedule, even though step-1
  predictability did not survive scaling;
\item
  hardening the Phase-1 method-trace classifier on grouped run-level and
  temporal splits rather than row-wise cross-validation;
\item
  consolidating the diagnostic findings and the reporting standard for
  publication.
\end{itemize}

Three more follow from the Chapter 6 results. The first is a
length-confounded regime, with the completion cap lifted and longer
horizons, in which a Dr.~GRPO length advantage would be expected to
appear if it exists. The second is the head-to-head protocol between ZVF
and the pairwise-contrast density, to test whether PCD meets its target
association. The third is a harder, unsaturated task on which algorithm
and group-size effects could be detected at all: rerunning the
same-stack PPO/GRPO contrast and G ∈ \{2, 8\} on the Qwen2.5-1.5B
GSM8K-CoT configuration, with five paired seeds and a disjoint
evaluation split, and reporting the difference with its interval against
a stated equivalence margin in accuracy points.

\textbf{Testing the methodological tools.} Three extensions come from
the Semester-4 work:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a pre-registered controller bakeoff against static G = 16 and naive
  boundary heuristics, the test P7 declines to claim in advance of;
\item
  a cross-framework divergence study that runs an identical task,
  reward, decoding and seed configuration across TRL, veRL, OpenRLHF and
  Tinker, turning P5's attribution argument from an audit of existing
  runs into a designed experiment;
\item
  dogfooding the registry schema on the campaign's own runs, so that
  every future benchmark result ships with a verifiable provenance
  record.
\end{itemize}

Two commitments carry forward, because the threats above are structural
rather than incidental. The first is the never-pool rule:
original-contract and replacement-scope numbers stay separate, and
per-suite native denominators are never averaged. The second is that
failures are reported with their mechanism, as the E11 sampler path, the
E4 zero, the lost execution sources, the seed-count discrepancy and the
failed-run spend are here.

\part{Appendices}


\chapter{Complete Run Registry}\label{complete-run-registry}

This appendix is the auditable index of every training and evaluation
run on which the claims of this report rest. It does not present results
--- those live in Chapters 6--8 --- but makes each claim traceable to
the artefacts that produced it: run identifier, library and platform,
base model, algorithm, group size, dataset, seed, steps, and terminal
status. Where a claim cannot be traced through this appendix to a run,
the project's evidence rules require it to be withdrawn rather than
stated.

Table A.9 at the end of this appendix reconciles every run total quoted
in the report. \textbf{The registry covers 1,708 runs: 1,662 in the
machine-readable ledger and 46 more recorded only in the Semester-4
opening artefacts of §A.3.1.} The ledger,
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/experiment\_ledger.tsv},
holds one row per run with the columns
\texttt{source,\ project,\ run\_id,\ model,\ task,\ algo,\ G,\ steps,\ created,\ headline\_metric}.
Its 1,662 data rows decompose into \textbf{844 runs inventoried from the
Tinker training service} and \textbf{818 runs inventoried from Weights
\& Biases}. The Tinker inventory was built by paginating
\longtok{RestClient.list\_\allowbreak{}training\_\allowbreak{}runs} and \texttt{list\_checkpoints};
the W\&B inventory was built per project and validated against the live
W\&B API. The ledger's provenance document,
\texttt{EXPERIMENT\_LEDGER.md}, records the same grand total and notes
that the W\&B workspace holds fourteen projects, not the thirteen
previously believed (\texttt{tinker-new-research} was the omission).

Two other totals appear in the repository and are deliberately
\emph{not} used here: the identity-audit manifest records 949 Tinker and
940 W\&B runs, and \texttt{tinker\_runs.txt} reports ``20 training runs
(963 more not shown)''. These are snapshots of the same platforms at
different times and are not reconcilable without re-querying them. The
ledger is the only artefact carrying per-run identifiers for both
platforms in one file, so it is the figure used throughout for ledgered
runs. The 46 runs outside it are counted separately, from the per-run
\texttt{name} and mode fields of the artefacts that recorded them, and
are named individually in §A.3.1.

carries a creation timestamp, the two eras separate mechanically rather

throughout, with 387 Tinker rows before the boundary and 457 after ---
which is why the inherited-versus-individual statement in Appendix B
rests on commit history rather than run counts.

\section{Semester-3 shared benchmark
runs}\label{semester-3-shared-benchmark-runs}

The Semester-3 contribution was the multi-framework benchmark
infrastructure: a common harness, a fixed evaluation configuration, and
integrations for several independent RL post-training libraries. These
runs are the shared baseline measurements, and they are the ones for
which the registry is \emph{most} complete, because each was produced
under a declared configuration.

\textbf{Cross-framework comparison at the fixed benchmark
configuration.} Configuration for all four rows: model
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B} (Tinker row uses \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base}),
algorithm GRPO, group size G = 8, learning rate 1e-5, dataset
GSM8K{[}:500{]}, 30 steps, seed 42. Metric is \texttt{last10\_avg}, the
mean of the final ten reported training rewards. Source:
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/framework\_comparison.json},
generated by \longtok{aggregate\_\allowbreak{}framework\_\allowbreak{}comparison.py}.

The two \texttt{dryrun} rows are a documented threat to validity, not a
result: the generator falls back to a deterministic seeded trace from
\texttt{verl.VERLTrainer} / \texttt{OpenRLHFTrainer} when the framework
is absent from the sandbox, and the JSON carries the note ``Seeded
deterministic fallback \ldots{} (verl not installed in sandbox)''. Any
comparison reading them as measurements of verl or OpenRLHF is invalid.
The Tinker row also initialises from the \emph{base} checkpoint while
the open-framework rows use the instruction-tuned one, so even the two
real rows are not a clean framework contrast.

\section{Semester-4 P1--P8 study runs}\label{semester-4-p1p8-study-runs}

The P1--P8 series is the individual Semester-4 contribution, and its
runs are indexed by \emph{claim} rather than by project. The audit
ledger at \longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/claim\_\allowbreak{}to\_\allowbreak{}run/}
maps each published claim to its supporting run identifiers, the W\&B
URLs where they can be inspected, whether the model named in the paper
matches the model actually trained, the seed and step counts, the
held-out metric, and an evidence tier. It holds \textbf{18 claims}
carrying \textbf{713 claim-to-run links} --- a count of links, not of
distinct runs, and the 368 links on P7-C1 are the \texttt{zvf-audit}
project count rather than 368 separable experiments.

\textbf{P1--P8 claim-to-run ledger.} Tier vocabulary: A = ≥5 seeds and
≥100 steps; B = 3--4 seeds and 50--99 steps; C = completed/descriptive;
R = deterministic resource or schema claim; X = unlinked, contradicted,
or provenance-conflicted.

\begingroup\scriptsize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
