Three facts here are load-bearing. First, \textbf{no empirical headline
row in the P1--P8 ledger reaches Tier A or B} under the
statistical-rigor thresholds; the rectified tally is C = 12, R = 2, X =
4, and the registry's only Tier A block belongs to P11, an extension
described below. Second, the four Tier X rows are X for specific,
checkable reasons: \textbf{P3-C2} rests on G = 32 \emph{sampling} cells
with zero optimizer steps, so no matched G = 4/G = 32 training
replication exists; \textbf{P4-C1}'s abstract names Qwen3-8B while every
mapped run is \texttt{Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct}; \textbf{P5-C1}
compares \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B-Base} against \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, so
the base checkpoint changes together with the backend and the pair must
not be cited as a backend-only causal effect; and \textbf{P8-C3} is a
proposed architecture with no execution, hence zero linked runs. Third,
of 41 exact Tinker identifiers in the identity audit, 33 are
model-consistent with their W\&B counterpart and 8 conflict; five
conflicts were resolved as W\&B mislinks by three-way arbitration
against the Hugging Face adapter listing, and three remain unresolved
(\texttt{0ef59237}, Qwen3.5-4B recorded against a Nemotron run;
\texttt{78e2d35b}, Qwen3.5-27B; \texttt{0dfee749}, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct
recorded against a DeepSeek V3.1 run). Those three \texttt{model} fields
are contested.

Two column caveats travel with the table. The dataset cell for P7-C1 is
\texttt{—} because the ledger's only dataset-adjacent field for those
368 runs is a prompt/tokenizer family (\texttt{llama3},
\texttt{qwen3\_instruct}, \longtok{gpt\_\allowbreak{}oss\_\allowbreak{}low\_\allowbreak{}reasoning},
\longtok{nemotron3\_\allowbreak{}disable\_\allowbreak{}thinking}), not a dataset name. And the P3
sweep here is \textbf{not} the Semester-3 sweep of Table A.4: it is a
different project (\texttt{rlvr-openings}), a different model
(Qwen3.5-4B), and it reports deltas of 0.0000 rather than held-out
accuracies near 0.99. The two share a group-size grid and nothing else.
Chapters 4 and 6 cite the Table A.4 sweep (Qwen2.5-0.5B) whenever they
give the 0.838 → 0.631 ZVF decline or the 0.98--0.99 held-out figures.
They do not cite P3-C1.

The extension ledger added in August 2026 carries five rows besides P11:
P10-C1 is Tier R (an algebraic identity, not an empirical result),
P10-C2 and P12-C2 are Tier X (placeholder figures and unlinked
prospective claims, zero runs), and P12-C1 is Tier R as a lemma and Tier
X as an empirical headline.

\textbf{P11} is the only block in the registry meeting the report's
strictest evidence bar: 40 arm--seed units at
\longtok{local:zvf-program/audit/results/full/\{grpo,dapo,gspo,drgrpo,aero\}-seed-\{11,23,37,53,71,89,107,131\}.json},
five arms × eight seeds on one stack (Qwen3-8B, GSM8K, 30 steps, Colab
A100, held-out n = 500). Its headline contrast, DAPO versus GRPO, is Δ =
+0.001, percentile-bootstrap CI {[}−0.0045, +0.00675{]} (paired-t 95\%
CI {[}−0.0063, +0.0083{]}, n = 8 seeds; the bootstrap undercovers at
this n), MDE₈₀ = 0.01012, all raw p \textgreater{} 0.21, with
Benjamini--Hochberg rejecting nothing. The mechanism is visible in the
diagnostic: DAPO's mean ZVF is 0.000 against GRPO's 0.693, bought with
3.61× the rollouts (1,734 versus 480) and 1.44× the wall clock. It is
the only place in the work where a claim rests on five or more seeds
over a hundred or more steps, and it supports a null.

\subsection{Opening and null-result
runs}\label{opening-and-null-result-runs}

Chapter 6 reports four null families --- a mixed-variance curriculum, a
matched token budget, hard-prompt filtering, and step-1 layer selection
(§6.4.3) --- and each rests on runs that are not in the ledger. They are
absent for a checkable reason: the string \texttt{rlvr-openings} occurs
nowhere in the ledger, and no file in the fourteen-project
\texttt{wandb\_inventory/} covers it, although every Tinker-managed
block below is logged to that project under the groups named in the
table. The rows are counted from the per-run \texttt{name} (or mode)
fields of each artefact's JSON --- \texttt{results.json} for the
curriculum, token-budget, hard-prompt and loss-formulation blocks,
\texttt{result.json} and its siblings for P1 --- not from an inventory
API, which is why this cohort is reported as its own total. Three
neighbouring blocks add nothing to it: the P3 group-size artefacts are
already indexed --- \texttt{groupsize\_zvf/results.json} reproduces the
per-group deltas of Table A.5's P3-C1 row exactly, and the second sweep
file is discussed below --- and the P2, P7 and P8 ``openings''
(\longtok{p2\_openings/collapse\_\allowbreak{}baseline\_\allowbreak{}analysis.json},
\texttt{p7\_controller/sim.json}, \texttt{p8\_openings/*.json}) are
zero-compute re-analyses that launch no runs.

\textbf{Semester-4 opening and null-result runs (46 runs).} Model is
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B} and dataset GSM8K unless a row says otherwise.
All rows are Tinker-managed except the P1 layer-freeze block, which ran
on Colab for per-layer gradient access. ``Curriculum'' means training
only on non-collapsed (mixed-variance) groups. Group sizes marked
\emph{default} are the launch defaults of the scripts in
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/openings/}; only the campaign
artefact records a group size per run. Δ is the change in held-out
accuracy.

Three provenance notes belong with this table, because two of its blocks
carry superseded siblings and one carries a refuted claim. First,
\texttt{campaign/FINDINGS.md} describes a nine-run, three-seed version
of the campaign on a twelve-prompt held-out set with arm means of +0.028
and −0.028; \texttt{campaign/results.json} holds fifteen runs over six
seeds on a thirty-prompt held-out set (0.8667 = 26/30), and the table
follows the per-run file. The FINDINGS warning that
\longtok{zero\_\allowbreak{}loss\_\allowbreak{}frac} was invalid (an empty-metrics-dict bug,
``since fixed'') belongs to the nine-run batch, not to that file.
Second, the P1 layer-freeze block has the same shape: the two-seed 3B
pass (overlap 0.1111 ± 0.0000) is superseded by the four-seed pass
(0.0833 ± 0.0481), and both sit near chance, so the toy-scale overlap of
1.0 must not be read as a transferable layer-selection signal. Third,
the two single-seed training blocks flagged SUSPECT by independent
verification (kimi, 2026-07-06) are superseded by the multi-seed blocks
above: the curriculum opening's +0.05 (campaign curriculum and baseline
arms separate by 0.006 over six seeds, at 3.3--7.2× oversample cost),
and a single-seed G = 4 gain of +0.125 in
\longtok{p3\_groupsize/sweep\_results.json} --- the campaign's G4 seed-0
baseline gains 0.0000, not 0.125, and that sweep file retains only two
of its twelve entries, so the W\&B group is its record.

\section{E1--E14 evaluation campaign
lanes}\label{e1e14-evaluation-campaign-lanes}

The fourteen evaluation lanes are not training runs; they are
evaluations of a single frozen actor, and they carry the registry's
strictest receipt discipline. The actor is base
\texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B} (commit \texttt{995ad96e…}) with adapter
\longtok{arvindcr4/pavlov-portfolio-qwen36-seed809-stepfinal-tinker-cf0ad8c1…}
(commit \texttt{64444133…}), bfloat16, trained on Tinker, run seed 809
taken from the adapter name. The adapter was trained for 40 steps at G =
4 and learning rate 1 × 10⁻⁵ by REINFORCE with a group-mean baseline (no
KL, no clipping), as recorded in the local W\&B run directory for run
\texttt{bsv8vx04} and the run receipt

Two source documents disagree on two lanes, and both observations are
retained rather than resolved into one, because the disagreement is
itself evidence: the historical snapshot records E7 as ``1/28 errored,
no grade'' against the terminal-state table's ``1/46 attempted'' ---
both real observations at different points in the lane's life. Likewise
E11's canonical figure is 129/312 = 41.35\%; a 129/311 sensitivity
figure survives in non-canonical receipts and is not used as the
headline.

\section{Completeness limitations}\label{completeness-limitations}

The registry is complete as an \emph{index} and incomplete as a
\emph{dataset}, and the distinction governs how it may be used. Coverage
is measured over the 1,662 ledger rows only --- the 46 opening runs of
§A.3.1 are logged per run in their own artefacts and are not part of
this table:

\textbf{Field coverage across the 1,662 ledgered runs.}

The largest gap is structural: \textbf{all 844 Tinker-source rows record
\texttt{task}, \texttt{algo}, \texttt{G} and \texttt{steps} as
\texttt{UNKNOWN}.} The Tinker pagination API returns training-run
identity, base model, LoRA rank and checkpoint metadata but not the
training configuration, so those runs' configurations cannot be
recovered from the surviving inventory, and every algorithm label in the
registry comes from the W\&B half of the ledger. The raw snapshot
\texttt{tinker\_runs.txt} shows the same boundary differently: run ID,
base model truncated to ``Qwen\ldots{}'', LoRA rank 4, timestamp and
status ``OK'' --- no configuration at all.

Four further limits apply.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Aggregate presentation.} This appendix names 105 runs
  individually: the 4 cross-framework runs of Table A.2, the 20 Modal
  campaign runs of A.3, the 12 sweep runs (in 4 rows) of A.4, the 46
  opening runs (in 16 rows) of A.6, and the 23 E-lane rows of A.7 ---
  plus the 18 claim rows of Table A.5, which index claims rather than
  runs. The remaining \textasciitilde1,600 runs are indexed by
  identifier and project in \texttt{experiment\_ledger.tsv}, presented
  here in aggregate because for most of them fewer than half the
  required columns have values.
\item
  \textbf{Non-runs inside the count.} Three entries are not training
  results: \texttt{verl-dryrun} and \texttt{openrlhf-dryrun} are seeded
  deterministic fallbacks, and the E4 base-model rerun performs zero
  training steps; to these add §A.3.1's P1 gradient probe, which takes
  no optimizer steps at all. They are counted because they are runs in
  the registry's sense, and flagged so no reader treats them as
  measurements.
\item
  \textbf{Identity conflicts.} Eight Tinker identifiers were linked to
  W\&B runs whose recorded model did not match; five were resolved
  against the Hugging Face adapter listing, three remain unresolved, and
  those \texttt{model} fields are contested.
\item
  \textbf{Snapshot disagreement.} The 844/818 split used here, the
  949/940 split in the audit manifest, and the ``983 runs'' implied by
  \texttt{tinker\_runs.txt} are three observations of the same platforms
  at three times. No reconciliation was attempted and none should be
  inferred.
\end{enumerate}

None of this changes a reported result, because every result in Chapters
6--8 is bound to named artefacts rather than to the registry as a whole.
It does bound what the registry can be used for: it establishes
provenance and identity, shows that the run population is far larger
than the reported claims, and shows that those claims rest on a small,
explicitly tiered subset --- one Tier A block, a majority Tier C, and
four Tier X rows that must not be cited as evidence.

The run totals that appear in different chapters count different
corpora. Table A.9 reconciles them.

\textbf{Run totals quoted in this report, reconciled.} Each total is a
different corpus, not a different count of the same one.

\chapter{Reproducibility and
Provenance}\label{reproducibility-and-provenance}

\section{What this appendix
establishes}\label{what-this-appendix-establishes}

This appendix records three things that should not be confused with one
another. The first is what a reviewer can independently re-run: a pinned
container, a pinned dependency set, a headline training command, and the
tolerance within which a rerun is expected to land. The second is what
the repository verifies about itself without re-running any model: an
audit suite driven by a single entry point, and a deterministic ledger
check that recomputes the reported arithmetic directly from surviving
evidence files. The third is provenance: which parts of this work were
produced by the Semester 3 group, which by the individual contribution
reported here, and what was lost along the way.

\section{The pinned runtime}\label{the-pinned-runtime}

The container definition is \texttt{Dockerfile}, built from
\longtok{nvidia/cuda:12.4.1-cudnn-devel-ubuntu22.04}. It installs Python
3.12 through the deadsnakes archive rather than the distribution
default, with the in-file comment that Python 3.12 ``matches the frozen
runtime validated 106/106''. It sets \texttt{PYTHONHASHSEED=42} and a
fixed \texttt{LANG}/\texttt{LC\_ALL} of \texttt{C.UTF-8}; stamps the
build commit into an OCI \longtok{org.opencontainers.image.revision}
label and a \texttt{.build\_info} file; fixes \texttt{SEED=42},
\texttt{WANDB\_MODE=offline}, \longtok{HF\_\allowbreak{}HUB\_\allowbreak{}DISABLE\_\allowbreak{}TELEMETRY=1} and
\longtok{OMP\_\allowbreak{}NUM\_\allowbreak{}THREADS=8}; self-checks by importing the core stack at
build time; and runs under \texttt{tini}.

The authoritative pin set is \texttt{requirements.txt}, headed as
``pinned to the frozen E1 runtime lock \ldots{} validated 106/106 on
this stack''. Its exact pins include \texttt{torch==2.7.1},
\texttt{numpy==2.2.6}, \texttt{transformers==5.5.4},
\texttt{trl==1.2.0}, \texttt{datasets==4.8.4},
\texttt{accelerate==1.13.0}, \texttt{peft==0.19.1},
\texttt{huggingface-hub==1.11.0} and \texttt{wandb==0.21.0}, with
bounded ranges for scipy, safetensors, tokenizers, rliable, math-verify,
latex2sympy2-extended and pytest, alongside the framework clients
\texttt{tinker}, \texttt{tinker-cookbook} and \texttt{atroposlib}.

The 106/106 figure is the recorded outcome of the frozen-runtime
validation, which \texttt{execution-notes.md} states as passing the
complete pinned pilot/preregistration suite on Python 3.12.13 with the
pinned versions listed above, and the focused amendment, protocol,
launcher and verifier suite passing 55/55. The same record binds five
artefacts by SHA-256 --- protocol document, screening manifest, launch
manifest, launch fingerprint and source binding --- and states that a
mismatch in package, Python, A100, FLOP, token or gradient conditions
fails closed rather than proceeding.

\section{The reviewer's entry point}\label{the-reviewers-entry-point}

\texttt{REPRODUCE.md} is the reviewer-facing document. Its fastest path
is the structural smoke test at
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/smoke\_test.sh}, which runs in about two
minutes offline and about eight when a live Tinker key is supplied so
that the wire protocol is exercised; the offline path needs no GPU and
no account.

The headline result is GRPO on Qwen3-8B over GSM8K for 30 steps at LoRA
rank 32, group size 8 and seed 42, recorded as a peak per-step training
accuracy (the binary reward) of 62.5 \% with a last-10 average of 34.4
\%; these summarise the training curve of the seed-42 run, not a
held-out evaluation, and Table 1.1 lists them beside the other Qwen3-8B
GSM8K figures they are sometimes confused with. Reviewer-side reruns are
accepted within ±5 percentage points on the last-10 figure and ±10
points on peak, justified in the same document by the residual
non-determinism discussed in §B.10. Per-seed reference values and W\&B
run identifiers are tabulated for seeds 42, 123, 456 and 789, with seed
1024 marked as a reviewer-only rerun; the canonical multi-seed set is
\texttt{\{42,\ 123,\ 456,\ 789,\ 1024,\ 2048,\ 4096,\ 8192,\ 16384,\ 32768\}}.
The group-size ablation is expected to reproduce a peak of 50.0 \% at
G=2, 75.0 \% at G=4, 100 \% at G=8 and 71.9 \% at G=16, with last-10
values of 37.5 \%, 52.1 \%, 84.4 \% and 38.0 \%. Five-seed aggregation
runs through \texttt{utils/stats.py} with rliable intervals and 10,000
bootstrap samples; \texttt{utils/verify\_results.py} checks a rerun
against the recorded expectations at those tolerances.

The cost is stated plainly: about five GPU-hours on a single A100-80GB
for the headline run, against roughly 446 GPU-hours for a full paper
reproduction. Dataset provenance is tabulated with pinned revisions ---
GSM8K at \texttt{740312a} with 7,473 training and 1,319 test rows, plus
MATH-500, HumanEval, ArenaHard Auto, NoRobots and OpenThoughts3 each at
a named snapshot --- with exact shas in \texttt{ARTIFACT.md} §3.4.

\section{Where the documentation has drifted from the
code}\label{where-the-documentation-has-drifted-from-the-code}

Four discrepancies exist between the repository's prose and its live
tree, and a reviewer following the prose literally would hit them. The
training driver has moved: \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} and
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} both invoke \longtok{grpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}base.py} at the
repository root, and the file now lives at
\longtok{platform\_tinker/grpo\_\allowbreak{}gsm8k\_\allowbreak{}base.py}, though the documented
command still works because the CLI in
\longtok{platform\_tinker/tinkerrl/grpo\_cli.py} carries a compatibility
entry point, \texttt{legacy\_main}, that translates the documented flag
spellings onto the current ones --- what changed is the path a reviewer
must type, not the flags the documents specify. \texttt{ARTIFACT.md}
names the smoke test at \texttt{scripts/smoke\_test.sh} rather than at
\longtok{platform\_modal/scripts/smoke\_test.sh}, though
\texttt{REPRODUCE.md} gives the correct path. The pins quoted in prose
are stale: \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} §9 states that
\texttt{requirements.txt} pins
\longtok{transformers\textgreater{}=4.56.2,\textless{}4.60.0} and a
\texttt{datasets\textless{}3.2} bound, whereas the live file pins
\texttt{transformers==5.5.4} and \texttt{datasets==4.8.4}, and
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} §3.3 quotes a third, older set; similarly,
\texttt{ARTIFACT.md} describes the environment as Python 3.10 from the
Ubuntu 22.04 archive, whereas the \texttt{Dockerfile} installs Python
3.12 via deadsnakes and the frozen-runtime record validates on Python
3.12.13. The \texttt{ARTIFACT.md} items are explained by that document's
frozen status --- it records a Semester 3 artefact at commit
\longtok{9019b5c946f17ffe4761b5f4eac13b86d1e51cc0}, dated 19 April 2026
--- but \texttt{REPRODUCE.md} §9 is live guidance, not a frozen record,
and carries the same defect.

\section{The audit apparatus}\label{the-audit-apparatus}

The integrity suite is driven by a single entry point,
\longtok{platform\_local/run\_\allowbreak{}all\_\allowbreak{}audits.py}, which imports a shared
result interface from \texttt{utils/audit\_utils.py} and registers its
audits as a tuple of name-and-function pairs. As it stands in this tree,
that tuple contains \textbf{nine} audits: \texttt{claim\_issues},
\texttt{sync\_issues}, \texttt{anon\_issues}, \texttt{strength\_issues},
\texttt{package\_issues}, \texttt{workflow\_issues},
\longtok{export\_\allowbreak{}guard\_\allowbreak{}issues}, \texttt{caveat\_issues} and
\texttt{scientific\_issues}. Each is implemented by a corresponding
\texttt{platform\_local/} module carrying a \texttt{\_\_main\_\_} block,
so each can also run on its own; \longtok{tests/test\_\allowbreak{}audit\_\allowbreak{}runner.py}
asserts that the registered modules and the
\texttt{platform\_local/*\_audit.py} files are the same set.

Some repository documents give different counts, and the difference is
historical. At the blind-review sweep the suite was thirteen audits,
registered as a list of script filenames at the repository root and
executed as subprocesses; the committed log of that run records
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total=13} with ten passing and three failing,
and the same sweep states that all seventeen \texttt{*\_audit.py}
scripts then at the root were executed, with the suite covering thirteen
of them. The registry now holds nine audits, all passing: a pruning
commit removed obsolete audits, and a later restructuring replaced the
subprocess runner with the in-process interface, so the seventeen-script
description has narrowed by the same route. No script now prints a
metric line directly; the line is constructed in one place,
\texttt{AuditResult.metric\_line()} in \texttt{utils/audit\_utils.py},
and emitted by the nine audit modules plus the runner. A repository-wide
scan finds several dozen files matching \texttt{*\_audit.py}, but that
population is not a suite: it includes unrelated analyses, test modules
and a third-party file in the virtual environment.

The output contract is uniform. \texttt{AuditResult.metric\_line()}
emits
\texttt{METRIC\ \textless{}name\textgreater{}=\allowbreak{}\textless{}issue\_\allowbreak{}count\textgreater{}},
so every audit reports one machine-readable line whose right-hand side
is the number of issues it found, and an audit passes exactly when that
count is zero. \texttt{render\_suite} prints each audit's metric line
and issue codes, then three suite-level lines ---
\texttt{METRIC\ suite\_issues}, \texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total} and
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_passing} --- before either listing the failing
audits by name or stating that all audits are passing. The entry point
exits \texttt{0} when the suite passes and \texttt{1} otherwise, which
makes it usable as a gate; the packaging step runs the suite before
exporting and refuses to package if it fails. The runner's own test
asserts this rendering against a synthetic two-audit suite, checking for
\texttt{METRIC\ audits\_total=2} and \texttt{METRIC\ suite\_issues=1}.

\section{The deterministic ledger
check}\label{the-deterministic-ledger-check}

Where the suite above audits the submission package's internal
consistency, a second instrument audits the campaign's reported figures.
\longtok{outputs/verification/LEDGER\_\allowbreak{}CODE\_\allowbreak{}CHECK\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-19.json},
under schema \texttt{ledger-code-check-v1}, records eleven checks of
which eleven passed and none failed. Each recomputes a claim from the
underlying evidence rather than trusting a summary of it, and the four
classes of check prove four different things.

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}

Exact divisions & E1 \texttt{2/731}; E11 \texttt{129/312}; E11
\longtok{67+62=129}; E14 \longtok{4426/4428} and accuracy
\longtok{0.5131043831902395} (which is 2271/4426, the scorer's judged-row
denominator; the reported headline counts the 2 unjudged rows as
failures, 2271/4428 = 0.5129); E9 \texttt{40\ of\ 75} & That each
reported ratio is the quotient of the two integers the report claims,
and that a partition sums to its whole \\
Receipt presence & E8 eight categories summing to 1967 with every
\texttt{evaluated\ =\allowbreak{}=\allowbreak{}\ expected\_\allowbreak{}total}; E9 graded-row counts; the
strict-completion set & That every claimed unit of coverage has a
surviving graded row behind it, and that the set of strictly complete
suites is exactly \texttt{{[}E8,\ E10,\ E11{]}} \\
Sealed-artefact hash equality & E1 v6 sealed request and fixture receipt
& That the sealed request and the validation receipt are byte-identical
to the artefacts recorded at sealing time \\
Lost-source absence probes &
\longtok{codex\_\allowbreak{}run\_\allowbreak{}execution\_\allowbreak{}sources\_\allowbreak{}absent} & That four
\longtok{.codex-run} paths are genuinely absent, so a later re-creation
cannot be mistaken for the lost original \\
\end{longtable}

The distinction matters under questioning. An exact division proves the
arithmetic is not a rounding convenience or a transcription error; it
cannot prove the underlying evidence is sound. A receipt-presence check
proves coverage claims are backed by artefacts; it cannot prove those
artefacts were produced correctly. A hash-equality check proves identity
between two artefacts --- here, between what was sealed and what was
later validated --- and is the strongest of the four because it does not
depend on re-running anything. An absence probe proves a negative: it
prevents a later reconstruction from being passed off as the original.
Two instances show the classes in use. The E1 records show the sealed
request's SHA-256 beginning \texttt{7adc6e2616f61bc7} matching
\texttt{validation\_v6.json}, with the fixture receipt matching on the
same basis. The E9 records show 41 graded rows resolving to 40 unique
competitions against a denominator of 75 in the streaming arm and
exactly one valid grade in the merged-VLLM arm, which is the check that
confirms the two serving arms were never pooled.

\section{The receipt model}\label{the-receipt-model}

A receipt in this campaign is a durable JSON artefact binding an outcome
to the inputs that produced it, and the binding carries the evidential
weight rather than the file's existence. The clearest statement of the
principle is the receipt-bundle auditor's docstring, which records that
the campaign and the receipt builders are kept deliberately separate
from the auditor, and that the auditor ``never imports a provider SDK,
contacts a service, launches a job, or treats
\texttt{status}/\texttt{online}/a URL/ID alone as provenance''. The
auditor reconstructs contract coverage, receipt predicates, canonical
hash rules, budget caps and cross-artefact bindings, carrying its
expectations as constants rather than reading them from what it checks
--- suite counts of 12 training, 14 primary-evaluation and 26 total, and
budget constants of 18.00 USD maximum, 16.50 USD operational cap and a
1.50 USD safety reserve --- so a receipt set that quietly loses a suite
fails the audit rather than redefining it.

Receipts bind four dimensions of a run: protocol, runtime, source and
event order. The frozen-runtime record applies that discipline to the
runtime dimension alone, binding five artefacts --- protocol document,
screening manifest, launch manifest, launch fingerprint and source
binding --- by SHA-256 and failing closed on any mismatch. The judgment
layer for subjectively scored questions follows the same pattern: one
durable receipt per judgment binds the state bytes, the question text
and the raw answer, and battery execution is gated on a health check
rather than the service's availability, so a degraded answer cannot be
laundered into a valid one. Where a lane could not be scored, its
terminal state is recorded in the ledger of record with a named reopen
condition, so the absence of a result is itself receipted.

\section{Evidence loss in the finish
era}\label{evidence-loss-in-the-finish-era}

One loss of evidence occurred and is recorded rather than concealed.
During post-review disk cleanup the untracked \texttt{.codex-run/}
working tree was deleted. Four paths were confirmed absent by the
absence probes described above:
\longtok{finish\_20260912/e1\_\allowbreak{}recovery\_\allowbreak{}v6/bounded\_sdk.py}, holding the
E1 wave10 repair code;
\longtok{finish\_20260912/e9\_completion/restored/}, holding 1,613
hash-verified restored dependency sources from the OpenHands and
Composio forks used to build the MLDevBench runtime;
\longtok{finish\_20260912/e6\_continuation/checks\_v1.py}, an offline
receipt validator the reconciliation record describes as structure-only
and of low impact; and
\longtok{public\_\allowbreak{}native\_\allowbreak{}improve\_\allowbreak{}v2/e1/metadata\_wave08.py}, the driver
for the E1 multilingual run that graded 35 of 300 tasks.

The loss does not invalidate any figure reported in this thesis, for
three reasons that hold independently. First, none of the four paths is
the source of any reported score. The E1 and E9 replacement lanes were
eventually run on 27 September with new thin runners rather than the
lost code (§8.4). The loss still bears on E1: the source-context
collector had to be re-implemented for that run and was checked
byte-for-byte against the original on only three recorded tasks, which
is one reason the 1/190 and 4/110 figures are not pooled. Second, the
sealed requests, validation receipts, regression fixtures and the
10-of-10 targeted-test log from the lost E1 code all survive, so a
faithful re-implementation is constrained by surviving fixtures rather
than free to invent behaviour; that re-implementation has since landed
under a tracked path and passes the lost suite's recorded regressions,
four process-group and six cleanup. Third, the loss is confined to
finish-era iteration code: the replacement-lane native runners survive
in tracked locations, including a 515-line multilingual runner carrying
no reference to the deleted tree. The policy adopted in response ---
that execution sources live in tracked repository paths and never in
untracked scratch trees --- is the durable outcome of the incident.

\section{The Semester 3 and Semester 4 provenance
boundary}\label{the-semester-3-and-semester-4-provenance-boundary}

The boundary between inherited and individual work is a git tag.
\texttt{capstone-final-2026-04-25} resolves to commit
\longtok{21a99ef766543a1ca86bfbad188445d3596ea73c}, dated 23 April 2026,
whose subject line records the final metric-separation rewrite that
segregated training rewards, held-out capability and proxy diagnostics.
Two hundred and fifty commits precede that tag. A reviewer can reproduce
the state at the boundary by adding it as a worktree,
\texttt{git\ worktree\ add\ ../tinker-rl-lab-sem3\ capstone-final-2026-04-25},
which materialises the Semester 3 tree without disturbing the current
checkout.

The Semester 3 group comprised six students --- Arvind C R, Sandhya
Jeyaraj, Madhu Kumara L, Mohammad Rafi, Dhruva N Murthy and Arumugam
Chetty K --- under guides Anwesh Reddy Paduri and Narayana Darapaneni.
Their work is the multi-framework benchmark infrastructure and the
initial GRPO baseline experiments. Semester 4 was carried out
individually under Ramesh Prakash Guledgudd, and the declaration facing
this report assigns to that contribution the research direction, the
experimental expansion, the statistical analyses, the P1--P8 paper
series and the E1--E14 campaign. The solo-authorship commit
\longtok{282e6b4f0f158de11b3f5bb4656e2d871f68f5c0}, dated 7 July 2026,
records that division at paper level. The provenance record states that
584 commits and 5,050 changed files separated the boundary from the tree
as it then stood, and that the eight paper PDFs were compiled at commit
\longtok{006e90a38e4dabe2d0b4778be01dc7b27f2d3462} on 9 July 2026, one
recompiled the following day after a findings-ledger rename. Those
counts carry an as-of date rather than describing the current tree, so a
reviewer recomputing them will obtain a larger number.

One consequence of this history shapes the repository's structure: the
implementation remains shared, with experiment drivers, result tables,
figures, LaTeX sources and audit scripts referencing one another across
the boundary, and the provenance record is explicit that this is why the
code is not split into separate trees. What the boundary separates is
authorship of the research contribution, not access to the machinery.
Two reporting rules follow and are enforced throughout this report:
original-contract and replacement-scope results are never pooled, with
no cross-suite aggregate, which the ledger check independently verifies
through the arm-separation and component-split checks; and no
replacement benchmark's score is presented as the score of the benchmark
it replaces, with every substitution named as such at its point of use.

\section{Residual non-determinism and the limits of
reproduction}\label{residual-non-determinism-and-the-limits-of-reproduction}

Not everything here is reproducible in the strict sense, and the
tolerances exist because of it. Three sources of residual
non-determinism survive even with fixed seeds and deterministic cuDNN
settings: remote LoRA training is not bit-deterministic across scheduler
decisions, with runs replayed months apart drifting by two to four
percentage points on GSM8K last-10, documented through three reruns of
the same seed-123 configuration; some matmul operations on H100 and A100
hardware introduce non-determinism through CUDA and cuBLAS; and sampling
at temperature 0.8 with top-p 0.95 executes in a remote virtual machine,
so a fixed seed does not fix the sampled trajectory. The record states
the consequence itself: run-to-run variance on this setup is of the same
order as the effect reported in the paper's PPO-versus-GRPO comparison,
which is why that comparison is reported as statistically
indistinguishable rather than as a difference.

Three limits remain. Lanes blocked on external access cannot be
reproduced from this repository, because the blocking resource is not in
it. The six lanes completed on 27 September can be rerun from the code
in their lane directories under
\longtok{outputs/finish\_\allowbreak{}pending\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-27/}, but only with cloud
accounts, a served copy of the actor and, for E6, a judge account with
credit; CORE-Bench's answer keys are deliberately kept out of the
repository. The evidence-loss paths cannot be reproduced in their
original form, only re-implemented against surviving fixtures, and that
distinction should be preserved in any future citation. And figures
originating at the Semester 3 boundary describe the tree at that tag, so
reproducing them requires the worktree checkout rather than the current
HEAD.

\section{Four collisions worth
flagging}\label{four-collisions-worth-flagging}

The symbol \(\varepsilon\) carries three unrelated meanings. In the
hyperparameter mapping it is the PPO/GRPO clip range, default 0.2, swept
over \(\{0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5\}\). In the ZVF definition it is a
numerical tolerance, \(10^{-6}\), deciding whether a group's sample
variance counts as degenerate. In the optimiser configuration it is the
Adam epsilon, \(10^{-8}\). It also appears additively in the advantage
denominator, \(\sigma_g + \varepsilon\).

\(K\) is used two ways: in the formal treatment of ZVF it denotes the
rollouts in a group, a synonym for the group size elsewhere called
\(G\), whereas in the derivations appendix \(G\) is the group size and
\(K\) is the number of successes --- a design parameter in the one case,
an outcome in the other. The letter \(r\) is similarly the reward, the
LoRA rank (swept over \(\{4,8,16,32,64\}\)) and the Pearson correlation.

Finally, ZVF is expanded two ways in the sources. The formal definition
and the abstract both read ``Zero-Variance Fraction'', the expansion
used here; one figure caption reads ``Zero-Value-Filtered''. The caption
variant is noted only so that a reader meeting it is not misled.

Counted from this table: three scopes are strictly complete (E8 and E10
replacement scopes, E11 original contract), E14 is terminal-complete,
and six original contracts are \texttt{CLOSED\_EXTERNAL} with written
closure records (E3, E7, E8-original, E10-original, E12, E14-original).
E13's original contract is also blocked on external access but carries
no closure record, because the lane continues through its replacement
scope. Original-contract full-coverage scores exist for E1 and E11.



\chapter{Campaign Lane Detail}\label{campaign-lane-detail}

This appendix holds the lane-by-lane record behind Chapter 8: provenance
of the training run, per-lane serving and scoping notes, the engineering
history behind individual figures, the per-lane narratives of the six
replacement scopes completed on 27 September 2026, the full E4 rerun
audit, and the per-lane caveats of the small-scale reruns. None of it
changes a figure reported in Chapter 8; every number here is quoted
exactly as its source receipt records it, and the two reporting rules of
Chapter 8 (no pooling of original-contract and replacement-scope
results, no precision beyond the receipt) apply throughout.

\section{Actor identity, inference conditions and scoping
notes}\label{actor-identity-inference-conditions-and-scoping-notes}

The design consequence of evaluating one actor for seed variance is
specific: every suite number reported in Chapter 8 comes from one actor,
and where a lane sampled more than one completion per task it did so to
satisfy the suite's own protocol, not to estimate variance.

Inference conditions were frozen per lane and recorded in that lane's
own receipt rather than imposed globally, and the lanes differ widely.
The E10 native run declared a temperature of 0, a 32,768-token context,
4,096 output tokens per request, 3,600 seconds of wall time and an \$8
envelope. Its receipt also declares \longtok{max\_\allowbreak{}generation\_\allowbreak{}requests} =
1,967, which is a ceiling on generation requests, not a task count: the
lane has 97 episodes, and the run recorded 414 native requests (405 in
the main run and 9 in the continuation). The collector is written
explicitly not to admit shorter native conditions. The E11 run was
sampled at temperature 0.2, top-p 0.95, 1,024 maximum output tokens and
seed 1816, one sample per problem across all 312 prompts. The point of
recording them per lane is that the sampling regime is part of the
result, not a detail beneath it.

Two scoping notes complete the picture of what the actor has actually
been measured on. The E1 original-contract evaluation was a
one-checkpoint evaluation whose 731 generations were produced by a mixed
backend within a single run --- 476 Tinker generations and 255 Modal
vLLM generations --- and that backend mix is recorded in the original
receipt. Separately, two small probes exist at other parameter settings
but are not suite results and are not reported as such: a base-model
smoke run of the E11 harness measured zero scored tasks, and a four-task
pilot on the step-40 checkpoint (the last training step, saved
separately from \texttt{stepfinal}) returned four passes out of four
with zero extraction failures while carrying an unresolved outstanding
blocker --- the decontamination receipt identifier for that pilot does
not exist, and the split-manifest validator still fails on it.

\section{Training-run provenance}\label{training-run-provenance}

The adapter's configuration survives in three places: the local Weights
\& Biases run directory for run \texttt{bsv8vx04}
(\longtok{wandb/run-20260809\_140744-bsv8vx04/files/}), whose
\texttt{config.yaml}, \texttt{wandb-metadata.json} and
\texttt{output.log} hashes match those recorded in
\longtok{outputs/public\_\allowbreak{}portfolio\_\allowbreak{}2026-09-05/training\_\allowbreak{}identity\_\allowbreak{}verified.json};
the local run receipt
\longtok{checkpoints/grpo/pavlov\_\allowbreak{}portfolio\_\allowbreak{}api\_\allowbreak{}swegym\_\allowbreak{}qwen36\_\allowbreak{}20260809\_\allowbreak{}seed809.json};
and the Hugging Face checkpoint branches. The two local files are
excluded from version control by \texttt{.gitignore}, so they exist on
the project workstation but not in the public repository.

Four things cannot be recovered, and they are listed so that no reader
fills them in:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The exact training code.} The run records git commit
  \texttt{8fdade2f} with uncommitted changes that were never saved. The
  \texttt{pavlov\_portfolio} preset and its dataset builder do not exist
  at that commit; they first appear in commit \texttt{72b9336d3} on
  2026-08-14. The reward and data-pool code in Table 8.1 is therefore
  taken from the current tree, whose dataset hash does not match the
  recorded \texttt{bd1f0db6…}. It is the best reconstruction, not proof.
  The loss function and training loop are identical at
  \texttt{8fdade2f}.
\item
  \textbf{Which 80 training prompts were drawn.} The draw used
  \texttt{random.Random(809)} over the 512-row pool, but the pool
  construction that ran is the unrecoverable code above.
\item
  \textbf{The Tinker sampler weights.} They were purged with the run;
  the four checkpoints survive only on Hugging Face.
\item
  \textbf{Any held-out training-time evaluation.} None was run
  (\texttt{evaluate\_heldout} false).
\end{itemize}

The run also departs from
\longtok{zvf-program/flagship/PAVLOV\_\allowbreak{}EXPERIMENT\_\allowbreak{}PROTOCOL\_\allowbreak{}2026-08-09.md}.
The protocol plans a learning-rate search and a twelve-suite curriculum
with 1,200/128 prompt and response tokens. This run used two suites, 40
steps, 1,536/384 tokens and a learning rate of 1 × 10⁻⁵, and the
protocol does not mention it; only the budget matches.

\section{Serving conditions: format limits and the scope of the
stop-sequence
mechanism}\label{serving-conditions-format-limits-and-the-scope-of-the-stop-sequence-mechanism}

In Table 8.2, most E8 responses and nearly half the E11 responses yield
no gradable answer, and two thirds of E14 responses hit the length cap.
The single-turn Tinker generations of E1 show no role-token run-on,
which suggests that the degeneration needs multi-turn agent context or
the \texttt{/v1/responses} route. The E4 finding document states the
mechanism more generally than this evidence supports.

\section{Strictly complete scopes: lane
detail}\label{strictly-complete-scopes-lane-detail}

The ledger lists E11 both among its ``replacement scopes'' and among its
original-contract results; Table 8.A lists it as original-public-subset.
It is one run and one result, not two.

\textbf{E8 --- LAB-Bench, public split.} Per-category accuracy, correct
counts and coverage are: TableQA 0.7869 (192/244); FigQA 0.5138
(93/181); ProtocolQA 0.2500 (27/108); SuppQA 0.1829 (15/82); LitQA2
0.1759 (35/199); DbQA 0.1308 (68/520); SeqQA 0.0333 (20/600); and
CloningScenarios 0 (0/33). No category is incomplete. Coverage per
category is at or above 0.9878 everywhere, and the CloningScenarios
coverage is exactly 1.0 with zero correct. The receipt also records zero
parser errors, 1,960 responses marked ``sure'' against 7 marked
``unsure'', and a completion profile of 1,086 length-truncated finishes
against 881 stop finishes. Because the receipt's correct and incorrect
fields partition all 1,967 rows, the 1,259 unparsed responses are
counted as incorrect in the published accuracy, and the figure is an
end-to-end harness score rather than an accuracy over the subset of
responses that yielded a parseable answer.

\textbf{E10 --- AgentDojo, benign utility scope.} The receipt records
\longtok{missing\_\allowbreak{}evaluation\_\allowbreak{}ids} empty and
\longtok{native\_\allowbreak{}handled\_\allowbreak{}error\_\allowbreak{}count} zero. The 97 episodes decompose
as banking 16, slack 21, travel 20 and workspace 40. The receipt carries
the run manifest hash
\longtok{00e4228f4b757dc32b69b74f05a88348151f13185b470c5f00e31fdaa830ef30}
and fixes the protocol identity as \longtok{benign\_\allowbreak{}task\_\allowbreak{}utility} v1.2.2
with attack, defence and security-evaluation fields all null and
\texttt{heldout\_claim} false.

An earlier attempt terminated with 94 of 97 episodes and score null,
missing workspace tasks 37 through 39. The cause was a confirmed
collector fault rather than a model failure: verified task receipts
recorded absolute \texttt{/cache/...} trace paths, and the frozen
summary recomputed those paths on the local host, so every completed
receipt failed comparison after extraction. The repair was deliberately
minimal --- the original archive and every extracted receipt and trace
byte are unchanged, and a narrowly scoped adapter rewrites only the
freshly computed \longtok{task\_result.native\_\allowbreak{}trace\_\allowbreak{}path} to the
verified canonical remote path before the frozen summary compares it,
leaving native task code, environment, prompt, result parsing, utility
values and handled-error policy untouched. The revised collector passed
18 offline tests, independently re-run in 0.507 seconds, and the
continuation run that closed episodes 37 through 39 was executed under
reservation \longtok{public-20260905-agentdojo-continuation01} with the
native wrapper hash
\longtok{11a8b61631390d9b20decd4263008e5a4c6f648a033d314384bac9e5d2456d77}.

\textbf{E11 --- VerilogEval.} The upstream NVlabs/verilog-eval harness
ran at revision \longtok{c498220d0a52248f8e3fdffe279075215bde2da6}, using
Icarus Verilog 12.0 at revision
\longtok{4fd5291632232fbe1ba49b2c26bb6b2bf1c6c9cf} and Verilator 5.050.
Pass verdicts were determined deterministically: for each pinned problem
the single \texttt{sample01} \texttt{sv-iv-test} log was read and marked
PASS only when it contained a full-line ``Mismatches: 0 in samples''
verdict, with missing or non-matching logs marked FAIL. The upstream
\texttt{sv-iv-analyze} summariser could not be used because it fails on
a missing optional langchain dependency; that failure is recorded in the
receipt and does not affect the aggregation, which reads the simulator
logs directly. An extraction failure writes an empty candidate file that
always fails.

The E11 lane also carries the campaign's clearest example of a scoring
decision taken for evidential rather than numerical reasons. The
all-problems reference sweep --- running the shipped reference
implementation of every problem through both simulators --- returned 311
of 312. One problem,
\longtok{verilog\_eval/spec-to-rtl/Prob099\_\allowbreak{}m2014\_\allowbreak{}q6c}, fails to
elaborate against its own test bench under both simulators, with Icarus
reporting that ports \texttt{Y2} and \texttt{Y4} are not ports of
\texttt{good1}; no candidate implementation can pass that task, and the
upstream defect biases pass@k downwards by at most 0.64 percentage
points on the specification-to-RTL split (1/156) and by 0.13 percentage
points overall (129/311 − 129/312). Excluding it yields 129/311 =
0.4148. That corrected figure is \emph{not} canonical. The recorded
amendment of 2026-08-22 states the reason plainly: no immutable
benchmark-level justification was captured for the exclusion, so the raw
129/312 result is canonical and the 129/311 value is retained only as a
noncanonical sensitivity; no samples and no verifier artifacts were
changed by the amendment. The ledger's integrity check confirms that the
component split sums correctly to the canonical total.

\textbf{E14 --- Omni-MATH replacement scope.} The 4,426 accepted rows
are 99.95 per cent of 4,428. The two rows not accepted are
\texttt{omni-01195-a6cbdc4ee3c8694b} at row index 1,195 and
\texttt{omni-02044-612ddacaa39d91fe} at row index 2,044. The terminal
note of 2026-09-19 resolves them in a direction that does not flatter
the lane: the Omni-Judge outputs for both rows are truncated before any
``\#\# Equivalence Judgement'' section --- row 1,195 ends mid numeric
answer and row 2,044 ends mid sum expression --- so the judge emitted no
verdict at all, and the native scorer's omission of rows lacking an
equivalence judgement is correct official behaviour rather than a parser
defect. The terminal note recorded the score impact as nil on the
grounds that both rows already count in the denominator as not correct;
that is not so for the official scorer, whose 0.5131 is computed over
the 4,426 judged rows, so Chapter 8 counts the two rows as failures and
reports 2,271/4,428 = 51.29 per cent. The lane closes as
terminal-complete under the native protocol with 4,428 of 4,428
dispositions recorded (4,426 accepted plus 2 recorded skip reasons), and
no re-judge was performed because re-judging would deviate from the
official scorer and constitute new evidence generation with no score
effect.

\section{Original-contract results: lane
detail}\label{original-contract-results-lane-detail}

\textbf{E1 --- SWE-bench Pro.} The denominator is the complete 731-row
test split of \texttt{ScaleAI/SWE-bench\_Pro} at revision
\longtok{7ab5114912baf22bb098818e604c02fe7ad2c11f}. Within that
denominator the run produced 713 native evaluations, fourteen generation
failures and four lost generation artifacts; those eighteen non-native
outcomes are retained in the denominator rather than trimmed, so the
figure is a rate over all 731 tasks and not over the subset that graded
cleanly. The declared licence for the dataset is null in the lane's own
receipt. The evaluation harness is \texttt{scaleapi/SWE-bench\_Pro-os}
at commit \longtok{ca10a60a5fcae51e6948ffe1485d4153d421e6c5}, and the
731-task scoring run used the 731 official run scripts unmodified on
native \texttt{linux/amd64} Modal workers. A 1,200-second timeout,
against the official 120, was used only in an earlier single-task
harness check that ran the gold patch under arm64 QEMU emulation. That
check is recorded as not a model score, so the timeout deviation does
not apply to the 2/731 figure.

\textbf{E2 --- FrontierSWE.} Beyond the missing revision-bound
authorisation, the original checkpoint was unavailable on Tinker at the
time; its Tinker sampler route was later confirmed purged, while the
adapter weights are preserved on the Hugging Face branch.

\textbf{E4 --- BankerToolBench.} The lane's public artifacts were
restored and 1,175 manifest checks pass, but the pass3/pass16 archive is
genuinely missing --- zero of six artifacts verify, and only
\texttt{TINKER\_BRIDGE.json} does.

\textbf{E7 --- BinaryAudit.} The terminal ledger of record gives the
denominator as 46 attempted; an earlier working snapshot, since
superseded, recorded the same lane as ``1/28 errored''. The two
snapshots differ in the scope of what they counted as an attempt, and
Chapter 8 reports the terminal figure while flagging that the superseded
snapshot's narrower count exists in the record.

\textbf{E9 --- MLE-bench.} Coverage without complete grading is not a
score, and the lane records it as such. Behind the coverage number sit
97 legacy receipts across 50 competition identifiers, of which 41 graded
receipts cover 40 unique competitions; the ledger's integrity check
verifies precisely that decomposition. The separate merged-vLLM arm
holds seven receipts, of which exactly one is a valid grade (H\&M,
0.02132) and six are invalid submissions, and the integrity check
confirms that arm is recorded separately with one graded row. One old
Spooky Author submission is absent locally although the later run's
complete submission is retained.

\section{Replacement-scope lane narratives, 27 September
2026}\label{replacement-scope-lane-narratives-27-september-2026}

Three things changed in how the lanes were executed, and each is
recorded as a deviation in the lane's own result file. First, where a
fail-closed controller blocked only on paperwork that the directive had
superseded, the lane used a thin runner that calls the benchmark's
native loop and native grader directly. Second, compute moved off AWS:
E2, E6 and E9 ran on Google Cloud virtual machines, E1's grading ran in
Modal sandboxes, and E13 ran locally. Third, every lane was served the
same actor, \texttt{pavlov-public-portfolio-bf16} (the base model with
the seed809 adapter merged, §8.1), through vLLM with the non-thinking
chat template. No lane went through the Tinker bridge whose missing stop
sequences produced the E4 zero (§8.1.2, §8.5), so that defect is not
expected here, although the lanes' result files do not record their stop
settings. The shared endpoint served at a 32,768-token context. E5 used
a dedicated 65,536-token copy of the same weights because its banking
prompts exceed 32k.

\textbf{E1 --- SWE-bench Multilingual.} This run covers the 16 wave-10
recovery tasks and the 174 tasks that the 12 September waves never
reached, 190 tasks in all. The actor resolved one
(\texttt{phpoffice\_\_phpspreadsheet-3463}), which is 1/190 = 0.53 per
cent. Only 57 of the 190 produced a patch that applied and could be
graded: 120 failed at patch application or evaluation and 13 produced an
empty patch. Over graded tasks alone the rate is 1/57 = 1.75 per cent
({[}0.3, 9.3{]}). Thirteen tasks produced no generation and were scored
as failures: eight exceeded the 32,768-token serving context and five
hit provider errors. The earlier waves covered the other 110 tasks, with
4 resolved (3.64 per cent, {[}1.42, 8.98{]}). They used the same task
universe, adapter and grader family, but served at a 65,536-token
context, and their source-context collector was lost and had to be
re-implemented for this run. Because the contract differs, the two runs
are not combined, and the lane does not report a 300-task figure.

\textbf{E2 --- CORE-Bench, hard difficulty.} The actor answered every
question correctly on 27 of the 45 \texttt{codeocean\_hard} capsules, a
task accuracy of 0.600. At the question level it answered 53 of 79
correctly: 28 of 33 written questions and 25 of 46 vision questions.
Thirty-two tasks ended with the agent calling \texttt{finish}, ten at
the 150-turn cap and three at the 8,100-second time limit. The agent was
a thin tool loop (\texttt{bash}, \texttt{query\_image}, \texttt{finish})
rather than the benchmark's reference CORE-Agent scaffold, and the same
actor answered the vision queries. Thirty tasks never reached a machine
because of a local DNS outage, and a driver crash then interrupted ten
of their reruns. All thirty were rerun from scratch, and every aborted
attempt is archived. Because a leak of the answers would inflate this
figure, the lane checked for one. No agent command referenced any host
that serves the answers. Each capsule's \texttt{results/} directory is
stripped before the agent starts, and the answer keys are held off the
agent's machine. Two qualifications remain. Twelve of the 27 correct
tasks left no files in the capsule's results directory, which the native
grader does not require. Some vision questions are categorical and could
be guessed. With n = 45 the interval spans 27 percentage points.

\textbf{E5 --- Tau3.} Eight of 97 tasks succeeded, a pass\^{}1 of 0.082.
Twenty-five tasks ended in infrastructure errors: fourteen exceeded the
65,536-token context and eleven returned an empty assistant message.
Their cause is not established, and they count as failures. An
accidental resume of the run overwrote 24 of those error records in the
results file; their identifiers and causes were rebuilt from the run
log. Over the 72 tasks without such an error the native pass\^{}1 is
8/72 = 0.111, reported as a sensitivity figure only. The 20 tasks
cleaned before 19 September were rerun under the same protocol rather
than carried over, and scored 1/20. The user simulator was
\texttt{gpt-4.1-2025-04-14} at temperature 0.

\textbf{E6 --- WebArena.} All 812 tasks were run, and 90 succeeded,
giving 0.111. The figure is a lower bound. Of the 114 tasks whose native
evaluator calls an LLM judge, 108 could not be graded, because the judge
account had no credit for the whole run; the other six were resolved to
zero by a non-judge check. Forty-one tasks failed with a harness-level
browser timeout. Both groups count as failures. The graded-only rate is
90/704 = 0.128 ({[}0.105, 0.155{]}). If every ungraded task had passed,
the rate would be 198/812 = 0.244. With the 41 harness errors counted as
failures, the true figure lies between 0.111 and 0.244, and nearer the
lower end if the ungraded tasks succeed at the graded rate. The native
judge model (\texttt{gpt-4-1106-preview}) is retired, so a later regrade
would substitute a different judge. Most failures are early stops by the
native loop: the same action three times (237), the same typing three
times (103), the step limit (101), and three consecutive parse failures
(98). The agent was limited to 384 output tokens per step, the few-shot
examples were passed as user and assistant turns because the chat
template rejects mid-conversation system messages, and sites were not
reset between tasks, as in the native runner. The last 103
shopping-admin tasks were split across three containers of the same
official site image to finish in time.

\textbf{E9 --- ML-Dev-Bench.} Ten of the 34 released tasks passed their
native validators, 0.294. The agent was the benchmark's OpenHands
CodeActAgent, limited to 50 iterations. No task errored or timed out,
but only after one harness repair: OpenHands 0.21.0 hangs when it
truncates its context window, and a one-line upstream fix was backported
without touching prompts, validators or tasks. Thirty-nine of 1,045
model calls exceeded the 32,768-token context and were absorbed by that
truncation path. A relay between the agent and the actor forced the
non-thinking template and restored tool-call text that the server's
parser had dropped. An earlier four-task batch that hung before the
repair is excluded.

\textbf{E13 --- BALROG.} All 255 native episodes were played and graded,
and the BALROG score is 26.1 per cent progression (standard error 1.77;
a stratified bootstrap gives {[}22.7, 29.5{]}). BALROG's score is the
mean of six per-environment means, not a mean over episodes. By
environment the actor reached BabyAI 82.0 per cent (n = 50), Baba Is AI
25.0 (n = 120), Crafter 22.7 (n = 10), TextWorld 16.9 (n = 30), MiniHack
10.0 (n = 40) and NetHack 0.0 (n = 5). Sampling followed the recorded
protocol at temperature 1.0, but with thinking off where the original
run had it on. A 2,000-step cap was placed on NetHack for cost; all five
NetHack episodes ended on their own before it, so the cap never took
effect. Episodes stopped by an operational stop or a hung worker were
restarted from scratch with fresh seeds. The result file records 262
episode attempt records for the 255 scored episodes, of which 7 are
marked killed for restart (\longtok{n\_killed\_\allowbreak{}for\_\allowbreak{}restart\_\allowbreak{}records});
its caveat text instead counts 11 restarts (one NetHack and five Baba Is
AI episodes at the 01:40 UTC stop, plus five hung workers), and the file
does not reconcile the two counts. If hangs correlate with episode
difficulty, that is a mild selection risk. The run used macOS arm64
builds of the pinned sources rather than the Linux image. The 26.1
figure is on a different scope from the 26-episode small-scale E13 rows
of Tables 8.A and 8.B and is not compared with them.

The launch-pending and quota labels of 19 September no longer describe
any lane.

\section{E4 rerun: closure and full
audit}\label{e4-rerun-closure-and-full-audit}

The E4 lane closed \texttt{CLOSED\_PARTIAL} on 19 September 2026: the
pass3/pass16 archive is genuinely missing, only one of six artifacts
verifies, and the 0.3115 recovery metric stands as receipt-only evidence
because unlimited budget removes a spending cap but cannot reconstruct
model outputs that no longer exist. The only funded path recorded at
closure was a fresh full rerun whose grader-only lower bound was
estimated at approximately \$59.45, which sits outside every envelope
then authorised. That path was subsequently authorised separately and
executed, and its result is reported as a diagnostic rather than as a
score, because that is what its design makes it.

The rerun was configured on the base model with a zero-initialised LoRA
snapshot --- the lane's record states this is equivalent to base
sampling, with no training steps --- executed through the Modal bridge
\texttt{/v1/responses} path at \texttt{-\/-n-concurrent\ 2} under a cap
of \$55.91445263. It completed 100 of 100 trials with one
\texttt{NonZeroAgentExitCodeError}, and returned a mean reward of
exactly 0.0, with all one hundred trials in the 0.0 bucket, over a
runtime of 3 hours 29 minutes.

The value of this run lies entirely in the failure mechanism, which was
audited across all one hundred trajectories. Agents terminate after 2 to
14 steps, with a median of about 6, and produce no
\texttt{deliverables/} directory; the verifier consequently
short-circuits to 0.0 on the message ``No deliverables found''.
Twenty-seven or more of the trajectories end in role-token degeneration
of the form \texttt{user\ user\ assistant\ ...} repetition. The record
at the time read this as the base model being unable to sustain the
opencode tool-use loop. A later check does not support that reading. The
bridge's \texttt{/v1/responses} path passed no stop sequences and
decoded with special tokens removed, so generation ran past the
end-of-turn token into invented \texttt{user}, \texttt{tool\_response}
and \texttt{assistant} turns, which is exactly the role-token
degeneration observed. Two implications follow, and they cut in opposite
directions. The positive implication is that the harness behaves
correctly at the boundary --- it fails closed, produces a legible
mechanism for every zero, and does not manufacture partial credit. The
negative implication is that no full-suite BankerToolBench score exists
for this actor family; the only other measurement is the six-task subset
of Tables 8.A and 8.B. The same bridge served E2, E5, E7 and the E9
streaming arm, so Table 8.2 marks those lanes as exposed to the same
defect. In particular, the rerun's 0.0 must not be set against the
original single-task outcome as though the two were a controlled
pairing: they are different configurations under different runtime
conditions, and the campaign makes no comparison between them.

Spend on the rerun was \$15.07 charged against the \$55.91 cap,
comprising 16.35 million prompt tokens and 2.18 million completion
tokens.

\section{Externally blocked lanes: access requests and
verification}\label{externally-blocked-lanes-access-requests-and-verification}

Access was requested through each provider's public channel: a GitHub
issue for E7 (\texttt{QuesmaOrg/BinaryAudit\#22}), a Hugging Face
dataset discussion for E10, and direct requests to the maintainers of
E3, E12 and E14. No grant had been received at the time of writing, and
delivery of the direct requests could not be confirmed from the sending
side.

Two further items complete the terminal record. First, verification: the
deterministic checks of 19 September passed 11 of 11 with zero failures,
covering exact divisions, receipt presence, sealed-artifact hash
equality and absence probes for the lost execution sources. Second, the
judgment layer: a model-based triage harness was used only for
classification and value ordering, never for arithmetic; it became
unreliable during a service degradation on 19 September, so the per-lane
blocker column in the terminal ledger rests on document evidence rather
than on model judgment.

\section{Small-scale reruns: detail and per-lane
caveats}\label{small-scale-reruns-detail-and-per-lane-caveats}

Each lane's items, raw outputs and grader logs are kept so that every
figure in Tables 8.A and 8.B can be recomputed.

For E12 the 151 rubric items come from only six generated applications,
so they are not independent. The cluster-level test is an exact
sign-flip test over the six per-app pass-rate differences (−0.042,
+0.030, 0.000, −0.160, 0.000, −0.042), and it gives p = 0.25. The E12
grader is an LLM judge that is the same \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B}
base model as the base arm, so self-preference could favour the base
arm. Two judge versions disagree widely (0.536 against 0.808 on the
Tinker base run; \texttt{E12/result.json}). The E12 rates and difference
are therefore judge-dependent, and no independent judge was run. For E4,
all six tasks score 0 in both arms except \texttt{btb-19b3361c}; a
Wilson interval is not valid for a mean of fractional {[}0, 1{]}
rewards, and a bootstrap over one nonzero difference in six is
degenerate. For E13, in 22 of the 26 episodes the progression is
identical in the two arms. No lane-level difference is interpreted as
evidence that the adapter improves or degrades performance unless its
test excludes zero, and none is extrapolated to the full suite.

The principal caveat for each lane, as recorded in its result file, is
listed below; the full list is in each lane's \texttt{result.json}.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{E1.} New base-model arm; not comparable to or poolable with
  the original 2/731 (Tinker sampler route for the seed809 adapter
  purged, weights preserved on the Hugging Face branch; temperature
  0.2).
\item
  \textbf{E2.} Substitute benchmark; never pool with or present as the
  FrontierSWE lane score (original: 1/17 tasks, replay 0.8628; the
  seed809 adapter's Tinker sampler route has since been purged, its
  weights are preserved on the Hugging Face branch).
\item
  \textbf{E3.} New base-model arm; never pool with or present as the
  original SDAB/seed809 lane score.
\item
  \textbf{E4.} Small n=6 of 100; one nonzero task, so no interval is
  reported (the result file's Wilson95 on summed fractional reward is
  not valid for a {[}0,1{]} mean).
\item
  \textbf{E5.} n=10, not the requested 20: the E4+E5 6M Tinker-token cap
  was exhausted (E4 1.56M, abandoned APEX attempt 2.33M, tau2 2.05M).
\item
  \textbf{E6.} Tasks drawn by random.Random(20260926).sample from all
  128 sorted miniwob env ids; episode seeds 0-2.
\item
  \textbf{E7.} New base-model arm; never pool with or present as the
  original lane score.
\item
  \textbf{E8.} New base-model arm; not comparable to or poolable with
  the seed809-adapter 1967/1967 run (22.88\%), whose Tinker sampler
  route is purged though its weights are preserved.
\item
  \textbf{E9.} Competition pool: 6 low-split competitions with small
  non-image-heavy data (detecting-insults, jigsaw-toxic,
  leaf-classification, nomad2018, random-acts-of-pizza, spooky); 5 drawn
  by random.Random(20260926).sample. n=5 so the CI is very wide.
\item
  \textbf{E10.} New base-model arm on AgentHarm itself; the
  seed809-adapter lane figure (AgentDojo benign utility 88/97 = 90.72\%;
  Tinker sampler route purged, weights preserved) is a different
  benchmark and not comparable.
\item
  \textbf{E11.} New base-model arm; not comparable to the retained
  adapter receipt 129/312 = 41.35\% (that run used thinking-default
  template, temperature 0.2, all 312).
\item
  \textbf{E12.} Public tasks, not held-out. Label mandatory. New
  base-model arm; never pool with the original lane.
\item
  \textbf{E13.} New base-model arm; not comparable to or pooled with the
  original seed809-LoRA E13 plan (21-episode BabyAI admission set).
\item
  \textbf{E14.} New base-model arm; the prior 2271/4428 = 51.29\% figure
  (51.31\% on the official scorer's 4426 judged rows) is not comparable
  (different actor, full split).
\end{itemize}



\chapter{Corrections to Internal Source
Documents}\label{corrections-to-internal-source-documents}

The numbers in this thesis are drawn from the project's internal source
documents: the unpublished P1--P8 reports and their section files, audit
summaries and result files. Some of those documents contain errors --- a
wrong sign, a test applied to the wrong unit of analysis, an interval
that does not describe its own estimate, or a label that does not match
the rows it covers. The chapters report only the corrected value and
mark it ``(corrected; see Table E.1)''. This appendix records, for each
correction, what the source states, what the thesis uses instead, and
the basis for the change, so that a reader who opens the source can
reconcile it with the thesis. Caveats about the evidence itself, as
opposed to errors in a source document, stay in the chapters. Paths are
relative to the repository root; ``sections/'' is
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/paper/sections/} and ``results/'' is
\longtok{platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/}.

\chapter{Applied Side Study: Credit-Card Fraud Detection
(P8)}\label{applied-side-study-credit-card-fraud-detection-p8}

\textbf{Scope statement.} This appendix reports a side study. It uses
synthetic data and sits outside the RL contribution of this thesis. The
dataset is generated with scikit-learn's \texttt{make\_classification};
the language-model arm is trained by supervised fine-tuning, not by
reinforcement learning; and no result in this appendix is used to
support any GRPO, PPO, ZVF, group-size or campaign claim elsewhere in
the report. The study is retained, in condensed form, as the P8 slot of
the internal report series and as one worked example of measuring a
claimed capability rather than accepting its label.

The study compares a gradient-boosted tree with a fine-tuned language
model on credit-card fraud detection. Its conclusion is that the tree
keeps the real-time scoring seat, and that the language model's
plausible roles lie in tasks the tree cannot perform at all.

\section{Dataset, splits, and the two
arms}\label{dataset-splits-and-the-two-arms}

The dataset is synthetic: \texttt{make\_classification} (seed 42),
50,000 transactions, 20 anonymised numeric features V1--V20 (10
informative, 2 redundant), two clusters per class, class separation 0.8
and \texttt{flip\_y=0.01}, with a realised fraud rate of 1.44\%. Four
per-row aggregates (mean, standard deviation, maximum, minimum) are
appended, giving 24 features. The data reproduce class imbalance and
anonymised feature shape, and none of the temporal drift, verification
latency or adversarial adaptation of real card-fraud data.

\toprule\noalign{}
Arm & Eval split & \emph{n} & Accuracy & ROC-AUC \\
\midrule\noalign{}

XGBoost (scorer) & full test & 10,000 & n/a & 0.7955 \\
Qwen3.5-4B SFT & positive-enriched test & 500 & 0.7920 & 0.4827 \\
\end{longtable}

\emph{Source:
platform\_hybrid/experiments/results/quick\_20260704/qp8\_fraud.tsv.}

The two AUCs sit on different held-out splits, so this is not a
like-for-like ranking. The language model's AUC of 0.4827 is
chance-level ranking: its Hanley--McNeil 95\% CI, with 100 positives and
400 negatives, is {[}0.42, 0.55{]} and includes 0.5. Its accuracy of
0.792 is slightly \emph{below} the 0.800 that a constant ``legitimate''
answer would score at 20\% prevalence, so it is not evidence of
discrimination. The tree's AUC interval is {[}0.75, 0.84{]}, with 144
positives among 10,000 test rows. The tree's precision 0.723 (34/47,
Wilson 95\% CI {[}0.58, 0.83{]}), recall 0.236 (34/144, {[}0.17,
0.31{]}) and F1 0.356 at the default threshold come from the released
script's own run (AUC 0.7942, 0.49 s training, about 6 ms inference per
10,000 rows), which uses a slightly different configuration from the
0.7955 artefact. An older record of 21 June 2026 (XGBoost AUC 0.975,
language model 0.948) could not be reconstructed and is not used.

\section{Why the scoring seat stays with the
tree}\label{why-the-scoring-seat-stays-with-the-tree}

Four operational arguments hold even at AUC parity:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Latency.} Card authorisation needs a score within tens of
  milliseconds; the tree takes about 6 ms per 10,000 rows, against
  roughly 300 ms for a language-model pass.
\item
  \textbf{Cost.} The released cost table puts the synchronous
  language-model path at about 35 times the per-transaction cost of the
  tree path.
\item
  \textbf{Calibration.} Downstream threshold and expected-loss rules
  consume the score as a probability; verbalised language-model
  confidences are poorly calibrated.
\item
  \textbf{Prompt injection.} A model that reads attacker-controlled text
  fields in a synchronous decision loop opens an injection channel that
  a tree scoring numbers does not have.
\end{itemize}

24-feature tree & Worse on every axis: AUC −0.0245 {[}0.0174, 0.0320{]},
accuracy −0.0135 {[}0.0109, 0.0160{]}, Brier +0.0109 {[}0.0099,
0.0120{]} & p5p8/p8\_\allowbreak{}headline\_\allowbreak{}cis.tsv \\
Adding the four aggregates to the 20 raw features & AUC +0.0002
{[}−0.0002, 0.0007{]}, within noise & p5p8/p8\_\allowbreak{}headline\_\allowbreak{}cis.tsv \\
Leakage caveat & Tables reporting AUC 0.998--0.999 fit on
\longtok{fraud\_data.csv} and score a subset of it; they are
within-protocol relative comparisons only. The leakage-free split gives
0.7955 & p8\_evidence.tex \\
Cost per fraud caught (\$0.50/alert, \$100 missed-fraud loss) & Raw
features \$2.68; sensor-augmented \$3.28; sensor-only \$14.92; the
sensor never pays for itself in the 25-cell (σ, L) grid &
p8\_evidence.tex \\
Sensor-noise break-even & \$5.29 (oracle), \$26.44 (σ ≤ 0.02), \$36.40
(σ = 0.05) & p8\_\allowbreak{}noisy\_\allowbreak{}sensor.tex \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Proposed roles for the language
model}\label{proposed-roles-for-the-language-model}

The study argues, without measuring, that the language model's useful
roles are tasks the tree cannot enter: document and image fraud through
a vision-language model, drafting compliance narratives (Suspicious
Activity Reports under 31 C.F.R. § 1020.320; adverse-action reasons
under 12 C.F.R. § 1002.9), cold-start triage of new fraud typologies
before labels exist, and post-score investigation of the alert queue.
The proposed architecture keeps XGBoost as the scorer and uses the
language model as a sensor (typed features, no authority) and a scribe
(narratives conditioned on the tree's own attributions, with a human
signature). The ``85 times cheaper than an analyst'' triage figure is an
internal estimate from June-2026 token prices and assumed investigation
times, and has not been validated.

\section{Limitations}\label{limitations}

The dataset is synthetic and single-generator, so no number here is a
production expectation, and there is no cross-institution or
public-benchmark replication. The challenger is one model family, one
serialisation and one fine-tuning budget. Of the proposed roles, only
the scorer comparison carries a measurement of the study's own; the
others rest on external literature and regulatory text, and nothing in
the compliance discussion is legal advice.
